in writing. the priorities set by the french presidency of the council of the european union can be considered rather ambitious. i particularly welcome the focus of this presidency on a more sovereign europe that better protects its external borders and interests, as well as the effort presented to strengthen the eu's common defence. we are in difficult geopolitical times and i believe it is important that the european union has sufficient capacity to defend its interests. the programme, unsurprisingly, features the renewal of europe and reindustrialisation to support the ecological and digital transformation. in my view, the social dimension should not be forgotten here either, and i welcome president macron's statement that the french presidency will focus on proposals aimed at improving the quality of jobs, introducing a european minimum wage and strengthening the rights of platform workers. the conference on the future of europe will also be a key topic for the fr pres, not only because it was president macron who proposed its creation, but also because it is during the fr pres that the conference will adopt its conclusions and recommendations. in this context, it cannot be ignored that in april, france will hold presidential elections, which will likely also have an impact on their presidency.
in writing. france has taken over the rotating eu presidency at a turbulent time not only for the union as a whole, which continues to struggle with the coronavirus pandemic, with high energy prices, but also for president emmanuel macron himself, who will probably have to deal more with the election campaign than the european agenda. paris aims to bring to a successful conclusion the contradictory legislation on digital services, as well as the introduction of a so-called carbon tax, which should help member states in the necessary transition to a greener economy. i am rather looking forward to the french presidency's determination to make a breakthrough in the negotiations on the establishment of a so-called european wage, which could help the most socially vulnerable workers on the lowest incomes. france and slovakia also share a common interest in preserving nuclear energy as a sustainable, greener energy source for as long as possible, even through the so-called taxonomy, and paris will certainly try to make this happen despite the opposition of countries such as germany, austria and luxembourg. in the eu, we have to take into account the different conditions in each country, while slovakia needs to invest not only in renewable sources but also in gas and, for a long time, in nuclear energy to decarbonise successfully.
mr president, i agree that we should promote the principle that what is not prohibited offline should not be prohibited online. the commission has already taken a number of initiatives to improve the quality of the services provided by the public authorities, but i think we are in danger of creating a lot of new rules which will have their costs and will not bring about adequate improvement. and the reason we're at risk is because we don't do a good cost-benefit analysis in advance, we don't rely on expert sources. despite numerous objections from the european court of auditors and commitments in its own resolutions, the european parliament often fails to assess the impact of its own substantial amendments. this is also the case for the intended restriction of targeted advertising. the complainants in the european parliament have not, in my view, put forward convincing studies to confirm that targeted advertising is harmful and that restricting it will be effective for the benefit of the consumer. we should only introduce regulation where the benefits outweigh the costs and follow the practices agreed in the better regulation initiative.

mr president, i would like to thank you for your time. tomorrow we're voting together on the digital services act. if i'm to judge what this vote means, i'd say it's a successful first step. i'm fully aware of the enormous possibilities that online platforms have brought to our lives. they enrich and simplify life. yes, we now routinely communicate, shop, make decisions, entertain, and live culturally online. but ladies and gentlemen, online platforms serve us, but they must not grow over our heads and become our masters. as a citizen of a smaller member state, i am particularly sensitive to these issues. small states are themselves less attractive markets for large platforms. but this note is primarily for you, esteemed platforms. this is the european space. the same rules must apply to everyone here. and every user, regardless of member state, must have the same conditions. so i appreciate the legislation that focuses on those we care about the most. the users, the citizens, the common people. they deserve to have an online space that's similar to an offline space. a safe place with clear rules and responsibility. our citizens are giving away so much information about themselves to the big internet platforms that we are asking for much more transparency from the platforms about the content they're sharing. online advertising must be unambiguous, clearly identified, including who is funding it. ladies and gentlemen, i would like to see a lot more commitment in this legislation. because some online platforms have elevated their profits over the values that make our society work. extreme views often dominate the discussion, with emotions taking precedence over the safety of users. i'm not interested in platforms providing the same services to non-english users. this week, however, ladies and gentlemen, i believe we're going to take the first successful step towards making our online space a little bit better.

member of the commission. – mr president, we have all been shocked by the unprecedented violence that erupted in kazakhstan earlier this month, in particular, in almaty. it is important that a clear distinction is made between the early peaceful protests, which began on 2 january and the ensuing violence that erupted nationwide, resulting in the president imposing a state of emergency and calling for the assistance of the collective security treaty organization (csto) in countering what he referred to as terrorist attacks. the picture emerging is that well—armed and trained violent groups hijacked what began as peaceful protests. however, their exact identity and motivations remain unclear. it is possible that those groups were associated with some security paramilitary forces, which would indicate that their formation and existence was covered up by some security structures. there could also be a link to a past struggle related to the succession and access to resources between the circles related to the former president and president tokayev. at this stage, we cannot rule out that external forces have also been involved in the events. according to official sources, initially, almost 10 000 people were detained, but many have been freed in the meantime. two hundred and twenty-five people have been killed in the events, including 19 security officers, and almost 5 000 people have been injured. approximately 2 030 troops were deployed for the first time in the csto’s history, with russia sending by far the largest number. the withdrawal of the csto troops, which began on 13 january, has been completed today, according to the latest news. an official investigation of the events has begun. at this point, the complexity of the crisis merits a cautious interpretation and reaction. it is probably too early to draw conclusions about the eu’s future policy towards the country. there are still many open questions, including about the origin, identity and motivation of the attackers. in our exchanges with the authorities, we called for a full, transparent and independent investigation into the events, including on human rights violations. peaceful protesters should not be treated like criminals. in this regard, we note the statement by president tokayev that participants in peaceful demonstrations should not be prosecuted. we expect the results of the investigation into these dramatic events to be shared with us in a transparent manner. president tokayev has committed to important social economic reforms. however, we call on kazakhstan to continue with political reforms and democratisation and we continue to support these reforms as an integral part of our enhanced partnership and cooperation agreement.

(the opening of the oral question without a microphone) ... the member states must take full account of the welfare of animals as sentient beings when formulating union policy. i've been following the work and working on animal transport for the past year. i must say that it has been a truly terrible experience to see how animals today suffer on our roads and also at sea, how they experience everything from heatstroke to standing in their own feces or having broken bones, and how young animals do not have the opportunity to swim. that is why, in my work in the parliament's committee of inquiry into the protection of animals during transport, i have worked on a number of basic principles for better animal welfare. firstly, i have proposed with my colleagues a maximum time limit of eight hours for the transport of domestic animals. for the transport of animals by sea, we propose a time limit of 24 hours. i think most citizens think that it is already this way. that's really the minimum. secondly, we should introduce stricter rules for the transport of pregnant animals. these animals have special needs and are particularly vulnerable. transport of pregnant animals may cause miscarriage or premature birth. thirdly, the transport of dead animals less than 35 days old should be prohibited. experts have made it clear that it is not appropriate to transport sleeping animals, as this naturally causes stress in the animals, but also because they are too young to be able to assimilate the food in the trucks. a maximum time limit of four hours should be introduced for the transport of animals at the end of their lives. fourthly, only animals should be allowed to be transported to third countries which are included in a green list of countries implementing eu animal welfare legislation. i would urge the commission to examine carefully the recommendations of the european parliament in its forthcoming revision of the regulation on the transport of animals. the situation on the roads and at sea in the union is unworthy and nothing less than a scandal. i would therefore urge the commission to propose urgently to tighten up the regulatory framework, but also to introduce tools to improve the monitoring and implementation of eu rules in the member states.

serious. the anit committee has indeed heard the experts very carefully and they have been quite clear that it is impossible to ensure good conditions for the animals in the selected modes of transport. i'm talking about transporting babies at an early age, when they're reliant on drinking milk, when we're transporting them as early as two weeks after birth, and we're not providing them mr. chairman, ladies and gentlemen, what is more important in life than human life? the lives of our children. and that's why this directive, which was created in 2009, when i was still working as an assistant to mep suzanne roith, who was calling for certain standards and quality of toys, was so important. she was a game changer. she set the standards that we wanted the chinese to follow, and for the sellers to quickly take down the low-quality toys. and i think it's time or need to be revised. because if i said at the beginning that the most important thing is the health of our children, then we really can't allow anyone to sell low-quality junk here and profit from parents who don't have enough financial resources to buy quality gifts for their children. i am therefore pleased that this review has taken place and will be implemented as soon as possible. thank you, sir.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i have listened very carefully to the whole debate and i am very pleased that in a matter of such a sensitive nature, which is of a strong ethical nature, we are all in principle in agreement that such regulation, such legislation, is extremely important. it is the health of our children that is at stake, and at a time when this directive has been in force for almost ten years, it is logical that it should be revised, that certain institutions should be tightened up, that certain rules should be better regulated. this is particularly the case for the trade in children's goods and toys on online platforms, as well as for chemicals that may be contained in toys. i'm very pleased. i like the idea that has been expressed here and i would personally support the idea of transposing these rules from the directive itself into a regulation if necessary, because it is much more effective in terms of regulation, enforcement and efficiency than the directive itself, which is then transposed by the individual member states into their own legal systems. i agree with what has been said here. it is very important to address the issue of online platforms, the sale of goods from third countries, where various low-quality toys are coming to us. the question of market surveillance authorities needs to be addressed, the questions relating to the descriptions on individual toys need to be addressed in such a way that parents are clearly informed about what the product contains, whether or not it can be harmful to children for certain applications. so it is certainly a good job and i would like the commission to translate these ideas into a new form of directive or regulation as soon as possible. thank you, sir.
vice-president of the commission. – mr president, thank you for this debate. toys must be safe for children to play with and ensuring precisely that toys in the eu do not put our children at risk is a major priority for the commission. our legislation aims to ensure that toys meet safety requirements that are amongst the strictest in the world and remain so, and we want to make sure that this will happen through the community method by the revision of this important emblematic directive. you were right, many of you, in insisting on the need to take account of new consumption patterns like the development of online sales, but also the corresponding risks on compliance, as well as new tendencies, new developments like internet-connected toys. and i want to assure all of you that we shall grasp all these new issues in the revision. we will continue to work to present this revised proposal by the end of this year, and your report comes at a very crucial, important time in this process. we are looking forward to keep discussing our forthcoming proposals with you to improve safety in europe and beyond.
the rapporteur. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i thank the shadow rapporteurs for their constructive cooperation, the commission for its leadership and all the stakeholders who have contributed to the debate on this important issue for the future of our children. i am confident that the report will have broad support, given the unanimous vote in the committee on the internal market and given the speeches by my colleagues, from which i draw a clear sense of agreement with the work done, compared to these months of negotiating together. we are therefore awaiting the proposal to revise the directive. we are ready to cooperate fully with the commission in an ambitious and forward-looking text which provides clear, clear and strong protection for children, legal certainty and also an opportunity for development for the many small and mediumsized enterprises in the sector. thank you all again.

for the greens/efa group mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the internal market is an instrument which must enable us to achieve our public policy objectives. to achieve the objectives of the green deal or the digital transition, we need a functioning single market with rules that serve these objectives. if non-tariff barriers are an important issue, beware: economic freedoms in no way take precedence over fundamental rights, social rights and labour rights. the establishment of common rules must not therefore be at the expense of consumer and worker protection, or the provision of quality goods and services for our citizens. the rules must also be consistent with our other objectives, which are to combat climate change and conserve our resources. i would like to hear the comments on overregulation in this connection. i do not think that responsibility for all the adoption of national technical rules, which, let us remember, are subject to a procedure supervised at european level, can be shifted to the states and at the same time deregulation can be requested to avoid additional burdens on companies. you have to be consistent. a single market based on harmonisation from above is good, but managed in a fair, responsible and ecological way.
on behalf of the ecr group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your kind words. the european union's action to deepen the fundamental freedoms of the single market, which will increase europe's sovereignty and competitiveness, especially now after the experience of the covid-19 pandemic, should be a priority for us. i am therefore all the more pleased that the report we are discussing, which was initiated by my group, the european conservatives and reformists, has been produced. the report addresses a number of very important issues that we have to face in the european market. barriers, particularly in the services sector, are holding back the potential of european companies, in particular small and medium-sized enterprises operating in a cross-border environment. the report focuses on practical solutions, calls for greater digitisation and streamlining of procedures. the key is to pursue greater harmonisation without increasing the regulatory burden on businesses. i would like to congratulate the author of this report, mr kosma zlotowski. i hope that his report will give the european commission a direction on how to make better use of the freedoms of the single market and how to implement pragmatic solutions to the problems raised by businesses.
the single market, mr president, is and will remain the crown jewel of european integration. we should be proud of it. in the meantime, attention is shifting to the digital single market, while the single market for goods and services is far from complete. as a member of the committee on agriculture and rural development and the committee on the internal market and consumer protection, i have contact with many contacts in the agri-food sector and, when visiting farms, i am constantly asked about the protectionist measures of neighbouring countries, whether through legislation or workbooks or in everyday life. thanks to the european green deal and because of the coronavirus crisis, there is more emphasis than ever on sustainability and local production. but we must not allow ourselves to slip into a situation where each member state, under the guise of local production, is shutting down its own market and only accepting products from its own country. this could have far-reaching consequences, especially for smaller member states such as mine. i would therefore ask the european commission to take on a greater role as guardian of the single market so that the single market can mean even more for all businesses in the union. i am counting on the commission and the task force on the maintenance of the single market to strengthen and make the single market more effective, and at the same time to complete the single market for goods and services. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's trade policy is not distorted by the introduction of new barriers to trade. only in this way can we further deepen the single market and maintain the confidence of businesses in the market.
mr. chairman, please. the eu's single market creates jobs and growth. it also ensures good consumer protection, and it ensures a green transition, and then it ensures fair and social progress. i believe that this is one of the greatest achievements of the european union in the single market. it's the engine of our collaboration. the single market is simply in many ways absolutely crucial for us. we also need the internal market to continue to develop. we have to protect it and make it better. that is why we must not accept protectionism or unjustified obstacles to the completion of the internal market, and that is why this report is good to have. we'll see what works and what doesn't. in my view, we need to look at whether there are any obstacles that we need to remove. for example, if there are unreasonable language barriers in connection with the tender or if there is otherwise insufficient enforcement of our common rules. and if there are unfounded rules and obstacles that are problematic, then of course they have to go. because we need a well-functioning single market. but we also have to recognize that just because it's a rule, it doesn't have to be a barrier. we have many rules which are also important, which serve to protect workers, the environment, etc. so while we are addressing the barriers, we must also respect the right of member states to ensure proper environmental protection, consumer protection or workers' rights. but in many ways i think this report strikes the right balance. our good internal market must be made even better.
mr president, the single market will never be completed until all four freedoms are fully available to the citizens. this means goods, capital, persons and – a missing piece – services. here in strasbourg, we are on the border between two big countries. if i need a plumber, an accountant or another service provider, it should not matter if they are registered in france or in germany, as long as the work gets done. i, as a consumer, should have a choice and they, as a business, must equally do so. this should be true across the european market, across any border in europe – from the czech republic to germany, spain to portugal. yet today, every member state continues to put up administrative and other non-tariff barriers. we are one european market and there should be no internal borders to our businesses. this house adopted an interesting study last term on the cost of non—europe. it showed that by not completing the single market for services, we are leaving eur 297 billion on the table – 297 billion! if we do this study again, i am sure it will be even more today. commissioner, you must have the courage to stand up to those member states blocking progress. we call for you to act.
mr president, i have long maintained that if we are to succeed as a country and as a union, we must open ourselves to competition, not protect ourselves from it. the free movement of goods works well in the union, but if i wanted my house to be designed by an estonian architect, for example, that is no longer without obstacles. the free market in services is still subject to many restrictions in the union, in particular due to various protectionist measures that favour domestic providers under the pretext of maintaining the quality of services or the public interest. if restrictions on services were reduced to the level of the three least regulated states of the union, the productivity of firms across the union could increase by around three per cent. i therefore agree with the call for member states to extend mutual recognition of diplomas and qualifications as soon as possible.
mr president, our common european market is the best proof of our european integration and it is the pandemic that has shown how important it is, but also how fragile it is. the restrictive measures imposed by many countries, whether out of fear of the spread of the virus or to protect their interests, have disrupted trade and supply relations and caused huge economic damage. today too, we are witnessing a number of obstacles in the single market, especially for smes. when implementing european rules, member states often use the so-called 'gold plating' to mean additional redundant administration. in the end, such a procedure is manifested in burdensome and costly red tape, in the misapplication of european legislation, in disproportionate sanctions and in unjustified measures by national control authorities, often without adequate redress. simplifying legislation and reducing the administrative burden is therefore the obvious solution. this is the best way of helping those who create the most jobs, namely small and medium-sized enterprises. today, when the digital market is developing most, it is important to identify all the obstacles to the development of e-commerce and to remove them effectively. let us therefore not only remember the need to complete the common market, but also to remove the unnecessary obstacles which ultimately cost us all too much.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, any non-tariff barriers affect and strike the smallest, in fact micro, small and mediumsized enterprises. any non-tariff barrier in any member state affects the entire internal market. that is why, of course, i agree, commissioner, that we do have a new reindustrialisation strategy, we have a new sme strategy, but it is essential that we identify those barriers which do not actually bring money into the budget, either to the eu budget or to the national budgets, because they indirectly undermine the activity of enterprises. and yes, i'm glad you identified 24 restrictions in 13 sectors. there are still sectors where you can identify concrete constraints, because that's the way we have to go. it's good that we have a task force, smet, but i think they have to not only identify, but also come up with solutions the moment they identify something in the market. and finally, there are a few things that i really care about. i'm from the private sector. for example, recognition of qualifications is an impediment to labour mobility. cross-border procurement for smes is a problem and we have to recognise it. i hope that through this amendment and through this proposal of ours we will be able to have a better single market.
mr president, commissioner, the single market is the greatest european achievement, and it opens up a huge number of possibilities. this is a considerable asset, which we must now fully exploit and put to the service of our new priorities: strengthening our strategic autonomy and propelling europe to the status of a model in the ecological and digital transition. yet we are trampling. the obstacles have been there for years, and the fact is that this weakening of the market is hurting us, we are losing time, we are losing money. we have seen this especially during the covid-19 crisis. we also know the reasons: poor application of european legislation, too much bureaucracy, insufficient access to information and too much protectionism on the part of too many member states. these barriers deprive consumers of a better choice and limit the potential of our businesses, especially smes. it's urgent, we have to get out of this deadlock. it is high time to achieve a fully effective single market, adapted to the new challenges.
the single market is the best thing the european union has produced. it has brought prosperity and the free movement of goods, services and capital. however, almost 30 years after the single market was established, it is clear that member states still adopt protectionist measures and thereby impede the functioning of the single market. in addition, 70% of smes make no or insufficient use of the single market. this is explained as follows: when smes, often lacking legal assistance, become embroiled in a dispute with local authorities over the cross-border interpretation of european legislation, they often decide to withdraw and simply return to their home member state. in the light of this, it would be appropriate to provide legal certainty to such smes at the outset of disputes, for example by introducing a temporary provision on legal clarity for smes at the outset of disputes. the commission has also taken note of the fact that the court of justice of the european union has not yet adopted a decision on the compatibility of the aid with the internal market. this leads to many smes withdrawing and not taking advantage of the opportunities offered by the single market. we must therefore consider whether a temporary provision can be introduced on legal clarity for smes when initiating disputes.
vice-president of the commission. – mr president, honourable members, thank you for this interesting debate. if i were to simplify this formidable adventure of european integration into three simple notions, into three success stories, i would say: single market, schengen and the euro. for the single market more than ever, this is the most powerful asset at our disposal to support economic recovery, growth and resilience across our society and the economy. it is also essential to accelerate our future growth model, which will be firmly based on the green and digital transition. we will continue working on all these fronts to find effective solutions to ensure that the single market continues to deliver the best possible conditions for businesses and consumers alike, and many of the very useful ideas and suggestions that you have forwarded tonight – rest assured – will be duly reflected in our future work. so, on behalf of the commission, the only thing that is left to me is once again to thank you for this report and to look forward to continuing our cooperation in tackling barriers to ensure a well-functioning, cohesive single market that works for all.

member of the commission. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would first like to thank you for this in-depth debate and to tell you again that the commission, like your parliament, is committed to the complete abolition of citizenship by investment programmes in all member states. we no longer want golden passports and we have not waited for russian aggression in ukraine to act. i would remind you that it was in october 2020 that we launched infringement procedures and we have already obtained the termination of the various programmes in cyprus, but also the abolition, on which the bulgarian parliament is currently deciding, of this type of system in bulgaria. we will continue to call for this to be completely abolished in malta too, even though there has already been an initial announcement concerning golden passports for russian or belarusian citizens. i believe that we should not want to legitimize this practice of golden passports. this is, as i said earlier, the reason why we are looking at your legislative proposals. the aim is to abolish citizenship by investment programmes. in addition, we asked cyprus to check the passports already issued, which is in progress, and i hope we can continue to work on examining what has been done in recent years in the member states that have granted citizenship by investment. it is not normal, it is not possible to sell this common good of european citizenship, this common good of the twenty-seven, just to feed a budget in one of the member states. the aim is therefore, i repeat, the complete abolition of the death penalty. it is not a question of setting rules to allow it, but simply of moving towards its complete abolition. as regards the investment residence schemes - the golden visas - my colleague eva johnson has already said all that we have done in the commission, either with regard to the member states or with regard to third countries, and we will continue. i can tell you that together we will look very carefully at all the concerns - some of which i mentioned earlier - raised in your report, including, by the way, possible legislative initiatives in this area. in any case, you can be sure that there is no question of allowing the values of the european union to be sold in any way, whether through access to citizenship or through other types of mechanisms, such as the right of residence granted by member states or by third countries. so i really must insist: the crisis we are experiencing today, the russian aggression in ukraine, is probably causing a greater awareness, i hope, in some member states; however, since 2020, measures have been taken to end the sale of citizenship altogether. this is the case in cyprus, i am glad that bulgaria is also passing legislation and we continue to ask that malta does the same, and not just for russian and belarusian citizens. of course, we will not hesitate to take further action if this abolition does not take place very quickly.

mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your work. we are discussing today a question for an oral reply from parliament to the commission, which asks, among other things, what measures could be taken to control the political pressure in this difficult, crisis-ridden political situation with the exploding energy prices. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. i want to concentrate on one: the important projects of common european interest, for which we always use the british abbreviation the english abbreviation ipcei. in the communication presented today by the commission, for which i am grateful, the commission commits itself to reviewing these ipceis for their fairness under competition law by the end of the first half of 2022. it's a little late for that, commissioner. we have just launched a whole bunch of ipceis from germany to boost the hydrogen economy in my country. i know of companies that are faced with investment decisions and are waiting for the release of this money and these projects. so if we want the investments -- the ones that are taking us out of fossil fuels, because they can substitute -- then we have to go faster.
mr president, commissioner, in slovakia we want solar panels on the roof, not russian tanks in the yard. so it's time to do something i've been calling for for years. start the energy revolution in full swing. we need to promote energy efficiency, because the cheapest gas is the one we don't use. remove the obstacles to green energy development by citizens and businesses alike in a way that is environmentally sensitive. solar, wind, geothermal, biogas, or hydro are cheaper than fossil gas. we should have done this years ago. not waiting for the climate crisis to get worse. until putin attacks the ukraine, and actually us. now we have to add. we must run, but not in a way that leaves the most vulnerable and the poorest behind. that's where the climate check helps, which puts the emissions fees on people, not polluters. putin won't cut off our green energy or raise our price on the stock market. it's ours, it's the energy of freedom.
mr president, today, we have to talk about both the energy prices hike and energy security. first, ets emissions allowances. it appears that while allowances prices have fallen by a third since the start of the war in ukraine, the gas price is rising extremely high. i think that we have seen genuine market manipulation and speculative trading in recent months. investors have taken profits from growing prices of the allowances and moved their money elsewhere, driving the price down. it’s important for the future that the european commission prevent such practices from happening. current developments in ukraine show that europe must reduce its dependence on russian gas as soon as possible. however, we should not forget about our own energy security. the eu currently has about 27% of gas in storage for the rest of the winter, which should be enough. but the big question is how to ensure that the storage utilities are full for next winter, by the end of november at the latest. several analyses showed that without russian gas, europe will be short of at least 30 billion cubic meters of gas. so how to replace it? i know that there is the option of lng. there are lng terminals on the coast of northern baltic sea. but how do we import it, how do we take it to central europe? i think this is a big question and we have to think about it. we have to invest in the gas infrastructure. i don’t think now but the other option is nuclear. i know that we are not friends of nuclear, but we will need it for some time.

you this morning. the european semester remains particularly relevant to ensure effective coordination of economic policies. clearly, we cannot ignore or accurately predict the impact that russia's brutal and unjustified aggression against ukraine is likely to have on the economic outlook. we will do everything we can to limit the impact on our countries, our citizens, our businesses. the council welcomes the economic priorities proposed by the commission in the 2022 annual review of sustainable growth, which aims at a sustainable and equitable recovery. however, in this difficult and uncertain environment, we have economic, social and environmental challenges to address and work to do. the economic impact of the covid-19 pandemic continues to be felt across our economies. the lockdown measures coupled with supply constraints have contributed to inflation rising beyond forecasts, which has a negative impact on the daily lives of our citizens. supply disruptions and other factors aggravating inflation, such as higher energy prices, are also likely to intensify due to the current geopolitical situation. some of the more vulnerable components of our society have been particularly affected by the pandemic. i am thinking of workers in atypical jobs or with fixed-term contracts, low-skilled workers, young people, single parents, disabled people and workers born outside the european union. many member states remain under pressure due to increased strain on health systems, coupled with staff shortages. it is essential to protect our citizens, their health and their jobs. in view of the current level of uncertainty, the council is of the opinion that member states should remain flexible in their fiscal policy response in order to be able to react to the evolution of the current situation. we therefore invite member states to regularly reassess the use, effectiveness and adequacy of support measures and to be prepared to adapt them, if necessary, to changing circumstances while preserving the sustainability of public finances. the council is convinced that the implementation of a coherent set of reforms and public and private investment supporting competitiveness and job creation will help to increase employment, productivity and innovation and support the dual transition. we also welcome the commission's proposal for a joint report on employment in 2022 and the increased focus on the principles of the european pillar of social rights, as well as the integration of the broad objectives and indicators of the social scoreboard in the proposal, in line with the european semester and the porto declaration. as your reports have pointed out, there are persistent and growing inequalities between and within the member states and regions. in fact, they've been exacerbated by the effects of the pandemic. these growing inequalities exacerbate poverty and social exclusion. thus, robust social protection and labour market measures are needed to mitigate the effects. this requires sustained and coordinated efforts. the proper functioning of the single market is by its nature an important source of productivity for the eu economy and remains a priority for the french presidency. the european semester is the main instrument we have to do this. the framework for meeting these challenges and ensuring effective coordination of economic policies remains in place. this is why member states, including national parliaments, should take every opportunity to engage in a transparent dialogue with the commission, at all stages of the european semester and within the framework of the recovery and resilience facility, in particular to promote the implementation of the national recovery and resilience plans and, at the same time, to ensure effective multilateral surveillance. the french presidency intends to ensure a smooth cycle of the european semester and to ensure that all parties concerned make every effort to achieve the common objective of having the country-specific recommendations endorsed by the european council.
executive vice-president of the commission. – madam president, today, our hearts and minds are with ukraine. russia’s barbaric and illegal invasion, its attacks against civilians, continue to take the ultimate toll of innocent people. i express my condolences to the ukrainian people for their losses, and i salute their courage and determination to defend their homeland and our european values. we stand by ukraine, providing all the support we can, and we are putting maximum pressure on russia to stop the aggression. there is no doubt that these dramatic events – including sanctions we are introducing against russia and russia’s counter-sanctions – will weigh on the european economy. we will see overall higher inflation, more pressure on energy and food prices, and market volatility. while uncertainty is very high, our current assessment is that growth will clearly be impacted. but this is a price worth paying for defending freedom and peace, and our economic fundamentals are strong. the eu economy has regained all the ground lost during the covid—19 crisis. unemployment is at a record low. this is not least thanks to the unprecedented policies we have put in place: next generation eu and the recovery and resilience facility (rrf). this very worrying situation once again shows the importance of europe remaining united, strong and resilient, and of sticking to our policies, because they are working. promoting economic and social resilience is vital to ensuring europe’s stability and prosperity. i would like to congratulate the rapporteurs, irene tinagli and helmut geuking. their report demonstrates the broad alignment of views for coordinated policies to support our inclusive green and digital future. the european semester remains our framework for coordinating the eu’s economic and employment policies. we have adapted it to take into account the implementation of the rrf. with its very large investments in energy efficiency, decarbonisation and renewables, as well as in the resilience of vital infrastructure, the rrf is a powerful tool to reduce our energy dependency and build europe’s future stability. the 30% of expenditure committed so far to employment and social goals will help to mitigate the impact of the transition on the most vulnerable, and also strengthen our social resilience. since we are now going through exceptional uncertainty, we will use the european semester to identify new challenges and to follow up on those not addressed under the recovery and resilience plans. on the fiscal side, member states need to continue supporting their economies this year with targeted measures. as you know, the general escape clause remains activated. last week, the european commission provided preliminary fiscal guidance for 2023. our current projections indicate that the general escape clause is due to be disactivated as of next year, but in view of the uncertainty, we will reassess this in the spring on the basis of our spring economic forecast. overall, fiscal support should remain broadly at the same level as this year – this means a broadly neutral fiscal stance – and the rff will continue to provide substantial support. in turn, high-debt member states should start to rebuild buffers in a growth—friendly way. to achieve this, budgetary atunement should be gradual, and all member states should promote and protect public investment in their fiscal plans. this underscores the importance of improving the quality and composition of public finances. honourable members, these are exceptional times. we are living through a period of very high uncertainty. our policies need to remain agile. we need to monitor developments very closely, see how different risks evolve, and adjust our policies as necessary.
rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on the environment, public health and food safety. – madam president, the european semester was born out of the financial crisis of the late 2000s. now we are facing the climate and biodiversity crisis, the – hopefully – post-pandemic recovery, but also the security and energy threats that culminated with the russian invasion of ukraine. it’s time to make it ready for the 2020s. it’s time to recognise that ‘business as usual’ is over. and that also means new ways of monitoring progress in delivery of what we agreed on together. the new deal, including the acceleration of the green energy drive, that we need to do to cut our strategically dangerous dependence on russian fossil fuels, needs to be properly reflected in the semester’s monitoring framework. true sustainability, fitting the eu into planetary limits, needs to rest on three pillars: the economic, the social and the environmental. we need to ensure that we are coherent in all three of them, and that means from an energy perspective that we need to further integrate the eu’s climate, environmental – including biodiversity – and social objectives in a more comprehensive manner. economic indicators are not enough in times of need to reduce our ecological footprint. we need to check properly the alignment of the recovery and resilience facility (rrf) and multiannual financial framework (mff) with the climate and biodiversity spending targets, but also policy objectives. we cannot allow our public money to do significant harm. we cannot afford regression, and we need to include partners, not just us in the parliament, but also civil society, social partners, scientific bodies and other stakeholders. we need a semester that looks at progress towards sustainability as a whole, not just part of it.
ladies and gentlemen, batteries are extremely important for european industrial competitiveness and for the achievement of the green agenda, which is ambitious. as technology advances, the capabilities and performance of batteries increase, which further encourages, of course, their widespread use. the commission also found that the chinese exporting producers had not provided any evidence that the chinese exporting producers had been able to obtain the necessary raw materials from the chinese exporting producers. recognising this importance, the european union has invested eur 127 billion by 2021 and is expected to invest an additional eur 382 billion by 2030. i commend such ambition and find it encouraging. i also welcome the efforts of this report which sets out a common set of rules for sustainability, safety, labelling and information related to the use of batteries. the report also defines the requirements for the collection, treatment and recycling of waste batteries. in this way, we strengthen the functioning of the single market, promote a circular economy and reduce the environmental and social impact throughout the life cycle of batteries. i would like to take this opportunity to highlight a positive example from my home country, the republic of croatia. it's about the success of young entrepreneur and innovator mate rimc whose company rimac automobiles produces high-quality batteries for the world's fastest electric car, the nevera. i also commend the recognition at the european union level that innovators and those already living through the green transition have that financial support. so the future has already happened somewhere. ladies and gentlemen, the future will increasingly be powered by energy stored in batteries, and it is up to us to lay the legislative foundations that will make them environmentally acceptable and, of course, economically viable.
madam president, i would like to thank you. as the eu moves from the coal and steel union to becoming the world's first climate-neutral region, electrification is the big key. we need batteries, lots of them. it is therefore important that the sustainable transition also becomes sustainable. the whole life cycle must be sustainable. our batteries must be produced with full respect for human rights, with sustainable production methods and with as much recycled material as possible. i am therefore pleased with the progressive mandate we are taking today in the battery regulation, with five clear increases in ambition: 1. higher targets for the recycling of portable batteries; 2. all car and industrial batteries will be collected; 3. clear labelling of the carbon footprint of batteries throughout the manufacturing process; 4. more common standards for chargers; 5. manufacturers must ensure that standards of social and environmental sustainability are met. i would like to conclude by thanking my party colleague simona bonafè for her excellent work as rapporteur, and i would like to wish you, simona, the very best of luck in the important negotiations ahead.
madam president, the impact of the war in ukraine is everywhere, even in relation to batteries. the war has already shut down the production of electric cars in a number of member states, including the czech republic, due to the electric parts that go into connecting batteries to the vehicles being blocked. as vehicle manufacturers struggle to find new sources of supplies, we must prevent a similar problem in the future with the batteries themselves. that is why this legislation should help support better production and recycling in europe, so that, in the future, we have a safe supply of raw and recycled materials. at the same time, this legislation must not be used as a tool against a global supply chain. when we are not faced with a crisis, the best source of many batteries and battery materials will continue to be outside the european union. i firmly believe that we must work with other international players, including in the un economic commission of africa (eca) forum, as we implement this legislation towards making these rules universal and not for europe alone. as we move towards a future of electric vehicles, the question of batteries will not be a question of aa or aaa batteries. we need a plan for what to do with these huge car and truck batteries. the car industry must be a partner in this and our laws must be able to be implemented. as we go into trilogues, i ask all sides to make sure this law will work in practice. only if we work with the industry will we get this right.
i have to say, madam president, batteries are an integral part of our daily lives, but batteries are now more than just our everyday household products. the demand for batteries will increase rapidly in the coming years, especially for electric vehicles. it was therefore right and important to have a bill that does not only deal with used batteries and only certain categories of batteries, but looks at the entire cycle of all batteries, from their creation to their final disposal. this is all the more important as batteries are expected to play a crucial role in the mobility revolution. however, batteries require a lot of raw materials. it was therefore only logical to adopt a circular approach. because we're running out of raw materials. their extraction must respect human rights and environmental standards and must not endanger protected areas. but not only did we need to increase recycling rates, but to really recycle all the raw materials, we also need a better collection system for all the batteries and a better design of the batteries. recycling starts at the point of production and must be considered holistically. equally important, the law extended the duty of care of economic operators for human rights and our environment to the entire value chain, that is, to all batteries, all materials, including waste treatment. i am very pleased that we have been able to bring this in. we've also managed to limit the amount of toxic substances in batteries. i would like to take this opportunity to highlight another aspect: the number of battery factories in europe is increasing. these could be built on industrial wastelands, but unfortunately they are increasingly being designed on green areas such as farmland or forests. in addition, they are even built in protected water areas. the example here is tesla in brandenburg, germany. in the fight against climate change, we cannot be blind to the loss of biodiversity and to the need to protect soil and water. to be sustainable, you have to think holistically. this proposal is a first good step in that direction.
madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the ideology of the green deal gives europeans the blind illusion that the ecological transition of our one, tiny europe can save us from the so-called climate crisis, while everything out there around us is leading to serious shortages of strategic raw materials. the commission has not found any evidence that the commission has taken into account the fact that the batteries are produced in the union. the european union now imports 98% of its needs from china, despite various partnerships with other countries, the most recent one being with the agonized ukraine on 13 july. so to have common standards and to think about battery production and disposal in a circular logic is a step forward, but we know that it will not be enough and that we need time. the commission should open its eyes to this reality: the objectives and timetables for achieving climate neutrality are not realistic and to think of achieving them by relying entirely on electricity will not only go against the principle of technological neutrality but will make us even more dependent on third countries, with the blessing of the much-vaunted energy sovereignty. the european union is not only a political force, but also a political force, and it is a political force, and it is a political force, and it is a political force. today's images, today's costs should teach us something, and instead we continue with the same suicidal approach as yesterday. stop before it's too late.
madam president, first of all i would like to congratulate simona bonafè on the work that has been done in this report on batteries. we know that moving away from fossil fuels will inevitably require more batteries, which is why the regulation as proposed today is necessary. indeed, more batteries will mean more recycling and more circular economy. we know that today only 12% of aluminium, 22% of cobalt and 16% of nickel are recycled, and 52% of waste is actually collected. more batteries will also mean more extraction of resources, and this cannot be at the expense of the countries and the people who own them. that is why the inclusion of a duty of care is so important to ensure that the batteries we use do not cause environmental or social damage. but more batteries means more potentially hazardous chemicals that will need to be replaced by more sustainable alternatives, as required by reach legislation at present. only then will the transition be sustainable.
madam president, commissioner, we carry our mobile phones and computers with us everywhere. batteries have become a very common part of our lives, and it's really not just about the little pencil batteries anymore. we love technology. but we only love them when they really work. and i'll tell you, it makes me very angry when i have to buy a new cell phone or a new computer just because i can't change the battery. the crazy thing is, it's often more financially rewarding than replacing the battery itself. that's why we need to change things. and i'm not just talking about batteries in computers or cell phones. it's a question of batteries in cars, batteries in bicycles these days, or in the electrical grid. we don't have endless resources at our disposal. we only have one planet. she has her own abilities. and i think we all realize that by 2022. that is why this legislation is so timely. it must have rules not only for the production, replacement or disposal of batteries but also for their recycling. we need to finally move to a circular economy for batteries as well. it's something we've all been calling for a long time. not only must batteries be fully recyclable, but they must also be made from secondary raw materials. and they must be made without toxic substances. we can't pretend that what's beyond the borders doesn't concern us. if we demand safe and fair working conditions at home, so do the workers who produce batteries for the european market. if we protect nature at home, we can't destroy the environment for our own needs where we can't see it. as europe, we have the opportunity through this legislation to influence the world market, to set standards that are respected not only in our country but around the world. we can make it the gold standard for the world. batteries are going to be very important in the future. but only batteries made and used in a way that is friendly to the planet and to people will help us create a green and just future.
madam president, the war in ukraine confirmed how vital it is for europe to increase its resilience and strategic autonomy in all key sectors in order to increase the competitiveness of our industries and, above all, decrease our dependence on third countries, including russia. in this context, let me mention the european battery alliance established by the vice-president of the european commission, maros šefčovič, is a good example of how to decrease both dependency on foreign countries and emissions at the same time. we are moving towards strategic autonomy in this sector, which is critical for the green and digital transformation. the total investment with the entire battery value chain reached eur 127 billion, which makes europe a global battery hotspot. at this pace, we are set to meet almost 90% of the domestic demand for batteries by 2030, but satisfying a growing demand depends on our ability to face two challenges: critical raw materials and skills. almost one million workers need to be re-skilled in the next three years. securing supplies of critical raw materials such as lithium, cobalt and magnesium is a strategic security question for europe. current regulation has to be used to demand stronger sustainability and performance requirements and clear targets for waste collection and recycling efficiency. thank you also to our rapporteur, ms bonafè. we are going in this direction. we must make europe more attractive for the development of sustainable raw materials projects, while respecting the highest environmental standards and protecting public health.

on behalf of the s&d group. – madam president, madam commissioner, let me thank the rapporteur angelika winzig for the fruitful cooperation in reaching the agreement on this revision, also to other shadow rapporteurs from other political groups. ‘roaming like at home’ became a reality in 2017 and it changed our travelling patterns forever. after more than two years of pandemic, with the war in ukraine, europe is now facing even more serious challenges. as s&d negotiator for roaming, i am glad we managed not only to extend the rules for another 10 years, but also to remove existing barriers and discriminatory practices. we managed to further lower wholesale prices, introduced guarantee of quality of service, improve transparency and secure free access to emergency services. all of this hasn’t been the case until now. europeans benefiting from free movement cannot be punished with lower quality or higher charges, no matter where they come from or how long they stay in another member state. facing the war in ukraine, i am happy that i insisted on inclusion of the free roaming provisions also in the international agreements to be concluded in the future. in this context, i think that the lower charges for mobile-phone use in europe should be very helpful to all the refugees coming from ukraine.

executive vice-president of the commission. – madam president, mr minister, honourable members, thank you for your fruitful exchange and for your comments and remarks. i want to assure you that the commission will continue engaging with the european parliament as this work is going forward. based on your questions and remarks, i understand you share the commission’s concerns that our efforts need to focus on the three main issues: ensuring affordable energy prices for european consumers; security of supply – meaning that we are fully prepared for the next winter season regardless of what happens; and diminishing and eventually completely removing our dependence on russian fossil fuels. these three aims mutually reinforce each other. in versailles, leaders supported the proposals in our communication of 8 march, and asked the commission to propose a repowereu plan to phase out russian fossil fuels by 2027, backed by the necessary national and european resources. this is what we are now working full-speed on, to make it happen. i also would like to thank all the members who today expressed their support for the commission’s work. our plans are very ambitious, as is required by the current situation. but it also means that we need your support to quickly adopt all legislative proposals, starting with the gas—storage proposal adopted this week. we also count on your support on accelerating the work on the december package, which includes important provisions on security of supply. and finally, a crucial part of decreasing our dependency on russian gas is diminishing our consumption of gas altogether. therefore, we hope to also move very fast on all the legislative proposals under the ‘fit for 55’ package and we count on your support. this is part of the solution to make the eu resilient to shocks and less dependent on russian fossil fuels. we need to be united while facing these challenging times and if there is one thing the crisis is showing us it is that the eu is more united than ever in responding to putin and supporting the ukrainian people.
president of the council. mr president, mr vice-president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to add a few comments to what the european commission has just said, which i very much agree with. we said it, as did the european commission, in the introduction to this debate, and this has been widely relayed in the trade: there are indeed two types of crises that are cumulating today and that result in a very significant increase in energy prices. first, a price increase that was already in place and which is mainly the consequence of the covid-19 crisis and the accelerated economic recovery, which was basically good news, but which had resulted in this very significant increase in gas, electricity and fuel prices. in addition, the war we are now experiencing has revealed a structural dependence on russian gas in particular, which in the present context is becoming unbearable and is doubling from one member state to another, with a very high degree of heterogeneity in relation to this dependence. it is in this context that we must act, stronger and faster, with several types of responses, which have been discussed and which i will only briefly summarize to report on the debates taking place in the council, and which will also take place in the european council tonight and tomorrow. first, the imperative need to strengthen our european regulatory and coordination tools. this is one of the debates that will take place in the european council today, on the basis of the recent proposals of the european commission, with the question of common storage to deal with supply risks - i believe this is probably the most consensual point today between the member states - the question of possible joint purchases - which i believe still needs to be explored and is a good track, in our opinion - and the question, debated between member states, of tools to cap final prices to consumers in difficult crisis periods - i believe we should not exclude this either, even if, as i have said in a transparent way, this subject is still the subject of complex discussions between member states. in any case, in this debate we must not only uphold the acquis of the internal market, and certainly strengthen our energy interconnections - which is imperative in the light of the tensions today - but also better protect our consumers, with regulatory tools that must be reconstructed or devised. the second type of response, which is intended as a reaction to the structural problem of dependence i mentioned, is, in essence, that of energy sovereignty. we see the need for this even more clearly - and i repeat, this was also mentioned in the debate - since the european project has been based precisely on energy sovereignty from the outset. today we need to set common climate targets at european level - this has been a given for just over two years now - for 2030 and 2050, and together we need to accelerate our transition. this is the subject of the legislative package on adjustment to objective 55 which we are seeking to push forward in the council under the french presidency, with the aim of developing green, affordable, stable and sovereign energy sources, with national choices which may differ, but which in our view must include both renewable energy and nuclear energy, both of which meet these criteria. this is also a diversification objective in terms of sovereignty, as envisaged and proposed in the european commission's repowereu plan. because we obviously need to accelerate this reduction in dependence on russian gas as soon as possible, the commission is proposing ambitious objectives, which we support, but which still need to be decided and discussed in the council. here too, coordination is useful, so that we are not competing with each other for new supply solutions. it is also - as the vice-president and many other speakers have just said - the reduction of our energy consumption whenever we can, because european dependence is also a fact of energy consumption which we must control better. with these tools for accelerating, diversifying and reducing consumption, i believe we have in our hands the tools for european energy independence, which, as you know, france has been defending for a long time, and which this council presidency has put on its agenda, but which, because of the tragic events we are experiencing - the russian invasion of ukraine - requires us to act even faster. thank you for your commitment.
the debate is over. written declarations (rule 171)
in writing. the eu is working tirelessly to address russia's high energy dependence and the associated price increases, as well as to ensure energy security and supply. we in the socialist group in the european parliament believe that solutions are needed to deal with the rise in energy prices, particularly electricity prices. cohesive and coordinated action is absolutely necessary to help the economy of citizens and businesses. the solutions are to accelerate the deployment of indigenous renewable energy and short-term measures to decouple the price of gas from electricity, while eliminating our excessive energy dependence on russia. in this respect, spain can play a very important role in diversification, as it has a large lng (liquified natural gas) infrastructure, including around 30% of the union's regasification capacity.
in writing. energy isolation from russia is a necessity for our future and a moral obligation towards ukraine. three weeks ago, the last notions that russia was a rational partner, at least within certain limits, playing the role of international agreements, were also dispelled. the solution is not only to diversify the suppliers of the current energy mix, but also to change the overall mix, and in particular to innovate to increase energy efficiency. we cannot be as dependent on one supplier as we are in the case of russia. each country has and will have its own energy mix, but the fundamental goal of our independence from russian raw materials is shared. cooperation in the energy sector is essential, particularly in the field of energy storage and joint energy purchasing.
in writing. once again, the european union has set itself objectives without really asking itself whether they are feasible or what the consequences might be: the commission is demanding energy independence in the shortest possible time to tackle a problem which has been known for a long time but which has been ignored until now. the elimination of two thirds of russian gas imports is undoubtedly a geopolitically and strategically extremely important choice, but we must not be under the illusion that it is risk free. further increases in energy costs or energy rationing would have a devastating impact on our economy, already weakened by the years of the pandemic. i would also like to stress the criticisms of the commission's intention to replace russian gas with imported lng. most of the world's producers already have long-term contracts with other buyers, which makes it difficult to negotiate new agreements. secondly, the eu's regasification capacity is already at its maximum and the construction of new plants takes a long time, which is incompatible with the commission's objectives. i would like to conclude by reiterating that the greatest risk is that citizens and businesses will pay for the consequences of years of wrong energy policies, characterized by compromises and green mania.
in writing. following the breakthrough of the european union's coordinated and solidarity-based response to the pandemic, the response to the impacts of actions to support ukraine and the guarantee of access to sustainable, affordable and secure energy for european consumers must be achieved also by following a coordinated, fair and solidarity-based course of action. the commission communication repowereu opens up lines of action for emergency support to households and businesses, for regulation and joint action in the market and for strategic restructuring of the energy market, in particular through increased focus on renewable energy and interconnections. i welcome the measures taken by the council to respond immediately to the crisis, in particular the joint purchase of gas, lng and hydrogen on the market and the exceptional authorisation of support for the most vulnerable companies. i call for the proposal by some member states, including portugal, to fix a maximum price for gas with an impact on the mechanisms for price formation, in particular electricity, and for an exceptional vat reduction to be discussed and adopted. finally, i would stress the need for an exceptional mechanism for approving and financing the new interconnections needed to ensure the diversity of supply and the fluidity of the internal market.
the next item is the debate on the following items: oral question to the council on the state of play of the modernisation of the energy charter treaty [2022/2510 (rsp) ], by bernd lange, on behalf of the committee on international trade (o-000009/2022 b9-0009/22), and oral question to the commission on the state of play of the modernisation of the energy charter treaty [2022/2510 (rsp) ], by bernd lange, on behalf of the committee on international trade (o-000008/2022 b9-0008/22).
executive vice-president of the commission. – mr president, as i mentioned in my introductory remarks, by mid-june the commission will take stock of the negotiations and will draw its conclusions. to come back more specifically on some of the questions which have been raised, first on transparency. the commission is strongly committed to ensuring trade policy is transparent and inclusive, in order to improve legitimacy and public trust. in this context, the commission has published an initial proposal for the modernisation of the energy charter treaty. the energy charter treaty has agreed to published after each round a common public communication. the communication after the first round presented the agenda and issues discussed. after the second round, on the instigation of the eu and some other contracting parties, the contracting parties helped publish the second document, which reflects the discussions of the second round even better, despite the initial wish of several contracting parties not to reveal this information. the eu will continue to urge other contracting parties to improve the transparency of the ect modernisation process. however, at the current stage, given the confidentiality of negotiations, as the contracting parties have decided, the commission is not in a position to share all negotiating documents. nevertheless, the commission is sharing its own proposals, as i said, the initial proposal and the proposal for fossil fuel are covered, and we are informing parliament regularly on the negotiations. the second specific question which was raised concerns a sunset clause and what would be the options to reduce or neutralise the sunset clause? indeed, theoretically speaking, there is a possibility that the contracting parties could enter into an inter se agreement to neutralise the sunset clause. well, as regards this question, we have always been of the view that the ect should not apply between the eu member states in the first place and this has now been confirmed by several european court of justice judgments. thus, an inter se agreement may indeed be needed but would bring little additional benefit. secondly there are probably very few contracting parties at this stage that are willing to enter into such an agreement with the eu and its member states because they have nothing to gain unless they want to withdraw themselves. but again, there is no contracting party that is now hinting at that. so the commission remains of the opinion that the best outcome is to have a reformed energy charter treaty in line with the eu’s sustainable development and climate action objectives, rather than having an unreformed energy charter treaty applying to the eu and member states during the 20 years sunset period triggered by unilateral withdrawal. beyond the eu, a reformed energy charter treaty will leverage a larger collective, positive impact by all contracting parties. this is very important considering that sustainable development and climate action are global challenges that the eu cannot tackle alone. but as i said also in my introductory remarks, if we cannot achieve these objectives, we will consider other options, including withdrawal from the energy charter treaty. and of course, we will look in this case at all possible ways to mitigate the impact of an unreformed ect and 20 years of a sunset clause.
president of the council. mr president, mr vice-president, i have little to add to the comments just made by mr dombrovskis. i simply want to say that we must give ourselves a useful chance for the two rounds of negotiations which are still ahead of us and which i have mentioned, on the second half of april and in may. it is then, in just a few weeks' time, that we will be able to make a real assessment of this modernisation process. can it or cannot it lead satisfactorily to the removal of investment protection for fossil fuels? i repeat, this will be our position with the other member states: if this is not possible in the next few weeks, we will have to consider alternative options. it is preferable to have a modernised charter that covers other countries and could apply its requirements adapted to the paris agreement, the ecological transition and the end of fossil fuels. if this is not possible, we should consider the coordinated withdrawal of the countries of the european union. coordination of a possible withdrawal is also more desirable than a process that would be limited to a few member states and disorganised, because we would obviously have more impact and the opportunity to think about other tools. this is the position i took for the first time on behalf of the french government a few months ago, with barbara pompili in particular. we will defend it in the light of the results, the negotiations and the cycles that come in april and may.
