the session is closed and will resume tomorrow, tuesday 3 may, at 9.00 a.m. with the priority debates on the commission communication on the threats to the safety of journalists and media freedom, in the context of world press freedom day. the rules of procedure have been published and are available on the european parliament's website.
dear colleagues, good morning. i have a short statement from my end because today is world press freedom day. the european parliament has always sought to defend and promote a strong and free press in europe and beyond. our position has always been – and will always be – on the side of the truth, on the side of the truth-seekers. journalists should never have to choose between uncovering the truth and staying alive. they should never be forced to spend years and savings to argue against vexatious lawsuits or slapps. so today, on world press freedom day, we launch the second edition of the daphne caruana galizia prize for journalism, a prize named after a journalist assassinated for speaking the truth. in her memory, we support those who promote and defend core european values and seek to establish the truth through outstanding journalism. i am proud that this parliament stands up for what we believe in. i am proud that we match our rhetoric with action – today and every day – because a strong democracy needs a strong press, and we know that there can be no democracy without freedom of the press. the next item is the debate on the commission statement on threats to the safety of journalists and media freedom, on the occasion of world press freedom day. dear colleagues, i remind you that we are testing some of the recommendations of the focus group on the plenary reform. the free seating is applied, with the exception of the first two rows that are allocated to group leaders. i would like to inform members that this debate is foreseen with one round of political group speakers and also that speakers can continue to speak from the central rostrum. so i remind colleagues to keep an eye on the speakers’ list and to approach the rostrum when your speaking time is imminent.
vice-president of the commission. – madam president, on the occasion of this year’s world press freedom day, i would like to pay tribute, in particular, to journalists who are covering the russian war of aggression against ukraine from the place. they inform us at the risk of their lives. they are our eyes under russian bombs and threats. they show us what russia does not want us to see. indeed, as the president has just said, we have to stand on the side of truth seekers. our first priority is the safety of journalists. this is why the eu has already dedicated more than eur 6 million to emergency support for journalists in ukraine, including protective equipment, training and also relocation when necessary. but even in the eu, journalists are at risk for doing their work. this is why the commission presented in september last year, for the first time ever, recommendations to member states to improve the safety of journalists. we want to provide journalists facing threats with legal and psychological support and increase their protection online and offline, for instance, during demonstrations. but any legislation is only as effective as its implementation. therefore, i call on all member states to fully implement the measures suggested in the recommendation. a few days ago, the commission proposed legislation to protect journalists against abusive lawsuits – strategic lawsuits against public participation (slapps). these abusive proceedings are often david against goliath fights. those bringing these lawsuits are wealthy and powerful, their targets usually not. journalists speak in the public interest, they keep power in check and this is why we need to protect them. i call this a toughness law. this directive is in honour of daphne caruana galizia and her family, who have fought so much for it. we adopted also a recommendation to encourage member states to align their rules with the proposed eu law, including for domestic cases and in all proceedings. the recommendation also calls on member states to take a range of other measures, such as supporting, training and awareness-raising to fight against slapps. and we will go further. we are now preparing, together with commissioner breton, the media freedom act. the act will enshrine, for the first time in eu law, common safeguards to protect media pluralism and the editorial independence of the media. hand in hand with the legislation, we are also dedicating more eu funding to supporting media projects. we have just announced eur 8 million for cross—border journalism partnerships, and more will come. we support innovation and digital transformation as this is important for the sector to thrive in the long term. and, at the same time, with the digital markets act and the digital services act, we have new rules to better protect fundamental rights online and to ensure fairer digital markets. to sum up, the eu has never done so much to protect and promote media freedom and pluralism. this is clearly thanks to the strong support of the european parliament and i would like to thank you for being such a great ally in our common fight to defend our european values and our democracies. recent crises have showed the importance of standing for media freedom and pluralism and protecting our democracies.
on behalf of the ppe group. – madam president, today we are here to honour and to protect the work of journalists. the job of a journalist is a brave job. a reporter in ukraine today is also a freedom fighter on the frontlines of russia’s aggression. dear journalists in kyiv and everywhere in ukraine, you who report on russian attacks and war crimes, you help shield millions of citizens in any of our european countries from foreign propaganda. all of you who write and work as war journalists, local fixers across ukraine, cameramen, producers, all of you working for the media that deliver news different parts of europe. across the western balkans, journalists are exposed to verbal attacks and smear campaigns. this is a region of deeply polarised politics, where the notion of free and independent media is constantly challenged by the idea that journalists serve either the government or opposition politicians. quality journalism and quality media anywhere, however, are about the constant search for facts and the truth. we are not immune to attacks against journalists who seek to uncover the truth in eu member states. many reporters, anchors and well-known people in the media i'm not going to tell you. instead of general ideological rhetoric, i would like to focus on a pragmatic reality, namely the reality of protecting journalists and human rights organizations from bullying, unjustified, life-damaging lawsuits, which are primarily the domain of the powerful, the rich and those with the financial means to defend themselves in this way either against investigative journalists or against some of their unfair activities being pointed out. it is undisputed that any person having standing to bring proceedings has the right to bring an action before the courts to enforce the protection of his rights and legitimate interests. however, this right cannot be absolute. this right cannot be abused to make bullying, unacceptable claims that make life unpleasant for the defendant. the commission, which has already proposed some of these measures, and the member states must take appropriate measures to this end. these are measures in the field of procedural law and in the field of the protection of journalists against physical attacks, in the field of criminal law. in my view, it is essential that such actions, which we simply cannot avoid, have the necessary elements of compulsion that are exceptional and different from other actions. first of all, the plaintiff should be represented by a lawyer, as is the case in administrative and other proceedings, because while it's true that rich people pay for a lawyer, nevertheless, the lawyer's status is in a way a fee that could somehow limit such bullying lawsuits. secondly, the laws on fees, on motions, where such a fee should be increased to the extent that no one can bring such actions at any time, would need to be amended. it wouldn't have made any difference. in the event of success, such a fee would be reimbursed. another very crucial point is that the relevant criminal codes must make it mandatory to hear such a case before it is brought, without the parties being involved in the proceedings, and that the defendant, and this is very important, in this particular case journalists or human rights organisations, should be entitled, by law, to free legal aid through the state legal aid centres. of course, then it's necessary to have more and more adjustments, but about two minutes is not enough. there's a lot to talk about. that's a matter of civil procedure. the inevitable. and then there's the danger of physical attack. this is already the domain of criminal law. it is essential that specialised teams are set up in the relevant police departments, as they are for various activities, drug crime, just another idea, related exclusively to this protection of journalists. i mean, i have to go. two minutes have passed. i could talk about it a lot.
executive vice-president of the commission. – madam president, thank you very, very much for this very rich and interesting debate. as, i think, many of you have mentioned, with this work the european union is pioneering: we are the first to take this endeavour upon us, to make this comprehensive legislative framework and to promote trust in a very promising technology. but the thing is that promoting trustworthy ai is a global endeavour. it is not something that can be done in europe alone. this is why we must complement our legislative work with an ambitious external agenda everywhere we go. we need to influence global standards so that they reflect democratic values – because fighting for that is also fighting for democracy as such. this is what we have done in several international forums, such as the oecd and unesco, and what we are now taking forward bilaterally with our like—minded partners, such as through the eu—us trade and technology council. there is one thing i think very characteristic for the debate today, and it is that dragoş tudorache was very right in his first takeaway from the work of the aida committee. ai is not about technology; ai is politics. the way we deal with technology reflects how we want our societies to develop. so it has indeed been a pleasure to be part of this, i think, very passionate, debate – a debate about how we would want to create societies that are inclusive, where people feel seen and heard and counted in, where we can live with the freedom of not being discriminated against or not being under surveillance by machines, in our lives, in our workplaces. i think this is a very promising starting point for the next chapter of our legislative procedure, but also for europe assessing itself in these global endeavours to be the creators of a human—centric, trustworthy, artificial intelligence. and, in that, i feel very encouraged with the debate today, so thank you very much.
i have to say that i am not in favour of this. the commission has already made a number of proposals. basically, i think we all realize one thing: we don't want to die in beauty here. values are important, but they must be balanced with the innovation of a technology, and that is what matters. we are not going to be able to do that, because we are not going to be able to do that. we need better access to data, and by that i mean personal data, because not every artificial intelligence is going to do without personal data. we need the single market so we have a lot of homework to give you. we need the stronger, more robust digital infrastructure that you mentioned, the ecosystem of excellence, the ecosystem of trust, an ai industrial strategy with sustainable investments, but also clear regulations for ai in the area of security and stronger cooperation, including cybersecurity, etc. but we also need the unconditional will to survive digitally as europe, as the european union, and to lead the way. we need a strategy, a plan, a design and an implementation. we need a lot of money, talent, cyber security, the pooling of our forces in europe so that we can also achieve greatness, european projects and, of course, legal certainty. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. we need the spirit of our society to move forward, and for that we need political leadership that also rests on your shoulders. i would like to thank all those who have made this possible, and i hope that the vote will also indicate what we want and what we are giving you, so to speak, for implementation.
the debate is closed. the vote will take place on tuesday, 3 may 2022. written declarations (article 171) the following documents shall be submitted to the court of justice:
in writing. artificial intelligence is playing an increasingly important role in the economy and democracy. ai-based solutions are increasingly being used in our daily work and life. the use of this technology could in the future significantly improve efficiency, boost innovation and drive economic growth in many european countries. during this mandate we will for the first time regulate artificial intelligence horizontally, and the report by mr voss, which is the result of the work of a special committee set up to consider the future policy needs of the union in this area, is another step forward. however, i would like to stress that we need an even more innovation-friendly approach that recognises the role of intellectual property rights protection, as well as an experimental approach such as dedicated regulatory environments. at the same time, we must strike a balance between the need to implement new technologies and the implementation of environmental policy. i believe that the green deal and technology policy should complement each other. the presumption that the green deal should prevail over everything is not a sensible solution, because it threatens the future of our economies and our overall ability to achieve our goals, both technologically and ecologically. i believe that in the near future we will be able to develop legislation that will seek to maximize the use of this technology while protecting citizens from the risks associated with it.
in writing. the aim is to create human-centred ai that serves our people while respecting their privacy. the report encourages the use of more ai-based solutions. the commission will continue to support the development of the european research area (era) and its activities in the field of research and technological development. the commission will also continue to support the development of the european research area (era) and the european research area (era) in the context of the european research area (era) and the european research area (era) in the context of the european research area (era). but the european union needs to significantly increase its investment in ai as a matter of urgency if we are to remain competitive.
artificial intelligence (ai) is no longer science fiction, but is already a reality today - in everyday life too. ai applications control what music you listen to and what videos you watch online. they make sure that the food you eat isn't unnecessarily treated with pesticides, and they help decide on the loan for your dream home and the determination of your cancer diagnosis. ai, with its vast data fuels, powerful algorithms and vast computing power, has the potential to provide answers to the challenges we face today in virtually every industry: in the social media, in agriculture, in the financial services, in health care, in law enforcement. of course, we must be aware of the possible side effects. we cannot blindly rely on an algorithm that cannot be adequately explained, cannot be monitored, and is fed with extremely incomplete or clearly incorrect data and potentially leads to discrimination. ai will only succeed with a balanced legal framework that allows ai systems to develop and refine themselves using large amounts of data while providing safeguards that enable us to deal with the potential negative side effects.
we have the prime minister of italy, mario draghi, with us today. prime minister, let me start by thanking you for accepting our invitation to address the european parliament as part of our ‘this is europe’ debates where, together with european leaders, we discuss our common agenda for europe’s future. we know, dear colleagues, that prime minister draghi is a committed european who, as president of the european central bank, has already steered the eurozone out of a crisis that could have seriously undermined our monetary union. prime minister, it is largely thanks to your vision, your commitment and your steady hand that we got through. you gave confidence to our people and to our banks, and i have no doubt that we can rely on your expertise as europe again faces challenges of a different kind, but that are just as existential in nature. after the russian army’s illegal and unjustifiable invasion of ukraine, europe faces another ‘whatever it takes’ moment. we have seen unprecedented european coordination, solidarity and unity against this war, and this must remain the blueprint for us going forward, be it on further sanctions, on sending aid to ukraine, on disentangling ourselves from energy dependence on the kremlin, helping the millions forced to flee, or building a new security and defence union. prime minister, you guided your country through the worst of times, coming out from the pandemic stronger. we know that we must take stock of the lessons learned and ensure that we have the flexibility that we need to care for our populations. we must president of the italian council of ministers. president metsola, ladies and gentlemen, fellow citizens, i am very happy to be here in the heart of the cradle of european democracy. i would first like to pay tribute to the memory of david sassoli, who presided over the european parliament in very difficult years. during the pandemic, parliament continued to meet, debate, decide, as a testament to its institutional vitality and sassoli's leadership. sassoli has never stopped working on what he called in his last speech to the european council a 'new project of hope' for 'a europe that innovates, that protects, that illuminates'. this vision of europe is more necessary today than ever. i thank president metsola and all of you for your contribution to carrying this idea forward every day. the war in ukraine is putting the european union in the face of one of the most serious crises in its history. a crisis that is at once humanitarian, security, energy, economic, and that is occurring while our countries are still grappling with the consequences of the greatest health emergency in the last hundred years. the european response to the pandemic has been united, courageous, effective. scientific research has delivered, with unprecedented speed, vaccines capable of curbing contagion, of drastically reducing the severity of the disease. we've organized the largest vaccination campaign in recent history, which has saved lives, gotten boys and girls back into school, gotten the economy going again. we have approved the next generation eu, the first major project for the reconstruction of europe, financed by everyone, to meet everyone's needs. the same readiness and determination, the same spirit of solidarity must now guide us in the challenges ahead. the institutions that our predecessors built over the past decades have served the people of europe well, but they are inadequate to the reality that we face today. the pandemic and the war have called on the european institutions to assume responsibilities never assumed before. the geopolitical landscape is undergoing rapid and profound transformation. we need to move as quickly as possible. and we must ensure that the management of the crises we are experiencing does not lead us back to square one, but allows a transition to a more just and sustainable economic and social model. we need a pragmatic federalism, one that embraces all the areas affected by the transformations under way - from the economy to energy to security. i have spoken of pragmatic federalism, but i must add that our european values of peace, solidarity and humanity need to be defended now more than ever. and this defence is now more difficult than ever for individual states and will become increasingly difficult. we need not only a pragmatic federalism, but an ideal federalism. if this means starting a process which will lead to the revision of the treaties, then embrace it with courage and confidence. if we can draw strength from the tragic events of these years to take a step forward, if we can imagine a more efficient functioning of the european institutions which will allow us to find timely solutions to the problems of the citizens, then we can give them a europe in which they can identify with pride. russia's aggression against ukraine has called into question the european union's greatest achievement: peace in our continent. a peace based on respect for territorial boundaries, the rule of law, democratic sovereignty; a peace based on the use of diplomacy as a means of resolving crises between states; the european union must not allow itself to be overwhelmed by the violence of the russian army against innocent civilians. we must support ukraine, its government and its people, as president zelensky has asked and continues to ask us to do. in a war of aggression there can be no equivalence between the invader and the resistance. we want ukraine to remain a free, democratic, sovereign country. protecting ukraine means protecting ourselves, it means protecting the project of security and democracy that we have built together over the last 70 years. helping ukraine means above all working for peace. our priority is to achieve a ceasefire as soon as possible, to save lives and to allow those humanitarian interventions in favour of civilians which today remain very difficult. a truce would also give new impetus to the negotiations, which have so far failed to produce the desired results. europe can and must play a central role in fostering dialogue. we must do so because of our geography, which places us alongside this war, and therefore at the forefront of dealing with all its possible consequences. we must do so because of our history, which has shown us capable of building a stable and lasting peace, even after bloody conflicts. italy, as a founding country of the european union, as a country which believes deeply in peace, is ready to take the lead in reaching a diplomatic solution. already today war is having a profound impact on our countries. since the beginning of the conflict, some 5.3 million people have left ukraine for the european union - mostly women and children. this is more than double the number of refugees in the union at the end of 2020 approximately 2.5 million. italy believes in the european values of welcome and solidarity. we have welcomed over 105 000 ukrainian refugees, thanks to the generosity of families, volunteers, ngos, to whom i extend my deepest thanks. other countries including poland, romania, germany and slovakia have made even greater efforts. many refugees want to return home soon, and some have already begun to do so. however, we do not know how the conflict will develop or how long it will last. we must be prepared to continue our initial momentum so that ukrainian refugees can be better integrated into our societies. from an economic point of view, the conflict has caused instability in the functioning of global supply chains and volatility in the price of raw materials and energy. ukrainian food supplies have collapsed due to the devastation of war and the export blockades imposed by russia on black sea and azov sea ports. ukraine is the fourth largest foreign food supplier to the european union it sends us about half of our maize imports, and a quarter of our vegetable oils. russia and ukraine account for over a quarter of global wheat exports. nearly 50 countries of the world depend on them for more than 30 percent of their imports. in march, prices of cereals and other staple foods reached record highs. there is a strong risk that rising prices, coupled with the reduced availability of fertilisers, will produce food crises. according to fao, 13 million more people could suffer from hunger between 2022 and 2026 due to the war in ukraine. many countries, especially in africa and the middle east, are more vulnerable to these risks and may experience periods of political and social instability. we can't let that happen. our commitment through the development banks and multilateral financial institutions and our commitment on a bilateral basis must be maximum. as for energy, the price of crude oil, which fluctuated between $70 and $90 a barrel between december and january, is now around $105 after a peak of $130 in march. the price of gas on the european market is around 100 euros per megawatt hour about five times that of a year ago. these increases following the upturns seen before the conflict have pushed the inflation rate to levels not seen for decades. in the euro area, the price index rose by 7.5 per cent in april compared with a year ago, and is having a significant impact on household purchasing power and business output levels. the european economy is in a phase of slowdown: in the first three months of 2022, euro area gross domestic product grew by 0.2% compared to the last quarter of 2021. the international monetary fund forecasts that the european union will grow by 2.9 per cent this year, compared with 4 per cent estimated until recently. each of these crises would require a strong response from the european union. the sum of these factors requires a decisive acceleration of the integration process. in the coming months we must show the citizens of europe that we are capable of leading a europe that lives up to its values, its history and its role in the world. a stronger, more cohesive, more sovereign europe, able to take its future into its own hands, as chancellor merkel said some time ago. over the past 75 years, european integration has often been the best - practical and ideal - response to common challenges. the founding fathers of the european union realised that economic development and social progress were difficult to achieve by the resources of individual member states alone. they identified the supranational model as the only one capable of uniting the interests of the peoples of europe and of exerting influence on events that would otherwise have been beyond their reach. integration has been a gradual process, marked by crises and relaunch, by successes achieved despite internal divisions and sometimes in the face of extreme resistance. a result built "piece by piece, sector by sector", to quote robert schuman, because the european union could not be "born out of thin air, like an ideal city". europe responded to the trauma of the second world war by creating the first institutions for economic cooperation. i am thinking of the european union of payments, which helped to bring about a return to monetary stability and the resumption of trade, or the economic community of coal and steel, which abolished customs barriers and other obstacles to the free movement of goods in crucial sectors of the economy. the geopolitical tensions that arose from the suez crisis in '56 helped to accelerate the path to the treaties of rome. faced with the collapse of the bretton woods system in 1971, european countries reacted by establishing the monetary snake and then the european monetary system. the growing euroscepticism of the 1980s was met by the targeted programmes of intervention proposed by the delors commission and the single act of 1986. the end of the soviet union and the reunification of germany were followed by the signing of the maastricht treaty, the creation of monetary union and, finally, the enlargement of the european union to the east. the eurozone crisis in the early years of the last decade led to a strengthening and modernisation of economic institutions, starting with the european central bank. the pandemic, as i mentioned earlier, has brought us together and led to the creation of the next generation eu. you see, this long journey of integration has changed our lives for the better, because it has given us peace, prosperity and a social model to be proud of. the single market has not only given a boost to europe's economy in a difficult time, but it has also provided protection for consumers, workers and forms of social security that are unique in the world. we have built common democratic institutions, such as this parliament, in which we reach joint decisions and enforce respect for fundamental rights. we have made the european union not only an economic area but also a place for the defence of human rights and human dignity. it is a legacy we must not squander, we must not back down from. now is the time to take this path forward. the conference on the future of europe will conclude on 9 may, and the final declaration calls for us to be very ambitious. we want to be at the forefront of designing this new europe. in a geopolitical context that has suddenly become much more dangerous and uncertain, we must address the economic and social emergency and ensure the safety of our citizens. investment in defence must be made with a view to improving our collective capabilities as the european union and as nato. the last european council took an important decision by adopting the 'strategic compass', which we must implement quickly. but we must move quickly beyond these first steps and build effective coordination between defence systems. our security spending is about three times that of russia, but it's broken down into 146 defense systems. the united states has only 34. it is a profoundly inefficient allocation of resources, which hinders the building of a real european defence. strategic autonomy in defence is first and foremost achieved through greater efficiency in military spending in europe. it is appropriate to convene a conference to rationalize and optimize our investment in military spending. the construction of a common defence must also be accompanied by a unified foreign policy and effective decision-making mechanisms. we must move beyond the principle of unanimity, which gives rise to an intergovernmental logic of cross-cutting vetoes, and move towards decisions taken by qualified majority. a europe which is able to take decisions in a timely manner is a more credible europe in the eyes of its citizens and the world. the first step must be to speed up the process of enlargement. the full integration of countries with european aspirations does not threaten the survival of the european project. it's part of his realization. italy supports the immediate opening of accession negotiations with albania and north macedonia, in line with the decision taken by the european council in march 2020. we want to give new impetus to the negotiations with serbia and montenegro and ensure that the legitimate expectations of bosnia and herzegovina and kosovo are given the utmost attention. we are in favour of the accession of all these countries and we want ukraine to join the european union. we need to follow the entry path we've drawn, but we also need to proceed as expeditiously as possible. the solidarity shown towards the ukrainian refugees must also push us towards a truly european management of migrants who come from other contexts of war and exploitation. more generally, it is necessary to define an effective european mechanism for managing migration flows, which goes beyond the logic of the dublin treaty. we need to strengthen and make the return agreements really effective, but we also need to strengthen the legal channels of entry into the european union. in particular, we must pay greater attention to the mediterranean, given its strategic location as a bridge to africa and the middle east. we cannot look at the mediterranean merely as a border area, on which to erect barriers. the mediterranean is home to many young countries, ready to pour their enthusiasm into their relationship with europe. with them, the european union must build a real partnership, not only economic but also political and social. the mediterranean must be a pole of peace, prosperity and progress. energy policy is an area in which the mediterranean countries can and must play a key role for the future of europe. europe is facing a profound geopolitical reorientation, which will increasingly shift its strategic axis to the south. the war in ukraine has shown the deep vulnerability of many of our countries to moscow. italy is one of the member states most exposed: in fact, about 40 percent of the natural gas we import comes from russia. we have no coal, we have no nuclear power and we have almost no oil. such energy dependence is economically unwise and geopolitically dangerous. italy intends to take all the necessary decisions to defend its security and that of europe. we have supported the sanctions that the european union has decided to impose on russia, including those in the energy sector. we will continue to do so with the same conviction in the future. in the last few weeks we have moved with the utmost speed and determination to diversify our gas supplies. and we've taken important simplification steps to accelerate the production of renewable energy, which is essential to making our growth more sustainable. the reduction in fossil fuel imports from russia makes it inevitable that europe will look to the mediterranean to meet its needs. i am referring to gas deposits as a transitional fuel, but above all to the enormous opportunities offered by renewables in africa and the middle east. the countries of southern europe, and italy in particular, are strategically placed to harness this energy production and to act as a bridge to the northern countries. our centrality tomorrow depends on the investments we can make today. at the same time, we must find solutions now to protect households and businesses from the rising cost of energy. moderating fuel bills and prices is also a way to make any sanctions more sustainable over time. since the beginning of the crisis, italy has been calling for a european ceiling on the price of gas imported from russia. russia sells almost two thirds of its natural gas exports to the european union largely through pipelines which cannot be redirected to other buyers. our proposal would allow us to use our bargaining power to reduce the exorbitant costs which today weigh on our economies. at the same time, this measure would reduce the sums we send to president putin every day, which inevitably finance his military campaign. we also want to look at the structural mechanism of electricity price formation, which depends on the cost of producing the most expensive energy source, which is usually gas. even in normal times, the production costs of energy from fossil sources are higher than those of renewable sources. this is a problem that is bound to get worse over time. as the share of renewable energy in our energy mix increases, we will have prices that are less and less representative of the generation cost of the entire market if we continue to have this system. in this period of very high volatility in the gas market, the price difference is disproportionate. the pressure on the gas market has been passed on to the electricity market, although the cost of producing renewables, from which we now obtain a substantial share of our energy, has remained very low. in italy, in the first four months of this year, the price of electricity has quadrupled compared to the same period last year, with a very severe impact on the economy. and the italian government, but also other governments, have reacted strongly to protect businesses and families, especially the most vulnerable. italy alone has spent about 30 billion euros this year alone. the emergency management of these surcharges has many limitations, first and foremost the sustainability for the public budget. the problem is systemic and must be solved by structural solutions, which break the link between gas and electricity prices. the problem of energy costs will be the focus of the next european council. strong and immediate decisions are needed for the benefit of all european citizens. the various crises arising from the conflict in ukraine come at a time when europe already faced enormous spending requirements. the ecological and digital transitions require us to make unwavering investments. to these must be added the costs of war, which we must face immediately, if we are to prevent our continent from sinking into recession. in both cases, these are asymmetrical costs, affecting different sections of the population and different sectors of production and therefore requiring different compensatory measures. no national budget can support these efforts on its own. no country can be left behind. it is a question of social peace on our continent, of our ability to support sanctions, especially in those countries which for historical reasons are more dependent on russia. the european union has already devised some useful instruments to govern these challenges. these are the responses we have put in place during the pandemic and which have ensured a rapid and widespread economic recovery for the european union. we need to build on that success, and adapt these same tools to the circumstances that we have in front of us. sure the european temporary support instrument to mitigate the risks of unemployment in an emergency has provided loans to member states to support the labour market. the european union should broaden its scope to provide countries that request it with new funding to mitigate the impact of energy surcharges. i am referring to measures to reduce the cost of living, but also to temporary support for the lowest wages, for example as we did yesterday, with compensation measures for the lowest wages. these have the advantage of protecting the purchasing power of households, especially the most vulnerable. the use of a lending facility such as sure would avoid the use of non-repayable grants to pay for national current expenditure measures. at the same time, in a period of rising interest rates, it would provide member states with weaker public finances with a cheaper alternative to borrowing on the market. we could thus broaden the scope of support measures and at the same time limit the risk of financial instability. this is a measure which should be put in place very quickly, because we have been in this situation for eight, nine or ten months, to enable governments to intervene immediately to support the economy. as regards long-term investment in areas such as defence, energy, food security and industry, the model is the next generation eu. the system of periodic payments, linked to timely checks on the achievement of objectives, provides a virtuous mechanism for controlling the quality of expenditure. spending the resources allocated to us well is essential to our credibility with the citizens and our european partners who, as i have said many times, have agreed to tax their citizens in order to help italy and other countries which have used these grants. good government is not just about responding to the crises of the moment. it is to move quickly to anticipate what is to come. the founding fathers of europe have shown us how to make democracy effective on our continent in its progressive transformations. european integration is the best ally we have to face the challenges that history has set before us. today, as in all the decisive turning points since the end of the war, we need determination, vision, but above all unity. i'm sure we'll find them again, together. two or three specific points before we arrive at what i believe is a shared vision of the aims and objectives of our european vision. two or three specific points: italy will be in favour of an embargo on all fossil fuels. as i said in my speech, we have supported the sanctions that the european union has decided to impose on russia, including those in the energy sector, and we will continue to do so with the same conviction in the future. we must do something, but it is not enough to support sanctions. we must work to become independent of russian gas. we have done so and we will continue to do so. we have moved with remarkable speed to diversify our sources of supply and to accelerate investment in renewables at a pace that was not expected last year. the measures taken yesterday by the government also lead to a liberalization of the permitting processes for the installation of renewable energy, because this is the only obstacle that exists today in italy, but i believe also in many european countries. these are the permitting processes for renewable energy installations. i think we made a very important step forward yesterday with regard to italy. companies that made profits and poor families, no doubt. companies that have been in the electricity business and the fossil fuel import business have made incredible profits. i would like to ask the commissioner whether he is aware that the commission has not yet taken a decision on the matter. the same is happening to companies in italy and other countries that have imported fossil fuels. our government yesterday decided to renew the taxation of these extra profits of companies that imported and produced different types of energy. the overall taxation has now reached 25 percent of this definition of extra profits that the government has given. in essence, this is a total of some 11 billion between the first and the second measures. and so we have been able to give a flat check, a one-size-fits-all check of 200 euros to 28 million people, to those of the 28 million people, that is to say pensioners and employees, who earn little. all those who are in the lowest or lowest bracket, i.e. who earn up to 35 000 euros a year, will get this check equally for all. so a very progressive measure, of course, that rewards the lower income brackets a lot and that compensates in part, in some cases largely, for the loss of purchasing power that these families have had due to inflation. you see, this is a way of helping the poorest families without necessarily, as it were, undermining the soundness of the public budget. this is very important for us because, as you know, our budget requires special care. second point on the climate. this government was born, as i recall in a speech in parliament, as an ecological government. so it's making climate, the green transition and also the digital transition the cornerstone. in that sense, i have to say that our minister of the environment has been extraordinary. he did what was hardly imaginable even a year and a half ago. he made extraordinary arrangements. we may disagree about the 110% super bonus and we may disagree about the validity of this provision. i will give you just one example. the cost of efficiency has more than tripled with the 110% measures. the prices of the investments needed to carry out the restructuring are more than tripled, because 110 per cent in itself takes away the incentive to negotiate the price and so this is the result. in any case, things are going on in parliament, the government has done what it could do and our minister is very good. thirdly: changes to the pnrr. then we must understand each other. first of all, the first problem with the pnrr is to ensure that the construction sites for public works and public works remain open and that those for new tenders are opened. to this end, yesterday the government allocated an initial 3 billion to enable businesses to recover 90 percent of the increase in raw material prices. this measure has been very well received, not only by the industries producing these infrastructures which are involved in public works, but by everyone in general. so this is the path we've chosen. so before we talk about changing the pnrr, let's make it work. the pnrr is not old. he's not old at all. we met all the objectives. we'll continue to do that now, at the end of june. we will do the same on december 30. he's not old. so there are points and if we need to change certain things in certain points, in certain sectors, specific investments, but we are not talking about revolutions in the pnrr. there are investments which may have to be modified and for which special action will be required. those are the three specific things i wanted to say. but let's now look at the big picture of this exchange. i believe that this exchange is characterized by a deep sharing. shared visions, shared projects, shared ideals. the union's challenges are many, we have seen, the first of them all. i agree with what the last group leader probably said, and he is not a group leader, i do not know. this is undoubtedly the main objective towards which the european union must move, towards which this government, my government, is moving, but there are many others and the answer to all of them is: the european union is a great ally. all of them. i said pragmatic federalism, what does that mean? it means that for many of these challenges, the only way to deal with them is to work together. tackling them together is not just about financing them together. because you think, i'm a banker, so i always think that. but that's not it. to tackle them together means to design them together, to supervise them together, to make sure that the money is well spent all together. italy is deeply aware of this and feels, as you know, because i have said it many times, very responsible for the good use of these funds which have been allocated for the next generation. but it will also be the case for decisions on energy, public investment, the green transition and digital. these are challenges of scale. you know that the green transition and the digital transition have been taken into account, so the need between now and 2030, the financial need for the entire european union is about 2 trillion euros. so these are not figures that can be addressed with national budgets. so we have two objectives: one is strategic autonomy as far as defence is concerned. we have said many times that a strong europe also means a strong nato. but for strategic autonomy, as i said in my speech and i say again, the first step is to make our military spending more efficient. i repeat: three times the cost of russia. it is not conceivable that anything can be done until military spending is made more efficient, more coordinated. that is why i have, as it were, called for a conference on this objective. i share the need, and this is the experience of the last few years, to achieve autonomy also for certain productions which are crucial for our continent. so, for example, microprocessors. it's one kind of thing, but there are other productions that are crucial in the production chains, that actually prove to be essential and for which strategic autonomy proves to be essential. the war, and even earlier, has shown that the market and production chains do not always function smoothly and that their disruption can create deep crises. so we have to equip ourselves for that, too. i have already said that this is programmatic federalism, but i would like to emphasize something else, what i have called ideal federalism. at the moment our values are being challenged in a way that has not been seen since the european union was founded. we must stand united in the face of these challenges. it is a question of the right of the people to selfdetermination, and it is a question of the right of the people to selfdetermination. so there is the real difficulty, in a sense, that we have to overcome and we have to be able to overcome it in order to succeed as europe. and finally, as i said before, if this is what i believe is necessary, if this is what will make it necessary, if this is what will lead to the beginning of a process of revision of the treaties, let us proceed with courage and confidence. the next item is the debate on the commission statement on the ongoing hearings under article 7(1) teu regarding poland and hungary. i will remind you that free seating is applied with the exception of the first two rows, which are allocated to group leaders, and also inform members that this debate is foreseen with one round of political group speakers. the interventions in the chamber will continue to be made from the central rostrum except for catch—the—eye, blue cards and points of order. i therefore kindly invite you to keep an eye on the speakers’ list and to approach when your speaking time is imminent.
member of the commission. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for putting the article 7 proceedings against poland and hungary back on the agenda of this plenary. as you know, the commission considers it important to keep the article 7 procedures on the council's agenda. the commission is also at the disposal of the council to support it in this context, in the course of hearings or in the context of the reports we present to it. it is in the same spirit that we naturally welcome the debate in the plenary with you. as regards the situation in poland, as you know, the fifth hearing under the article 7 procedure was held before the general affairs council on 22 february. the commission informed the council of developments in recent months, including a number of decisions of the polish constitutional tribunal calling into question the foundations of the union's legal order. these decisions have led us to initiate infringement proceedings before the court of justice, not only over the question of the primacy of european law, but also over our comments on the lack of independence of this constitutional court. the commission has also informed the council of several recent judgments of the european court of justice and the european court of human rights, which have confirmed the commission's concerns regarding the rule of law in poland, in particular on the issue of judicial independence, which i have just mentioned in relation to the constitutional tribunal. the commission could only conclude that the situation on the ground continued to give rise to very serious concerns in recent weeks. we have closely followed the legislative developments concerning the amendments to the polish legislation aimed at amending the disciplinary regime for judges in poland. the fact that there seems to be momentum towards reforming the disciplinary regime for judges in poland is a positive step. yet what will ultimately matter is the extent to which the legislation, as finally adopted by the polish parliament, if it is possible to go to final adoption, will address the requirements set out by the court of justice in its ruling of 14 and 15 july last year. i also note that last week the polish government put forward two draft laws which seek to fundamentally change the structure of the ordinary judiciary, including by changing the structure of courts and by transferring judges. we understand that these draft laws are now subject to public consultations. in this respect, i would stress that any new legislation concerning the judiciary must comply with the requirements of eu law on judicial independence, taking into account european standards and the case law of the european court of justice in this regard. the commission will continue to follow closely the legislative development in that respect and you know that it is in line with some decisions of the court of justice, including decisions on interim measures and including a decision on a fine of eur 1 million per day if there is no fulfilment of the requirements of such a decision on interim measures. and we are now at a total amount of more than eur 160 million with such a fine, until there is a possible positive development in poland. as regards the situation in hungary, the commission has had the opportunity to explain on different occasions that it shares a significant number of concerns expressed by the european parliament in its reasoned opinion and proposal triggering the article 7 procedure. the commission has also made it very clear that whatever position the council takes on the article 7 procedure, it must ensure a fair handling of the reasoned proposal tabled by parliament. the commission stressed this again in the geneva council of 14 december last year during a state of play point on the situation in hungary. hungary's fourth hearing under the article 7 procedure has been scheduled for the meeting of the geneva council of 23 may. we appreciate that the french presidency has included this hearing in the council's agenda. in addition to the article 7 procedures, the commission, as i said, has all the instruments at its disposal to protect the rule of law, and we do not hesitate to use them. one of these is the rule of law conditionality regulation, a financial instrument. we have to protect the eu budget and financial interests against breaches of the rule of law, as the court of justice has underlined. last week, the commission sent a written notification to hungary under the conditionality regulation. the commission has identified a number of rule of law issues, including corruption-related issues, that constitute reasonable grounds for the launch of the procedure under the general regime of conditionality. in particular, the commission should be able to assess the impact of the measures on the union budget and the budgetary impact of the measures on the union budget. the identified issues, together with the long-standing commission recommendations and request to address them, combined with the lack of measures to address the identified issues in a structured manner over time, are indicative of a serious risk to the sound financial management of the union budget and to the protection of the financial interests of the union. hungary can now submit comments on the findings in the written notification within two months. it may also propose the adoption of remedial measures to address the findings. such a written notification is the first formal step of the procedure set out in the conditionality regulation, which establishes exchanges between the commission and the member state concerned with a view to addressing the commission's findings and protecting the union budget. throughout the procedure, hungary has the possibility to submit remedial measures. we are of course continuing to monitor the situation in all the other member states on the basis of the same regulation. the commission also does not hesitate to open infringement proceedings to address concerns about the rule of law. in the case of hungary, the commission has launched several infringement procedures related to respect for eu values. these procedures included the rights of civil society organisations, academic freedom, the rights of migrants and asylum seekers, media freedom and the rights of lgbtiq persons. as regards the situation in poland, as i said earlier, the commission decided last december to open a new infringement procedure against poland concerning the polish constitutional tribunal and its recent case law. this court expressly called into question the primacy of union law, and we added our doubts about its independence, which had already been raised in the context of article 7 since 2017. where relevant, the commission also has other instruments, such as the european semester, to promote and uphold the rule of law. in 2019, the council, following the commission's proposal, recommended to hungary to strengthen its anti-corruption framework and to strengthen judicial independence. in 2020, the council recommended to poland to enhance the investment climate, in particular by safeguarding judicial independence. the commission shall assess the national recovery and resilience plans in order to assess whether they are expected to contribute to effectively addressing all, or a significant subset, of the challenges identified in the relevant country-specific recommendations. moreover, in july the commission will publish its third annual rule of law report, and you know that for the first time the report will include recommendations. these recommendations will help member states to focus their follow-up on the key issues, including on justice, anti-corruption, media freedom, and checks and balances. they should also help and support member states in their efforts to uphold the rule of law and to prevent problems from emerging or deepening. and in conclusion, ladies and gentlemen, i regret that i am not able to report today on the positive developments in the rule of law in poland and hungary. i can only hope that the ongoing article 7 proceedings, like the other proceedings i have just mentioned, will give the polish and hungarian authorities time to reflect on how to respond to the concerns expressed. i also thank the french presidency again for keeping the article 7 proceedings against poland and hungary on the council's agenda. and we will of course continue to speak at every opportunity before the general affairs council when the items are put on the agenda, as i announced for hungary at the end of this month, as we will continue to move forward in all the other procedures we have initiated, until the legal systems in hungary and poland are fully brought into line with the european standards we are trying to share across the union.
on behalf of the ppe group. – madam president, before i go into the content of my contribution here today, i do have to say that i find it disappointing that we are having this debate today only with commissioner reynders. this is nothing personal against commissioner reynders obviously, but the fact that we’re talking about the ongoing article 7 procedures and there is an empty chair on my left, even though we all know that the main challenge, when it comes to article 7 procedures, lies with the council – it is very disappointing that the council is not present here today for this debate. in the plenary session in march or april, we already discussed the ongoing procedures for hungary and poland in detail and the lack of any progress when it comes to protection of the rule of law. so today it is also good to look at the procedural issues that we have in this european union. for the epp, the european union is, first and foremost, a community of values. a shared commitment to democracy, the rule of law and fundamental rights is the glue that holds our union together. we can make all the progress we want on the internal market, on trade policy, in any other policy field, whatever we want, but, without a joint commitment to our founding values, none of that progress will ever be sustainable for the future. because how can we create a single area of freedom, justice and security if we cannot rely on the impartiality and independence of judges in all member states? how can we expect to have a level playing field for our smes if we cannot rely on the fact that member states will actually follow the rules that we have set together? and how can we count on the democratic legitimacy of the decision—making process when national elections are marred by the absence of a level playing field? when the rule of law is undermined in one member state, it affects the whole of the european union. this is precisely why we have always been very strict with countries that want to join the european union. the rule of law and the copenhagen criteria are the main focal points of the whole accession procedure and for good reasons. but, when we look back, it’s also fair to say that, as soon as an accession treaty is signed, that scrutiny seems to somehow disappear. this is a situation that we can no longer accept. the rule of law is so important and so crucial that it requires a constant and full commitment from all european institutions. it’s not that no progress has been made. the annual rule of law reporting that the commissioner referred to is a very welcome and important step because it also shows that the rule of law and the debates we have here in this parliament are not directed towards only one or two countries, but it’s something that all member states need to work on. but, even more importantly, with the conditionality mechanism, we took the historic step of linking values to the european budget, making sure that european taxpayers’ money is not used to further undermine and weaken the rule of law and democracy, but instead to strengthen it. we welcome the commission’s decision to send a formal notification to hungary, and we look forward to seeing the concrete follow—up. we call on the council, in that context, to show full political commitment to bringing this procedure to a successful conclusion without delay. as much as the annual rule of law report and the conditionality mechanisms are necessary and welcome supplements to the article 7 procedure, just as with the article 7 procedure, without real political commitment in the council, added value will always remain limited. for a long time, it seemed that, in the council, the rule of law was, like voldemort in harry potter, something that should not be mentioned by name. even though we now see article 7 hearings resume in the council – and we welcome this resumption under the french presidency – actions speak much louder than words, and it’s actions that we need. we need concrete recommendations with strict deadlines. eu leaders cannot just talk about the rule of law every now and again and then pat themselves on the back for a job well done, tick off the box on the to-do list and continue work as usual. we need concrete recommendations. if nothing concrete comes out of these hearings, there is little point in having them at all. so concrete recommendations with strict deadlines and, if these deadlines are not met, we need to look at all the other possibilities that the article 7 procedures offer us. we cannot allow our common values to be put at risk. this is also our main call to the council in the resolution we will adopt this week: show genuine commitment in defending our founding values and make real and meaningful progress in the ongoing article 7 procedures. i know there are people who argue that, with the terrible war in ukraine ongoing, now is not the time to focus on rule of law issues at home, and that it would not be fair to bother member states like hungary and poland with procedures or conditionality at a time when the polish and hungarian people are making such great efforts to welcome ukrainian refugees. but let me be absolutely clear and repeat what i said last week. yes, we need solidarity in the eu. we need to help the member states most affected in hosting hundreds of thousands of ukrainian refugees. this is why we are together in one union. but let me also be clear: helping member states deal with an unprecedented crisis should not come at the expense of being silent about rule of law issues at home. it would be cynical, while ukrainians are fighting – are dying – for freedom, democracy and the rule of law, to turn a blind eye to the attack of those very same values at home. if anything, putin’s russia should be a stark warning to all of us about the dangers of a system where checks and balances, democracy and the rule of law have ceased to exist. it should embolden us to fight for those values even more passionately at home.
on behalf of the renew group. – madam president, mr commissioner, dear colleagues, the trouble with authoritarian politics is that without pushback, it becomes ever more aggressive and dangerous. the european union has learned this the hard way with putin. but it seems to me that we haven’t really learned this lesson when it comes to our own values and rule of law in europe. we now have a member state government that has stifled independent media, captured independent institutions, which is spreading hate against minorities, and where billions of eu money has disappeared into the hands of oligarchs close to the ruling party. we also have a member state whose government works to dismantle the independent judiciary by targeting judges, by firing them. now, all of this takes place in the eu, a democratic community, with eu financial support and despite the many mechanisms and tools that we have in place to protect our values. today we are here to discuss article 7, which if you look at it, is a fairly straightforward procedure with hearings, with recommendations and ultimately sanctions. so on paper, it is a pretty powerful tool. in practice, the council has made it toothless and quite opaque. hearings are random and non—transparent, and even today, even after everything that has happened in hungary, the council has failed to even agree that this constitutes a serious breach of eu values, despite no unanimity needed for that statement. now thankfully, and after much hesitation, the commission has finally activated the conditionality regulation. but even here there is a risk that the scope will be too narrow and that it will fail to address the systemic deficiencies and risks that stem from the absence of checks and balances in the rule of law, which is precisely what this regulation is for. colleagues, i remember last time around many of you were saying when we had this debate that the situation is now unprecedented with a full—scale war on our borders. and now here we are four weeks later after the massacres in bucha, mariupol and many others, and what strikes me is that i hear many voices, even in this house, saying that while in times of war we should relax our principles and our rules when it comes to defending democracy in europe. but i strongly believe that this is precisely the opposite of what is needed. after putin’s invasion, many have said that we are now locked in a broader, protracted conflict between autocracy and democracy, between vladimir putin’s model and our european model, between tyranny and freedom, between brute force and the rule of law. and i agree. i agree that this is this is the conflict that we’re facing, which is precisely why, now more than ever, it’s important to protect our democratic model in europe, why we cannot relent, why we cannot now relax our rules, because otherwise, how are we going to prevail in this conflict that is upon us?

mr. chairman, i join my colleagues in calling for the unacceptability of these attacks and, of course, for the resilience of our cybersecurity. unfortunately, we're starting to see our weakness and european cybersecurity in action. certainly every eu member state is vulnerable, and we've seen these attacks in practice. in my country, it's not just attacks on government offices, but it's even attacks on hospitals, on transportation companies. we must also recognise the serious threat these attacks pose to our member states and their citizens, businesses and institutions. at the same time, we should build our system's resilience and readiness for these attacks and also change our policies, as our colleagues have talked about. cybersecurity is part of our critical infrastructure. and we should also help ukraine, because i really feel that we have an eu space agency, but the internet in ukraine was saved by elon musk. i think we should really think about this.
vice-president of the commission. – mr president, thank you for this rich debate. this is a defining moment for a europe that protects. we are now at a crossroads. what is happening in ukraine and in our neighbourhood is not isolated. it is a sign of things to come. it prefigures what modern warfare will be like. cyber-security is no longer a side tech issue of concern to the specialists. it is a fundamental pillar of the security of our economies, of our societies and of our democracies. it has nothing to do with industry. it has a lot to do with democracy. we have no time to lose if we want our union to be prepared, resilient and able to respond to these threats. the perpetrators will not wait for us. we need to work together, now more than ever, and put in full motion what we have already identified and announced in our 2020 eu cyber-security strategy. the whole lot leads to critical infrastructures, the cyber resilience act, the cyber-security skills gaps and, of course, investment. these are the foundations of our european cybersecurity shield. but, even more importantly, this is now the moment to move from a cyber-shield to an actual cyber-defence capability, and i am sure that we will be able to rise to this challenge. let me conclude with a final point. members of the european parliament, mr waszczykowski and mr papadakis, after thousands of dead, after bombarding the innocent and the vulnerable, after five million ukrainian refugees that have been kicked out of their homes, in today’s debate suggested that we should be ‘realistic’ with russia. that’s their assessment of what needs to be done well, with all respect, i think it’s not us that have to be realistic with russia. it’s russia that has to be respectful to us, and they should stop warfare now, be it with bombs, cannons or with computers. the best moment to stop is now.

member of the commission. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, i would first like to thank you for this debate, which demonstrates the work we have to do together to uphold the values which, i repeat, were endorsed by the member states when they joined the european union or when they joined the european union, by approving the treaties. these are not imposed rules which the commission is trying to enforce and which are of its own accord: these are values which are enshrined in the treaties, i repeat, and approved by the member states. as regards the report itself, i would like to confirm what i have already said on several occasions: it is the commission's report. this is the commission's assessment of the rule of law, in a very correct way, in the 27 member states - when i say correct, that is to say on an equal basis: we work in the same way, with the same methodology and the same principles with regard to the 27 member states. but this is the commission's assessment, after the debates we have had, as i have recalled, with very many participants, whether they be the authorities of the member states, the judicial or political authorities, but also civil society and a whole range of stakeholders. in the end, this is the commission's report, which we are eager to discuss with a whole range of actors to ensure its follow-up, and we are doing this in the context of the debate here in the european parliament. we do this in the national parliaments as we do in the council, in the various council formations, but first of all in the general affairs council, and as we do in the member states with civil society. the aim is to ensure continuous follow-up of these successive reports. it is a real annual cycle, with the intention of establishing a culture of the rule of law. and i would like to confirm that the cycle now ends with an adoption by the commission in july, but we want to start the debate as soon as september and i would like to say that i am fully open to the idea of organising what has been mentioned as a values week where not only the rule of law report but also the other elements would be dealt with. there is a report, as you know, on the charter of fundamental rights. there are debates about democracy. we are quite prepared to discuss these various aspects with the european parliament. i would also insist that we have this debate on all the member states. i think this is important, even though i understand the point about the systemic nature of the problem which i myself have already mentioned in some member states. the report on the various national chapters makes a very clear distinction between the various member states. this report is primarily concerned with prevention. and you will have seen - i repeat, i am ready to talk to you about each of the member states - that very many reforms are being implemented in a very large number of member states to address the concerns expressed in the report. this will be even more so thanks to the recommendations that will appear this year in the third report on the rule of law, because we will have the opportunity to state clearly, perhaps even more clearly than in the first two editions, the reforms that are expected and therefore to discuss them with the member states. but of course, if dialogue is not enough, we must go further. the report is not enough and we must use the other instruments. i'll end by saying that we use them regularly. today, as part of the european semester, we have adopted country-specific recommendations which also concern the independence of the judiciary or elements of, for example, the fight against corruption. we have translated these country-specific recommendations into recovery and resilience plans. and you know that three plans still need to be approved: the dutch plan, because it was introduced very late, but also the hungarian and polish plans, which have not yet been approved by the commission or, even more so, by the council, and in which we repeat the same demands on the reforms that must be carried out in these countries concerning the rule of law. you are aware of the ongoing article 7 proceedings in hungary and poland. as regards conditionality, as i said earlier, we have started a formal notification procedure with regard to hungary. and, as you know, there are real and regular infringements before the court of justice, with convictions that go as far as financial penalties, which are expressed in deductions from payments to a member state. i am thinking in particular of poland, which was sentenced to a fine of eur 1 million per day for failure to apply the provisional measures decided by the court of justice in the context of the mechanisms for implementing judicial discipline in poland. we will continue to use all these instruments if the report is not enough, but i am very pleased that we have the opportunity to continue together these discussions on the permanent cycle that the tabling of the rule of law report now represents and especially the follow-up to be ensured in the european institutions, but also within the member states, with all the stakeholders and in particular all the actors of civil society.

for the ecr group. madam president, the initiative was initially launched as a legitimate attempt to tackle the problem of economic activity leaking out of the european union. and this happens for two reasons or two forms. on the one hand, it is the escape of taxation of economic activity to tax havens and, on the other hand, the escape of economic activity due to a worse business environment. the first is what we're trying to address now with the introduction of a global minimum tax. but we should be careful not to accept it as the only one, otherwise economic activity will continue to escape from the eu, for example to the united states. the latter should be addressed by structural reforms, otherwise economic activity from the union will continue to elude us, including investment, of course, not in tax havens but in countries with more efficient systems. in any case, with such a huge step, which will of course have many positive effects, the commission should have both positive and negative effects well analysed, and in my view we have reservations today.

mr. chairman, many of you here are saying that europe must immediately cut off its supply of russian gas because by buying russian gas, we are financing the russian war. i'm asking you, what kind of nonsense is this? what nonsense, after all, by buying gas we are ensuring first of all our european, in my case slovak, industry, which would not last a day without gas. it's just a fact. now we're supposed to stop buying chinese products because we're funding a communist regime, or arab oil because we're funding arabs? slovakia is 100 percent, 100 percent dependent on russian gas and all the smart people who say we have to disconnect immediately, i ask why ukraine is not an example. why ukraine, also 100% dependent, doesn't connect first, why ukraine still pays for russian gas. are we supposed to accuse them of funding the russian invasion? no, we're not doing that. i'm all for, when there's a cheaper, more affordable energy supplier, we buy oil and gas from whoever we want. but unless there is a supplier, which there isn't, i urge you to be prudent and keep a cool head.
mr president, many of the measures proposed, commissioner, can only be welcomed, because they really do allow for energy savings, mainly through greater energy efficiency, but also through greater use of renewable sources. i do have a few notes, though. first, we have to be really honest with our citizens, if we want to reduce our energy dependence on russia quickly, it will cost a lot of money, which will be largely paid by citizens, whether it's higher energy bills or even stopping at gas stations. and we also have to even invest in infrastructure to import alternative fossil fuels. that's what we normally wouldn't want to do, we have a green deal. renewable energy sources will not allow us to find alternatives to russian oil and gas immediately. we also have to keep in mind that those countries that could be alternative carriers for our needs are not democracies by any means, but rather regimes, and they are also more interested in long-term contracts than short-term contracts. we're not talking about 300 billion either, because that tells people that it's some new money, but it's just a transfer of money that's already there from existing funds. and for inland countries like slovakia or hungary, which are at the end of all alternative gas routes, it is very important to secure investment in lng terminals, as well as in interconnectors, and it seems to me that the proposed 10 billion will not be enough.
in writing. – with sanctions against russia, little is said about an important issue – the form by which trade barriers against russia are and will be imposed. whether these are import bans (quotas, embargoes) or tariffs. in my opinion, it is more advantageous for eu member states to introduce import duties instead of bans. this is why: with trade bans, the supply of imported goods is reduced and therefore the price of the goods in the importing country rises, while the money from this increased price remains in the exporting country. on the contrary, with tariffs, although the price of the goods in the importing country also rises, the money from the increased price will remain in the importing country. moreover, unlike bans, tariffs can be adjusted gradually and flexibly, precisely according to the evolution of the situation. the revenue from tariffs can also more easily compensate countries that will suffer the most from the restrictions on imports from russia.
