in writing. i commend the european external action service (eeas) for its efforts to address the links between climate change and defence by submitting a roadmap on climate change and defence. with this roadmap, the eeas proposes to mainstream climate change into eu defence actions, including the common security and defence policy (csdp). it is important to set voluntary targets for reducing the carbon emissions of military missions and operations and to adopt a more effective pathway to climate neutrality by 2050, without these targets compromising the security of missions and without compromising the operational capabilities of the armed forces. i call on the vice-president of the commission/high representative of the union for foreign affairs and security policy to ensure that environmental protection and the fight against climate change and its effects are properly integrated into the union's external action, and i consider it important to integrate climate security experts into csdp missions and operations.

rapporteur. – mr president, commissioner várhelyi was very precise and i will be just as brief. i would like to thank all colleagues for their contributions. i thought this was a constructive debate and having listened carefully to all the contributions, let me try to summarise. i guess most of us do agree that for us in the european union, the indo-pacific offers great opportunities, but indeed also poses many challenges. but one thing is clear: our prosperity and economic interests rely on the openness for stability and the security in the region. several colleagues mentioned increasing geopolitical competition. well, yes, this does produce tensions not only in trade and supply chains, but also in broader political and security matters. it is authoritarian regimes in the region that are suppressing democratic principles and human rights and this, in the end, puts regional stability at risk and this also directly affects european security and prosperity. from my point of view, the key message the european parliament is sending to the entire indo-pacific is that we are ready – ready for deepening cooperation and engagement with our partners and like-minded countries, like-minded democracies in the region, in order to respond to emerging dynamics that are affecting regional but also global stability and security. to sum up, it is in our joint interest to maintain a free and open indo-pacific for all, and it is in our interest to promote a rules-based regional order in line with the principles of democracy, the rule of law, human rights and international law. i would like to thank once again for the support i received from most of the political groups yesterday, and i am quite convinced that tomorrow we can adopt this report with a large and broad majority in this plenary. once again, dear colleagues, thank you for a fruitful and constructive debate.

in writing. the eu and the indo-pacific countries are facing increasingly similar security challenges and also non-traditional challenges. in recent years, developments in the region, in particular chinese activities, have led to intense geopolitical tensions and competition that threaten the rules-based international order. there is no overall regional security regime and no confidence-building mechanism to alleviate the related problems and tensions. such dynamics pose a serious threat to the stability and security of the region and the global community, directly affecting the strategic interests of the eu as the main political and economic partner of the countries in the region. the ideological struggle between authoritarianism and democracy in the region may influence the outcome of similar struggles around the world, including in the vicinity of the eu. a stable and peaceful indo-pacific region, based on respect for international law, is essential to the protection of the security and interests of the eu. given also the broader geopolitical complexity of the russian war of aggression against ukraine, the european union needs to focus on capable defence cooperation towards the union and to cooperate, inter alia, with partners in the indo-pacific region.

madam president, it is the scientific consensus, and therefore the great responsibility of scientists, that humanity cannot afford to slow down in stopping global warming. the task of politicians is to achieve an agreed halt to warming with the least possible negative impact on people's living standards. we must therefore choose the most efficient processes, i.e. those that reduce the production of one tonne of co2 the most cheaply. according to an oecd study that evaluated the effectiveness of various emission reduction methods, as well as twenty-eight nobel laureates in economics, market-based solutions are the most effective. so the alternative to a market-based ets is just to remove greenhouse gases by less efficient methods. that means higher taxes, higher negative impacts on people's living standards. although, at first glance, it seems to be the opposite. but in such a fateful matter, we simply cannot afford for populism to triumph.
in writing. we must give clear priority to renewable sources, but at the same time i insist on mandatory exemptions for socially vulnerable and poverty-stricken citizens, who already in slovakia have to pay increasingly higher energy prices. i put this forward as lead rapporteur in the revision of the energy taxation directive voted on recently in committee and i believe that this approach should be applied to other parts of the fit for 55 package, whether it be emission allowances, carbon taxes or the climate fund. i fear that a complete ban on the sale of cars with internal combustion engines from 2035 would harm not only the automotive industry but also people. similarly, the intended charging of fuel for heating and road transport should be limited to administrative and commercial buildings and buses only for a certain transitional period and should not apply to private passenger cars and household heating.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. it is important and right that we make the right choices now to make our mobility climate-neutral. the eu commission is therefore proposing that only new battery-powered vehicles be allowed to be registered by 2035. so far, so good. i'm going to go. but there are still many open questions that cannot be answered seriously or fully. first, how climate neutral is an electric car really? second, how can we generate enough electricity from renewable energy by 2035 to make the use of electric batteries truly climate neutral? or do we not have to import an enormous amount of energy in the form of hydrogen or synthetic, climate-neutral fuels? thirdly, will we still have the necessary charging infrastructure, and especially in rural areas and in eastern europe, by 2035? fourthly, are we not entering into a very dangerous addiction to nickel, cobalt or lithium, a new addiction to raw materials? fifth, will people with low and middle incomes be able to afford an electric car in the future? all these questions have not yet been satisfactorily answered. so i think it's a risky move to put everything on a horse. no, i'm not against electric mobility. i am simply advocating a little more flexibility and, above all, open competition in the field of technology. because openness to technology has always been an asset, and it's an opportunity here, too. i do not want the technology of the internal combustion engine to disappear from europe, including engineers and hundreds of thousands of jobs, while china produces electric fuel and, crucially, china has access to the raw materials we need for electric cars. what would be wrong if in 2035 the consumer had the choice between a much cheaper electric car than today and a hybrid with imported electric fuel? it should be possible to do both. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. it's climate neutral, but it doesn't have any blinds.

mr. chairman, we are facing a truly enormous challenge to save the climate, and therefore ourselves. i am very pleased with the many objectives which this parliament represents, proposes and supports in this fight. but i also watch with concern as we reluctantly accept that ecosystems and nature itself can work for our benefit. we literally intuitively don't give them a chance. we're pushing technical solutions, which is fine, but we're also pushing the natural, the green solutions. we're inadvertently enabling a fundamental property of soils and ecosystems, which is to capture and store carbon, to take it out of the atmosphere and to incorporate inside of it amazing processes in ecosystems. we have the biggest problem with lulucf, and to me, it's a sad signal that we still don't understand the bottom line: that being compatible with the planet and really effectively addressing the climate crisis and the biodiversity crisis is critically dependent on how well we work with nature. cooperation with nature is clearly our greatest weakness. and that has to change. it can and it must be done because it is a matter of life and death.

mr president, commissioner, colleagues, i would like to strongly support our call for the convention and raise three points in this context. first, european treaties codify our past. they, of course, also reflect lessons learned from problems we faced together and overcame or not. but they should also give us effective instruments to cope with the challenges of tomorrow, and that is why treaties need changes. for the years to come, we will need a legal framework that will offer a more unified european jurisdiction, leave no space to populist political forces undermining european foundations, allow us to be more proactive, hopefully also less prescriptive and more principle-based. of course, thinking ahead requires political will, strategic vision, but also a lot of political courage. and my second point is that the good news here is that aiming towards treaty change, we will not be starting from scratch. the one-year-long debate in the framework of the conference on the future of europe and citizens’ engagement offer a vision of a europe caring about democracy and the rule of law, competitive internationally, climate-friendly, socially sensitive, providing security and able to build global alliances. the moment is, indeed, as many of you said, unique. as for this treaty change, we have brave and pragmatic citizens with us. and the last point: our resolution will send a strong political signal about the need of changing european treaties in all areas that will make the union fit for the future. and the conference has proven that a convention is the best path towards the new treaties the union needs.
mr president, the concerns of the citizens go far beyond the revision of the treaties and there are certainly many things we can and must do now, exploiting the potential still available in the lisbon treaty. but it is also a fact that several of the citizens' proposals which became conclusions of the conference on the future of europe imply a timely revision of the treaties. taking these conclusions seriously therefore means allowing these proposals to be discussed at least in the proper place, which is a convention. it is therefore sensible to adopt this resolution so that a convention can discuss the limitation of the rule of unanimity, the strengthening of european powers, the right of legislative initiative of the european parliament and the mechanisms for defending the rule of law. other issues, such as the institutional architecture to reconcile deeper integration with enlargement scenarios, would also merit discussion. but the first step is to call a convention, and that is what we must do.

in writing. i think the union would benefit from more transparency, value for money analysis based on objective data and best practice in the member states, and less protectionism. if we want to give the commission more powers, it must first show better results. the transfer of powers from the member states to the commission is therefore conditional on the transfer being supported by a correct analysis of objective data. example: before the eu starts to put pressure on member states not yet in the monetary union to join quickly, it would be worthwhile to assess why, according to several studies, countries that have joined the euro area have lower gdps than if they had not joined. without this step, rapid centralisation could stimulate centrifugal forces in the union.
for the ecr group. madam president, croatia will become the 20th member of the eurozone next year, and this is an opportunity not only to wish croatia success, but also to say a few words about the eurozone, which was conceived as a club of countries with low debt, sound public finances, and a stable currency. but with the financial crisis, this paradigm has changed a lot. the ecb's purchases of government bonds are turning the euro area into a transfer union. fiscal rules are regularly violated, and they were actually de facto abolished with the arrival of the pandemic. public debts are rising, inflation is at record levels. perhaps these are the reasons why sound academic research suggests that many new and older eurozone members would have a better economic performance today if they had kept their own currencies. and i say this as a supporter of slovakia, my country, joining the eurozone. i wish croatia and the euro area that its membership is a success story for both sides.
in writing. last year, i expressed serious doubts that the evaluation of the recovery fund would be based on an assessment of effectiveness (technical assistance instrument written explanation of vote, 2021). this deficiency has obviously also contributed to the enormous bureaucracy that i have been informed about by officials working on the implementation of recovery plans at national level. i therefore call on the commission to move swiftly from the control of compliance with administrative requirements to the assessment of value for money for the citizen in the management of the national recovery plans.
madam president, it has been six years since the european commission launched infringement proceedings against slovakia for long-term discrimination against roma children in the education system. despite warnings, but also despite the latest call of the council of europe, the slovak ministry of education has done nothing to prepare roma children for the labour market. also because of the approach of the slovak ministry of education, tens of thousands of children in slovakia are lost. they end up with no education, no job prospects, no future, or even no dreams. the ministry of education neglects the crucial period of pre-primary education, which cannot be completed, and continues to allow the unlawful placement of roma children in special schools. we all hoped that the new slovak minister of education, gröhling, would take systemic measures. unfortunately, in two years, he has produced nothing but the usual response that roma inclusion is important. dear friends, this school year thousands of roma children will leave the education system in slovakia. unfortunately, without a future. colleagues, colleagues, how long are we going to watch what is happening in the field of education of roma children in slovakia?

the negotiations have thus begun and the first item on the agenda is the report 'gas storage' presented by mr cristian-silviu buşoi, on behalf of the committee on industry, research and energy (com (2022) 0135 - c9-0126/2022 - 2022/0090 (cod)).
mr president, mr timmermans, ladies and gentlemen, the platform for voluntary joint gas orders from the member states, the institutionalisation of 80% fullness on 1 november 2022 and solidarity with the member states which do not have sufficient storage space today give us the opportunity to talk about an important european step ahead of next winter. however, prices are not falling significantly. the commission has already been given a mandate to study the change in the electricity pricing model and the possibility of disconnecting it from natural gas. as i'm sure you understand, dear executive vice president, we cannot continue to price electricity based on the world's most expensive fossil fuel. at the same time, the european council must act immediately and impose a ceiling on the wholesale price of natural gas, as proposed by the greek prime minister kyriakos mitsotakis months ago. the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of the environment is a step in the right direction, and it is a step in the right direction. let's not give any more time to putin, the speculators and the populists.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, vice-president, thank you to the negotiators who have made it possible to complete this process which was correctly initiated by the european commission in the form of that solidarity which the european union is once again able to demonstrate in difficult times. we saw it with covid and we're seeing it on this very dramatic occasion. in this debate, colleagues have rightly expressed concern in some cases about the continued supply of russian gas to the european union. it is legitimate to be perplexed, but reality must prevail. we have a task today to ensure that the stocks are filled, but at the same time to gradually reduce our dependence on russia. what do you mean, "what"? with this regulatory action, but above all with a proper pricing policy. it is true that we must get out of the overly financial market, but we must not get out of the market, because it is important that the encounter between supply and demand should regulate prices. the problem is that supply and demand has to be about the product and not the financial product. so the reason why we are proposing a price ceiling at this strategic stage is precisely this: the european union is not only a market economy, but a policy which strongly defends the democratic principles on which the european union is founded, and which we hope ukraine will soon join.
executive vice-president of the commission. – mr president, let me just briefly react to some of the things that were said. obviously, the only way we’re going to create long-term energy sovereignty in europe is through renewables. and we also need to do more to save more energy. the cheapest energy is the energy you don’t use. so obviously that remains our main goal. but at the same time, if putin can succeed to disrupt our societies with the weapon he has, which is gas, then we will never get to the situation that we can take our society into a sustainable and renewable future. so that’s why we need this legislation right now. it’s not because we have an eternal attachment to fossil fuels and natural gas, it’s because we know that if we don’t fix this problem collectively as europeans, we give putin a weapon to disrupt our societies, and disrupted societies are societies that cannot embrace the need for change also in the right direction. so that’s why this is so essential. that’s why i’m so grateful to the european parliament for having moved so swiftly and so clearly in a legislation that we desperately need right now. i also want to point out that when poland was cut off, germany immediately stepped in. when bulgaria was cut off, greece immediately stepped in. so we do see solidarity, material solidarity, happening between our member states when there is a need for that. finally, let me just also say that i am a bit surprised that those who for a long, long time felt very close to putin – let me put it in very careful words – in this house are now invoking democracy or even liberty as an argument against the eu acting collectively. the aim of putin was, and is, to destroy and disrupt the european union. those who follow the agenda by being anti-europeans or europhobes should understand they are playing into his hands and not into the hands of our citizens, who want us to collectively solve problems they can’t solve individually.
rapporteur. – mr president, once again, i would like to thank president jerzy buzek and the whole negotiating team and also the coordinators for their extraordinary mobilisation and dedicated work to conclude fast and efficiently this very important legislation in the context of the illegal invasion of ukraine by russia. i would like to thank also all colleagues contributing to the debate today. the debate reaffirmed the strong commitment of all colleagues from the european parliament to protect european citizens and our industries from any major disruption and pressure from russia. we showed our will and readiness to act together in solidarity to strengthen the eu’s resilience and preparedness against external threats. with minimum 80% capacity this year and minimum 90% from next year, with solidarity agreements between member states – and vice-president timmermans expressed some of the excellent examples in the near past, with the need for gas storage operators to pass the certification, and with the mechanism of joint gas purchases, member states have now clear legislation to respect and the tools to act to ensure the protection of citizens and households. i would like to stress that continuous support and guidance from the commission’s side is still needed along the process and commissioner kadri simson, during many discussions with the itre committee, and today executive vice-president timmermans entrusted us with this. we showed our will and readiness to act together and with the gas and hydrogen package and the rest of the fit for 55 package, and with the implementation of the repowereu plan, investing in renewables and energy efficiency and hydrogen will achieve: firstly, security of supply and energy independence; will avoid, secondly, in the future the volatility of prices and the high prices of energy; and thirdly, and the most important for future generations, will reach our climate goals and ambitions.

member of the commission. mr president, in recent months we have sometimes compared the legislative process to a marathon. in fact, i think it would have been better to compare it to a real sprint because the speed of adoption, which was welcomed by all, is all the more evident when one sees the difficulties faced elsewhere - in writing. – the dma is about the digital transformation being fair. it’s not about punishing the big guy. it’s about allowing the little guy to compete and broadening consumers choice. enforcement by the european commission will be key. dialogue and coordination mechanisms between public authorities and private stakeholders will determine whether this law is a success. the dma covers a fast-moving sector. that is why the european commission will need to have permanent expertise to monitor trends and respond quickly. there may be clear dos and don’ts listed in the dma, but the constant emergence of new technologies and business models will inevitably lead to new questions regarding interpretation. thus we have future-proofed the regulation to account for still inexistent digital technologies and markets.
the next item is the debate on the report by mr siegfried mureşan, on behalf of the committee on economic and monetary affairs, on the proposal for a council decision on the adoption by croatia of the euro on 1 january 2023 (com (2022) 0282 - - 2022/0179 (nle)) (a9-0187/2022). the first speaker will be mr siegfried mureşan, rapporteur.
rapporteur. – mr president, commissioner, dear colleagues. on 1 january 2023, croatia will become the 20th member of the euro area. this is good news for the people of croatia. it is good news for the croatian economy and for croatian businesses. it is good news for the european union as a whole. this is why here from the european parliament, we need to congratulate the people of croatia and the croatian authorities for all the achievements of recent years and for fulfilling all the criteria required for joining the euro area. on 1 june, the european central bank and the european commission both concluded that croatia fulfils all legally—required conditions to join the euro area. its debt complies with the requirements, its interest rate, its exchange rate, its inflation, and croatian legislation is in line with the treaty, including with the statute of the european central bank. this is why we, as the european parliament, say very clearly that once a country fulfils all legal requirements, it should be accepted to join without any further delay. this is why i have put forward, during the month of june, the draft report here to the econ committee in parliament in which i propose that we give a green light to croatia joining the euro area on 1 january 2023. i would like to thank my shadow rapporteurs and i would like to thank our colleagues from the committee on economic and monetary affairs for voting with an overwhelming majority – 43 votes in favour, just one vote against, and two abstentions – and giving a green light on the side of parliament for this important achievement. this shows that de facto there is no opposition here in the european parliament towards croatia joining the euro area. during my visit to croatia, i could see on the ground that croatia fulfils the conditions in a very sustainable manner. throughout the crisis, throughout the economic and social crisis generated by the coronavirus that debt, the interest rate, inflation – all the economic indicators – respected the rules. i could also see that the politicians in that country, the main political parties, are supporting this. i could also see the popular support in the country in view of croatia finally using the euro as their currency. this means that the people of croatia can easier travel to other countries of the euro area. it will be easier for investments from other countries of the euro area to invest in croatia, to help develop the croatian economy to generate jobs. it will be easier to trade. it will be easier for european tourists to go and enjoy the beautiful landscapes in croatia. i am also of the strong belief that croatian authorities know that 1 january will be the beginning, not the end, of the further economic development of croatia. reforms need to continue. the economy needs to become even stronger and more competitive and, of course, the eu funds from the traditional budget of the european union and from the recovery and resilience facility can play an important role. during my discussions with croatian government representatives, the national bank and colleagues in parliament, i had the feeling that the authorities are aware of the importance that the eu funds grant, that they are committed to further managing people’s money and the budget with care, to further strengthening and reforming the economy. this is why, to conclude, once again thank you very much to you, to our co-rapporteurs. a big word of congratulations to the people of croatia. we are looking forward to welcoming croatia as new member of the euro area. we are of the strong belief that this will be good not only for croatia but for the european union as a whole. it shows the trust that exists in our common currency.
member of the commission. – mr president, i am very pleased to be here to discuss the adoption prospects for croatia. this is an historical step for this country – a country that 30 years ago was at war. and i think we see the progress that was made with the eu candidates who are entering the eu and now the prospects of joining the euro. the parliament is playing its full part in the enlargement for the ecr group. mr president, croatia is entering the eurozone at a very tense time of record inflation and great uncertainty. something similar happened in my home country of slovakia, which introduced the single currency in 2009 during the financial crisis. experience has shown that the introduction of the euro does not in principle cause price increases and can be more advantageous for a small country in difficult times because of greater monetary stability. but let us not forget that the guarantee of the stability of the single currency is not the number of members but its fiscal rules. accession to the euro area must therefore not be the end but the beginning of the path to fiscal responsibility. let it be a reminder to us 19 euro-zone members that the fiscal consolidation and reforms we expect from croatia are a necessity for us too. only fiscal responsibility will ensure that the euro is a benefit for all and not a burden for those responsible.

i thank the honourable member, the honourable member, the honourable member, the commissioner, and i would like to begin by congratulating the rapporteurs, and in particular my colleague mrs margarida, on this positive report. countless times it has been shown that in times of crisis like this, strength lies in community and integration. that is why further integration of croatia into the european union is positive for both sides. i believe that croatia's entry into the eurozone will strengthen both the croatian and the european economy. but we must not forget that the euro is not an end in itself, but a means to a better life for our citizens. quality control of the euro changeover process is crucial to save citizens from additional costs. we all need to work together to ensure that this positive news is reflected in the daily lives of our people.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, it is clear that croatia will adopt the euro. the introduction of the euro, however, did not bring much good to portugal, greece, or italy. we in croatia could delay the introduction of this currency, as poland, sweden or hungary did, countries which are doing very well with their currencies. so smart countries are working with other european countries, but they're also deciding when and how closely they're going to integrate. unfortunately, the rulers of croatia have never wanted to discuss it, nor have we ever used the advantage of having our own currency because for federalists of all colors the only political goal is the greatest possible integration of croatia into some european super-state, no matter which part of that integration is really good for us. the croatian government did not want to hold a referendum on when to introduce the euro, but i hope that we will fight for the future economic interests of the croatian people to be the first and main criterion of all economic decisions.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your work. it is a beautiful moment that croatia is to become the 20th country of the eurozone this year, and our colleagues have already pointed out: croatia has worked hard to get here. because monetary union - and you always emphasize this, commissioner - is based on rules, and therefore those who, like croatia, respect the rules are ready for the euro. this is extremely positive, and we should therefore support the government's course accordingly and ensure that the common monetary policy of the euro area continues to follow the convergence criteria, because without fiscal discipline a monetary union cannot function. that is why, after a lot of hard work, croatia has finally proved itself for this accession. it has already been pointed out that such financial conditions do not exist everywhere in the european union. so the key is not just to get croatia into the euro in the coming months, but also to ensure that we get inflation in the euro area as a whole and the fiscal policies of the member states on a sound track.
mr. president, please! allow me to begin by congratulating the people and the people of neighbouring croatia, its government, on the successful implementation of all the reforms that have led to croatia meeting all the conditions for joining the euro area, and on the other hand, the people have also expressed their willingness to join the euro area framework. i come from slovenia, where after joining the european union in 2004, we decided to join the euro area in 2007. and if you ask today's economists, as well as the general public, what is the most important thing about joining the european union, the adoption of the euro is one of the biggest values that unites us today and also gives us a competitive area for our work. i am sure that croatia as a tourist country will benefit greatly from this entry into the euro area, but on the other hand, tourists coming to croatia will also be a little less downhearted when we lose money on currency exchanges, so congratulations croatia and much success on the challenging journey that still awaits you in the future.
member of the commission. – mr president, i think that this debate is confirming that we are discussing an historical step for croatia. i repeat that the convergence criteria were positively assessed by the commission and the ecb. now we will have, after your decision and the council decision, a common commitment on the changeover, which will be very important, especially in this environment of high inflation, and then croatia will continue on its path of reforms and investment, also supported by next generation eu. so there is a very good perspective for this country, but also progress for us, for the union. we have now almost 350 million european citizens sharing the same currency – 20 member states. so i think that with your support, with your decision, the european project will be stronger.
rapporteur. – mr president, dear colleagues, thank you very much for this debate and for the overwhelming support in view of croatia joining the euro area on 1 january 2023. croatia will be stronger as a member of the euro area and the european union will also be stronger by having croatia as a member of the euro area. the people of croatia have shown their determination during the last years in strengthening their economy, in keeping croatia on course for further integration with the european union. now it is our duty to show our commitment to croatia and to vote by a large majority tomorrow in favour of accepting croatia into the euro area on 1 january. i am confident, following the interventions of the commission and of colleagues of various political groups, that croatia enjoys large support here in the european parliament, which it deserves. the government of croatia, under the leadership of prime minister plenković, the minister of finance, the authorities, the national bank especially, have prepared everything for the introduction of the euro area. we are confident that following the quick decision of the parliament tomorrow and the confirmation by the council on 12 july, the final technical preparations and the information of the population will take place, because the people of croatia deserve to know about the benefits of the introduction of the euro area. it will be easier for the people of croatia to travel to euro area countries. it will be easier for croatian businesses to export and it will be easier for companies from the euro area to invest into croatia. taking into account the fact that the people of croatia already have savings in europe, taking into account the fact that the major investment countries into croatia are euro area countries, we believe that croatia joining the euro area will lead to more benefits. we have seen, throughout the crisis, croatia meeting all of the criteria, and now croatia still is one of the countries of the european union with the lowest rate of inflation. this is why, once again, colleagues, thank you very much. congratulations to the people of croatia and we are looking forward to giving our positive vote tomorrow here in the european parliament.

the first item on the agenda is the debate on the report by mr milan brglez, on behalf of the committee on employment and social affairs, and mr sirpa pietikäinen, on behalf of the committee on women's rights and gender equality, on the draft report on a common european policy on care (2021/2253 (ini)) (a9-0189/2022).
vice-president of the commission. – mr president, first of all, i want to thank you very much for your contributions. i was listening carefully and i’m really very grateful for the support of the european parliament in putting care high on the european agenda. i was listening carefully and what i heard is that all of you agreed that there is demographic change, that there is a change in family structure, and that we need the infrastructure, budget, public infrastructure. so this is what all of you said. a number of speakers referred to care not being an eu competence. this is what you said. there is no idea and there is no reason to interfere in the sovereignty of member states. but what we are trying to do, we do not question the sovereignty of member states. we are not planning to organise care at the european union level with a care strategy. we are only setting a policy framework to better structure mutual exchanges and to better structure the eu level, so that member states can learn from each other and the commission supports them with eu funding and research. nobody said that you don’t want funding, at the same time you don’t want to interfere into sovereignty, so nobody is interfering into sovereignty. but at the same time we are ready to invest in this. a few speakers also asked for care to be left for the families. yes, all of us know that care has traditionally been a family matter, but all of us also know that family structures are changing. families have fewer children. this is what some of you said. family members live further apart. women want a normal job – a normal and formal job, a normal career. and it’s not possible because of labour shortages. therefore, we can no longer rely on family care and we need to adapt our public services. this is what we are doing. so the european care strategy will call for better working conditions and wages, supported by strong dialogue and education and training to make care jobs more attractive. and as all of you know, we do not start from scratch. many european union initiatives you mentioned also already address the situation of care workers. and this strategy will call for better implementation and enforcement of this acquis to the care sector, and it will aim to trigger actions identifying specific implementation gaps. at the same time, the european care strategy will also aim to highlight the specific challenges of particularly vulnerable care workers, such as migrant workers, domestic workers and live-in carers. we will follow up on your report in finalising the care package and thank the european parliament for the work already done on putting the spotlight on the different aspects of care. we count on your continued active cooperation in ensuring that the strategy brings concrete benefits to the lives of all in the european union, making sure we leave no one and nowhere behind. and once again, i want to particularly thank our rapporteurs, milan brglez and sirpa pietikäinen.
rapporteur. – mr president, thank you for the debate and thank you to the commissioner for the preparation about an eu care strategy. we just would like to encourage you to raise the ambition level, with this parliament’s support – which you can hear very strongly – and as i said, to take the informal carers’ situation into account. commissioner šuica, you quite rightly said that at the conference on the future of europe, citizens’ first priority was the care and social sector and the eu’s improvements there. and it is true that access to care is a human right. it is a constitutional right and it is the eu’s duty to take care of that. and so, no, it does not touch with the subsidiarity. we demand and we ensure that there is universal access to care when it is needed. then it is up to the member states how they organise it – public or private, informal/formal, community-based, personal budgeting – there are millions of ways and this is clearly said in the report. we do not touch with the subsidiarity. it is in no way threatened and there is no pressure on it. but if the quality and human rights is a pressure to the subsidiarity of member states, then i really do wonder. then, yes, i agree that it would be wonderful to also encourage the better use of digitalisation, research and innovation and the benefit of this knowledge at european level. this is the right place for the eu to support the member states and to create programmes and platforms, as it is called in this report, to support member states’ development. last but not least, if member states, by themselves, could solve the care issues, they would have done so already. and if they could take care of the cross-border needs of 8 million new people to be demanded in the sector, it would have happened already.
the debate is over. the vote will take place on tuesday, 5 july 2022. the competent authority shall inform the applicant of the reasons for the refusal.
the next item is the debate on the report by mrs maria walsh, on behalf of the committee on employment and social affairs, on the proposal for a directive on mental health in the digital world of work (2021/2098 (ini)) (a9-0184-2022).
mr. chairman, the pandemic, the long-term uncertainty, and the russian invasion of ukraine have further exacerbated what the polls have been showing for a long time. mental health is a neglected but deteriorating field. and that's true for adults, but even more so for children. these conclusions also stem from my meetings with experts, which confirmed to me that we face daily a shortage of specialised departments, a shortage of specialists and therefore very long waiting times. sometimes sad, sometimes life-threatening. so we should be mobilizing all our forces to manage this pandemic of mental health issues. and to do that, we need, and can use, a financial aid package from the recovery plan. let us see these unprecedented resources as an opportunity that we must use well. to do this, we must assess the situation and needs, set targets and, last but not least, be able to respond flexibly to developments by means of and through changes to the recovery plan. so that we can use these funds where they're needed most, including protecting the mental health of children and adults.
mr president, for too long mental health has been considered a taboo subject, even though millions of people are facing such situations. the isolation created by the pandemic, the shift to a digital dimension and the multiple crises we're going through have accentuated many of these trends. the absence of real prevention measures and the lack of digital literacy have led to new problems that we need, and we must, find new answers to. digital addiction, online bullying, fake news, born-out, abnormal teleworking and digital violence are just some of these new aspects. worse still, more and more young people and children are facing such difficulties, some even taking their own lives. among young people in romania, suicide is the second leading cause of mortality, and about 10% of them are experiencing depression as a result of the pandemic. in this context, i proposed a pilot project to develop the network of school counsellors and psychologists in schools and to develop programmes to provide integrated assistance to young people and families. we need solutions and we need to find a balance at european level to deal with this situation.
member of the commission. – mr president, thank you all for this very inspiring debate. when more than 80 million europeans are suffering from mental disorders, mental problems, it’s really a european problem we have to take care of. and i think the debate shows that there’s a strong willingness to take care of this also at a european level. and i think we have an obligation, especially at the level of companies, at the level of employers, to protect employees’ health – physical and mental health. we have to work on stress, and we know that a lot of people are suffering from stress. there is an agreement, which was concluded in 2004 by social partners on stress. and i think we have one big issue. we can adopt a lot of new directives. the problem is prevention and implementation – to convince everybody, to convince mainly also employers, that stress at the working place is not good for the company. it’s not good for competitiveness. you cannot have a healthy company with sick people. and i think this is the work we now have to pursue collectively.

mr president, thank you mr vice-president, mr commissioner, dear colleagues, the cost of living crisis is indeed affecting everyone and especially low income households. but i would like to focus on one group in particular, and that is young people. because for too many young people in too many member states, even obtaining their own housing has now become an almost impossible problem, an impossible challenge. in 2020, the share of young people living with their parents was over 67% in the eu, and that was before the war. that was before the pandemic and before this crisis. as rents and property and property prices grow, housing as such basically becomes inaccessible to millions of young europeans, regardless of their social status. and now, of course, we must think about how to tackle inflation and energy prices, but i would really like to see a long-term solution for affordable housing to ensure that nobody is left behind and that young people have the opportunity that older generations, including my generation, have taken for granted.
mr president, if we were faced with the situation six months ago when the commission proposed to extend the taxonomy to include gas and nuclear, i would support the objection to this step. but new energy pricing and security challenges from the war in ukraine force us to look at the whole thing from a slightly different perspective. failure to include nuclear and gas could undermine our energy security, create pressure for further price increases and deepen poverty. i know that they are not green energy sources, but we need them in the transition. moreover, if we are to replace russian gas, we also need to invest in infrastructure capable of transporting lng from distant terminals to central europe. as for nuclear, take germany, which recently gave up nuclear power and now has to go back to coal, which probably won't speed up the reduction in emissions. the commission notes that the tax exemption for the electricity sector is not a tax exemption for the electricity sector. this will only increase the financial pressure on countries such as slovakia, which will have to invest even more in the necessary technological changes with their own taxpayers' money. in conclusion, i will summarize by saying that we have two choices. one is ideologically pure and the other is a compromise, but a rational and pragmatic one. i don't think that the second approach in any way reduces our decarbonisation targets.
mr president, the role of the green taxonomy was to make a very clear, unambiguous and precise list of green, renewable and sustainable technologies. there's a lot of interest in investing in these technologies, and there's a lot of need to invest in these technologies, because they define our path to saving the climate. the inclusion of gas and nuclear in the list weakens the list, contaminates it and misleads the list user. that's why i'm voting against. it's not a vote against the plan or the core. we're not in any way banning these resources, but it's a vote against green-lighting these technologies. every single euro that we spend on these technologies as green in self-deception will ultimately be wasted in supporting the truly green technologies that are at the forefront. that's why we're voting for clear rules. i vote for facts, i vote for green solutions, and i don't vote for greenwashing, i vote against taxons.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, it is with great regret that i must say and note that this report is weak. this report is weak because it covers up the facts on the ground, on the spot. he does not disclose the circumstances as they are, and they are the following: the croatian community in bosnia and herzegovina is not guaranteed its rights of representation. croatian interests in bosnia and herzegovina are not guaranteed and they are threatened, they are violated. the balance between the three ethnic communities has been upset. and this report, instead of showing things as they are, is, as usual, full of nice but empty stories, clichés, wishes and other alabasterisms that aim to present a sugary picture that is not true. in order to have stability in bosnia and herzegovina, the croatian community must be represented on an equal footing, protected on an equal footing. everything else is in the service of belgrade and the kremlin.
member of the commission. – mr president, many thanks for this debate. calls were made concerning the candidate status for bosnia and herzegovina. let me underline that the commission will recommend bosnia herzegovina for candidate status. the commission will make such a recommendation to the eu council once a substantial set of reforms will have been implemented in line with the 14 key priorities that are set out in the commission’s opinion, following which, as we know, the council decides unanimously on granting candidate status. concerning the rights of constituent peoples of bosnia and herzegovina, i would like to underline that eu law explicitly forbids ethnic discrimination. collective rights can be made compatible with respect for individual rights, but all power-sharing arrangements that result in discrimination need to be revised as european standards have to be respected. bosnia and herzegovina needs to remove from its constitution all ethnic and residence-based discrimination in the election of the presidency and the house of peoples, as highlighted in the jurisprudence of the court of strasbourg. the 2019 commission opinion provides a detailed screening on what needs to be done. solutions should be in line with the eu’s values and standards and should not lead to further divisions. the upcoming 2022 enlargement package will be the occasion to take stock of our relations with bosnia and herzegovina. we expect to see concrete reforms on the 14 key priorities set out in the commission’s opinion. only by delivering on a substantial set of reforms will bosnia and herzegovina demonstrate that it can advance on its eu path. let me conclude by noting that next week we will commemorate the 27th anniversary of the genocide in srebrenica. with our work in support of the european future for bosnia and herzegovina we are also paying tribute to all the persons who perished in the war and in that genocide and to all survivors. the next item is the debate on the report by mr vladimír bilčík, on behalf of the committee on foreign affairs, on the 2021 report on serbia (2021/2249(ini)) (a9-0178/2022).
so, colleagues, i don’t want to repeat what you already know about the procedure for the debate, but just to remind you that for the blue cards we normally leave it for the first group representatives, so no asking for blue cards then, and then later on we can start with the blue cards.
rapporteur. – mr president, mr commissioner, dear colleagues, it is my pleasure to present to you the 2021 report on serbia. let me begin by thanking all of those who have contributed to the report, especially my shadows. it was my ambition to set out a fair, realistic and accurate report on the current political developments in serbia with a particular focus on serbia’s eu path. the draft of this report was prepared as russia’s aggression against ukraine unfolded. and, as for most issues linked to world affairs, february 2022 was a defining moment that will change europe’s history and certainly europe’s future. we are committed to serbia’s european union accession. this is very clear and it is stated in the first paragraph of the report. the european parliament’s political priority has not changed over the years. it remains solid. this house remains a staunch supporter of eu enlargement. as the european parliament reiterates its support for serbia’s eu membership, it also recalls that a credible enlargement perspective requires political will, sustainable efforts and irreversible reforms in all areas, particularly those linked to the rule of law, as well as unwavering commitment to european standards and values. how do we measure the accession pace? the report reiterates that the progress on the rule of law and fundamental rights chapters, full alignment with eu foreign security and defence policy, including sanctions against russia, as well as the normalisation of serbia’s relations with kosovo, will determine the pace of accession. this is no news to our partners in serbia. the ep’s position has been clear on this point in many years and in other ep reports during previous years. and while repetition might sometimes seem a bit dull, it remains important to be consistent. certain open issues remain as open as they were at the opening of the accession negotiations. this regards improving electoral conditions, fight against corruption and organised crime, improving media environment, and repeated calls for cross—party consensus on eu—related reforms for ensuring the country’s progress on its eu path. and we do not have to look far in history. we can just look at this year’s events when we do regret that even after three months since the elections in april, we are still weeks away from forming a parliamentary majority in serbia. the official results were announced today and this is good news, i welcome it. and this happens, however, at a crucial time in europe’s history, marked by major geopolitical shifts, socio—economic problems, and as war in ukraine rages on, serbia is without a new government. so, i do believe valuable time is being lost. a time to make strategic choices is value even more. eu accession is serbia’s strategic priority and it is serbia’s strategic decision. so we need to see it treated strategically by political leadership in serbia. it is therefore important to start moving towards the eu, not sideways. this report is a complex political document that tackles the most pressing issues linked to serbia’s reforms and eu accession path. it is meant to be read as such. it is not a single—issue report, and credit is given where credit is due. for instance, in the successful conduct of the 2022 referendum on constitutional amendments. also, the april elections were a step forward compared to the 2020 elections, and rightly resulted in a more pluralistic parliament. we welcome the constructive role of the opposition and, as parliamentarians, are glad that an electoral boycott was averted. this resulted in a more pluralistic parliament, which is ultimately the most important thing in a modern parliamentary democracy. and i would like to take this opportunity to thank my former colleagues, tanja fajon and knut fleckenstein, for their work in the inter—party dialogue and pay tribute to the late eduard kukan, who worked on issues related to serbia’s eu future until the very last days of his life. i am sure he would like to see serbia moving forward on its european path, and as this report underlines, there is a clear map and a clear plan how this can be achieved. but the ball is very much in the hands of the new leadership, in the hands of the new government. and this relates to all the key issues which i have outlined: rule of law reforms, dialogue with pristina, as well as full alignment with the eu’s international positions. i hope we can make progress on this, and as the standing rapporteur of this house, i stand ready to help to work together with our partners in belgrade. and, of course, i look forward to listening to the commission, and also to colleagues, and i look forward to a fruitful debate on this report.
madam president, serbia is a beautiful country with great people, with a lot of cultural heritage. serbia is a large country compared to the others in the western balkans and an economically strong country. it could serve a lot for the region and for all europe. but i have to remind all of us about our decision in the plenary with a large majority on 1 march, about our deepest concern about serbian behaviour when it comes to war on european soil. this is why i highly appreciate the work of the rapporteur, vladimír bilčík, because it must be extremely hard to conduct a report on such a country with such leadership. we all have to learn our lessons from history. even we austrians had to learn our lessons from history, and we still have to. and it is also on serbia to learn the lessons from history, to align with europe and european values, to align with the sanctions which are the unbloody defence against the bloody war, and take the first in history, the status of our civilisation, for the sake of the serbian people themselves.
i would like to thank mr bilčík for his report. however, i would like to address a few problematic points which in any case do not show the sincere and genuine commitment of serbia to its european path. first of all, it's the suppression of media freedoms in serbia, which is why their citizens, i really believe, don't have a real insight into what's happening in their country. on the other hand, we don't have clear evidence and clear commitments, primarily foreign policy, which is very important, where serbia really wants to go. when we talk about the rule of law and democracy, i'm afraid that these are still unfamiliar terms for many serbian politicians. serbia must make it clear that it wants a genuine european path. this also means that it must respond to the question of the missing in the greater serbian aggression against the republic of croatia, which is an obligation, i remind you, built into the accession negotiations. walking on a wire and sitting on two chairs is not a sincere commitment to the european way. one eye on russia and the other on the european union and taking from one what is good for them and from the other what is good for them, and that is primarily the money of european citizens, is not an honest and good way.
madam president, yes, i agree, this serbia has no place in the european union. this is the same serbia that is provoking in kosovo. this is the same serbia that is provoking bosnia and herzegovina. this is the same serbia that supported russian propaganda during russia's war against ukraine. this is the same serbia that maintains the largest logistical intelligence spy center of russia in the balkans, in all of europe, it's located in the city of niš, i guess you all know that. this is serbia, which is crushing national minorities. here in this room, very often many of you defend various minority rights, where justified, where not, but today they are not, they are silent because we are talking about real problems. yes, this serbia oppresses the croats in its country, oppresses the bulgarians, violates their rights, crushes them, looks to disperse them, assimilate them, expel them and continues to behave in a way that is unacceptable and unacceptable. this is serbia today. i'm surprised there are people here who are literate. how do you think that if you continue to give in to those, the rulers in belgrade, who are proxies of the kremlin, they will what, become europeans all of a sudden? no, they won't. this serbia, which is a conduit for the kremlin and putin, has no place in the european union, understand this.
member of the commission. – madam president, honourable members, thank you very much for this discussion. i believe that these unprecedented times call for even stronger engagement with our western balkan partners, including serbia, in order to support the strategic decision to join the european union, which is also serbia’s strategic decision. i also believe that the russian aggression against ukraine is a wake—up call on the need to accelerate the accession process for the western balkans. we have no time to lose. the western balkans remain a priority for the european union. at the eu western balkans leaders meeting on 23 june, the eu leaders reiterated their full and unequivocal commitment to the eu membership perspective of the western balkans. they called for the acceleration of this process – however, based on credible reforms by partners, fair and rigorous conditionality and the principles of own merits. the european union will therefore continue its strong support – political, technical and financial – to all six partners in the western balkans to help them advance the key political, institutional, social and economic reforms which are necessary to achieve progress on their path towards eu membership. speaking about reforms, let me underline that the rule of law is a cornerstone of european values and it is a fundamental condition in the accession process. in 2020, the commission adopted the revised enlargement methodology, which puts fundamental reforms – especially the rule of law – even further at the heart of the process and of our engagement. this is also the case with serbia. the assessment of the progress of the countries on the eu reform path is subject to a long and thorough process. the final decision is made by the college as a collegial body, and this will also be the case for this year’s report. concerning alignment, let me repeat that serbia has demonstrated its commitment to eu values by aligning with several eu declarations and by supporting the relevant un general assembly resolutions on ukraine. nevertheless, as a candidate country, serbia is expected to progressively align its policies towards third countries with the policies and positions adopted by the european union, including restrictive measures. we are in contact with serbian authorities to help them to increase the alignment rate with the eu common foreign and security policy, including restrictive measures against russia. we count on serbia, as a sincere european partner to stand with us and our common values, security and prosperity. on the commission side, we will continue to do all we can to speed up serbia’s pace of negotiations and support the eu perspective of the western balkans as a whole.
rapporteur. – madam president, commissioner, colleagues, i want to thank all of you who took the floor today. i would like to thank those who see serbia as a strategically important partner in the western balkans, and who understand that serbia’s successful eu path is more important now than ever. i have noted all of your concerns and i will communicate them to the serbian political leadership. let me just say three things. one: accession talks are not single issue talks. serbia did open cluster 4 last year and can make progress on accession talks if it makes progress on domestic reforms. i do believe – and this is my second point – that serbia belongs in the european union. but much has to be done by the leadership and the new government and new parliament in belgrade. third point, when i look at the past 100 years of serbian history, i certainly do not see russia as playing a dominant role. i hope that all the relevant pro-european and serbian political forces can discover and rediscover this part of their history and base their decisions on this when looking to the future. i am convinced that serbia has a european future and serbia’s place is among the eu member states. but at the same time, after the april 2022 elections and russia’s war aggression, there is a real sense of urgency that all parties in serbia move forward decisively on their european path. this report reflects factually on the complex political reality in serbia and encourages all pro-european forces in that country to focus fully on progress in rule of law reforms, the belgrade—pristina dialogue and alignment with eu international positions. we note – and i think all of us heard it today – that much can be done for serbia’s move towards the european union. as the european parliament’s rapporteur, i stand ready to engage with the new leadership in belgrade in order to work on the european future of serbia.

in writing. – the council meeting of 23–24 june has fast-tracked eu candidate status for ukraine and moldova and closer status for georgia. the speed used for granting this status is tied to the ongoing russian invasion. however, the long-term political implications must be kept in mind. on the basis of prevailing rules, the process for these countries to attain membership will take years, perhaps decades. the underlying political rationale could fade or alter with time. consider turkey, where claiming that only this country has shown bad faith in its accession process is not tenable. disillusionment or worse bitterness could eventually colour the relationship between the eu and accession aspirants. as has happened with the eu’s candidacy outreach to the balkan states. on a more fundamental basis, the eu needs to face out the strategic question that old-timer henry kissinger has clearly defined: post putin and post the eu’s current leaders, europe will still have to define what relationship it wants with russia. does it want russia as a full partner in a european system or would it like russia as an asian power to be held at arm’s length? mitterrand and de gaulle also raised the question and their concerns were side-lined. how sad and short-sighted.

in place of the rapporteur. madam president, i shall speak very briefly before the vote opens because, unfortunately, the issues relating to the committee on fisheries seem to be of little importance to certain groups which, session after session, deny the possibility of them being discussed and having visibility in the house. the report we are now voting on, drawn up by mr nuno melo, is as important and substantial as the others. it is urgent to ensure that the fight against the negative impact of the war in ukraine is included in the objectives of the european maritime, fisheries and aquaculture fund. the fisheries and aquaculture sectors are crucial in the european union and therefore the union must intervene to minimise this impact and ensure the survival of businesses and jobs. the financial package we are going to put to the vote will allow for the use of additional crisis measures to support the union's fisheries and aquaculture sectors in the context of the russian invasion of ukraine, will make available union funding to compensate for additional costs, loss of income and storage of products, as well as the temporary cessation of fishing activities, and will extend aid to companies that temporarily cannot continue fishing due to economic constraints. the final adoption of this legislative proposal will provide much needed support to fishermen and the aquaculture sector in record time and with retroactive effect, as businesses will be compensated for the impact of the crisis from february this year. the report was unanimously approved in the committee on fisheries and we hope that it will also receive broad support in parliament today.

mr president, the reform of the directive should have been about reforming the use of biomass for energy purposes, so that we do not destroy our forests in a futile attempt to save the climate by burning wood. it was necessary to limit the subsidisation of wood burning and to prohibit the inclusion of wood burning in renewable energy sources. that was the position of the envi committee. instead, we have a compromise proposal that defines primary biomass that is not eligible for subsidies, but at the same time applies a huge exemption to primary biomass that is logged, which represents more than half of the amount of timber that we can subsidise. the remaining primary subsidy is also included in the consumption share on a basis of 2007 to 2022, which is unknown and allows for an increase in the extraction of wood. for me, unfortunately, that's a red line. the red line that the reform has taken, a well thought-out reform for renewable energy, which applies a number of good steps for wind, solar, geothermal, but opens the way for a european biomassass, and i do not intend to support it.
in writing. in order to combat climate change, the proposers of the directive propose to increase the share of renewable energy sources (res) in eu energy consumption to 45%. environmental protection, including the fight against climate change, must also be done effectively. the relevant directive does not yet provide for this. the commission notes that the slovak authorities have not provided any evidence that the measures are in line with the market economy operator test. because there's a risk that we're spending a lot of money on these goals and we're not stopping climate change. that's why i voted against it.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your kind words. the massive increase in energy prices and the shortage of electricity, gas and oil are putting a huge strain on households and businesses. the current energy crisis is threatening livelihoods across europe. so there is indeed an urgent need for action. as an economist, i want to tell you very clearly: it is absolutely clear that this crisis cannot be solved by austerity and austerity alone. we are experiencing a supply shock which cannot be met by reducing demand alone, at least not without completely destroying the foundations of our prosperity. we must increase the supply of energy, and to do this we must use all available sources of energy in the short term. it is symptomatic that the ministers of the eu countries, when they met last week, did not work out any solutions to this problem and instead focused on the exploitation of corporate profits. it is a sign of catastrophic incompetence that the german federal government continues to insist on the nuclear phase-out of the largest eu country, thereby putting our common prosperity in existential danger.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. the situation has been described by many colleagues: we are faced with horrendous prices which are a huge burden on private households and are driving businesses into ruin. what's the answer? i am quite frank with you, commissioner, we do not need deficiency management, we need to remedy the deficiency. i have been reading green papers and commission action programmes for over 20 years, and i have been wondering why, for example, the european demand for energy should be concentrated. when, if not now, is the time to pool the power of demand and look at what is available on the world markets as the european union? it is not enough to go to qatar, as the german energy minister did, and then turn around and come back without a supply contract, because it is not ideologically acceptable to sign a 20-year supply contract. we really need solutions now that help on the spot. they will not permanently eliminate the problems by intervening in the market, but will rather create more chaos. that is why i warn against working here with short-term measures - excess profits taxes, market design interventions and the like. we don't want to cause the blackout ourselves, but we should prevent it together.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, i think it is clear to all of us today that: the most serious threat to the citizens of europe is the unstoppable rise in gas prices, with repercussions that are destroying entire industrial realities and killing the real economy and the life of even average families. we must therefore get to the root of the problem. the subsidies are not enough, the state aid is not enough, we cannot go on indefinitely with these means. we ask the commission to prepare, after last week's council meeting, concrete and courageous proposals because it is already too late, it is already too late to introduce a ceiling on gas prices. a cap on gas prices means that in europe no buyer buys above a certain price and in this way it is the buyers who will determine the market rules, not the seller, who will be able to simply burn his gas, as he has already done, but also burn his revenue. it has to be done, it takes courage, it takes clarity. finally, on the subject of the ttf platform: i do not really understand why the commission is so cautious, so cautious in taking action: there's a problem of transparency, there's a problem of unfair market, there's a problem of at least manipulation in that market. you need to get your hands on it. don't you want to stop that business? change the reference market for the gas price, let's get off that rig! that platform is made up more by finance than energy operators. you have to intervene!
madam president, commissioner, i am afraid that all the crises and problems we have experienced in recent years may be nothing compared to what the energy crisis may cause. because it's not going to affect just select sectors or just vulnerable groups, it's going to affect everyone and it's going to affect everything. if we do nothing, in some countries, household energy bills can reach completely absurdly drastic numbers, a few percent of gdp. it's really not manageable. and if we do nothing, the economy will collapse, schools will close, our industries will freeze. and on top of all that, oil and gas companies are making historic profits. so the next few days, the next few weeks, are going to be critical. just as during the pandemic, people rightly expect a european solution, price stability, compensation for households, aid for companies, but also a tax on excess profits. and it's really simple. either we agree quickly, or the europeans will judge, quite rightly, that when the going gets tough, the european union cannot be trusted.
madam president, commissioner, we are facing a very difficult winter. many businesses are going bankrupt, production is being curtailed, and there are layoffs. and the fall of many households into poverty. in addition to measures such as the price stability of various energy sources or, where appropriate, the limitation of the income of some energy companies producing electricity at low production costs, which must be temporary, we need other measures. and that is, above all, a rapid and adequate response to our people who rightly ask us for help. and they don't care if we do it by taxing the excess profits of the energy companies or by subsidizing the energy prices of these companies. simply put, we have to transfer these billions from these rich companies to the poorer people and especially to households. it's now clear that putin's not the only one who's in college, that's just an excuse. but also our bad decisions from the past and also the speculation of various hedge funds, especially on the amsterdam stock exchange, where as we know, with borrowed money they can push gas prices up. if we are talking about the solidarity of europe's water supplies, it cannot be just a recommendation to heat less or to save less light. we cannot tell the poor to save, because that is as absurd as telling the hungry to eat less. we simply need strong social measures. and those governments that have structural funds that are not used up must be given the opportunity to use them for social compensation for households and businesses.
madam president, mrs von der leyen, you said a moment ago that the european union must root out corruption from its own ranks. so i ask you, in the spirit of that, why don't you release your communications with the ceo of pfizer for the purchase of the 30 billion euro vaccine? why are you hiding this from the public and why are you currently hiding it from the auditors? because this is the real picture of the european union. is this what you call transparency? on this basis, do you want european citizens to trust the european union? you said that europe has already given ukraine more than 90 billion euros. and i ask myself again, perhaps uncomfortably, where does this money come from and at what cost? where's this money going? who's this money going to? aren't they being stolen by some oligarchs? does anyone really control where these billions of europeans go? are these now toxic questions that we can't ask because it might make someone uncomfortable? you know, i refuse to accept that the european union or europe is only meant to serve some multinational corporations or the interests of the great powers. we'll point that out again and again because that's our job.
in writing. some of the measures proposed by commission president ursula von der leyen to address the energy price crisis are moving in the right direction, coming too late. in a europe whose people are paying a high price for our support for ukraine, we need strong and swift action. people don't care what measures we take, but we need to do it now. the billions of euros that some big companies have made speculating on energy prices must be put into the budgets of households and businesses that are in trouble. the measures proposed do not seem to me to be sufficient in this respect, and even the energy savings recommended for people already living on the poverty line seem absurd, as if we were recommending that the hungry eat even less.
