the meeting will continue tomorrow. the agenda is published on the european parliament's website.
mr president, i am pleased that the european parliament is acknowledging the existence of institutional racism. these are practices that the institutions in the european union are supposed to protect, educate or develop. excessive violence during police interventions against roma or other minorities, segregation and unlawful placement of roma children in special schools for the mentally disabled, discrimination in employment or health care, lack of access to basic needs such as drinking water or sanitation. these are just some of the cases that reflect the reality of today's world, which is often based on discrimination on the basis of ethnic or racial background. we must realise that all these practices not only undermine the dignity of the people they are aimed at, but also undermine all our efforts towards prosperity and well-being, as well as cohesion and security. we live in challenging times. but if we are to rise to these challenges and move forward, racism in our societies simply must not be tolerated under any circumstances, in the interests of all of us.
madam president, while the borrowing strategy has been conducted without major difficulties, new european debt also brings many hazards into the future which the report overlooks: higher inflation, increased indebtedness of member states and resistance of member states to fiscal consolidation and structural reforms. i think it is a mistake that the commission does not set fiscal consolidation as the main condition for drawing the recovery funds. borrowing strategy is the easier side of the coin. the harder one is the not-borrowing strategy, the growth strategy that has been missing so far, fiscal consolidation and structural reforms in member states. without them nextgenerationeu risks to be a missed opportunity that we cannot afford.
the next item is the report by mr michal šimečka, on behalf of the committee on civil liberties, justice and home affairs, on the proposal for a directive of the european parliament and of the council on the resilience of critical entities (com2020) 0829 - c9-0421/2020 - 2020/0365 (cod)) (a9-0289/2021).
rapporteur. – mr president, colleagues, madam commissioner, it is quite an honour for me to stand here and present to you the result of almost two years of legislative work, which was grounded in extensive debates and consultations with stakeholders, the member states and the european institutions. the initial promise was to deliver on a europe that protects, which in practice also means strengthening the resilience of the critical systems underpinning our way of life in our internal market. and today, with the cer directive, the eu is in a position to deliver on that promise. before we vote, let me just give a brief overview of what has transpired in europe while we have been working on this file, which also provides the strongest arguments for why the european union needs a new piece of legislation to better protect its critical entities. right before the commission rolled out its draft, the covid pandemic broke out, infecting and killing millions of europeans and disrupting our healthcare, our economy and our societies. later, while we were negotiating this law, or this directive, russia launched its brutal attack, its brutal aggression against ukraine. and since then, the european union has been facing an unprecedented increase in sabotage operations on its critical infrastructure – from the energy sector to transport services – and experiencing an increasing number of hybrid attacks on its public institutions. and in the meantime, the climate crisis has been burning and flooding our continent from greece to portugal, sweden to italy. and all of these crises, all of these events have also served as a sort of compass for the co-legislators as they were finetuning this directive. now more than ever, we must prove to our citizens that the european union protects their lives, protects their jobs, their companies, the essential services that affect the daily lives of our citizens and, eventually, the entire european union. therefore, our ambition with this directive is to strengthen the ability of critical entities to cope with these risks to their operations, while improving the functioning of the internal market in these essential services. there are 11 crucial sectors covered, and this directive addresses the various measures and requirements so that its implementation will adequately respond to both natural catastrophes and man-made attacks. i trust that our joint compass throughout this process has brought solid rules and established the cooperation, coordination and information flows among the member states, the entities and the european commission that will strengthen preparedness for these incidents, the response to these incidents and, eventually, their evaluations. because resilience is not just about preventing a risk – a disaster can always occur; the crucial thing is how we cope with it and how we, as the european union, bounce back from it. moreover, this is reflected in the legislation. there are entities which have a pan-european dimension because they operate simultaneously in several member states, and a risk to their operations is therefore a risk for the single market as a whole. and this directive puts a special focus on these entities and on the cross-border cooperation of these entities, which are of particular european significance. finally, i would like to say that i’m delighted – and this is thanks to you, madam commissioner, and thanks to my colleagues, our shadow rapporteurs, our advisers in the european parliament and our counterparts in the council – that our mutual work has resulted in what i believe is the best possible legislation. it is a big step for the european union, and i really hope that this parliament will broadly support it by adopting our interinstitutional agreement later today.
mr president, commissioner, the resilience of europe's critical infrastructure needs to be strengthened in terms of both cybersecurity and the physical environment. this legislation, which will now be adopted, together with the is2 directive, is an important step towards strengthening common european security. we have seen in recent months the devastation russia is wreaking in ukraine by destroying critical infrastructure and driving people into a corner. in the european union we have seen the nord stream 2 explosions and the sabotage of the german railway network. they show the importance of strengthening joint preparedness and joint response and also of intensifying cooperation with nato, as the commission proposed in october. it is a good step that in addition to energy and transport infrastructure, the legislation now to be adopted includes a total of eleven very critical sectors, including digital infrastructure, banking, drinking water, health, food. we know that these are all very critical activities for society and for the lives of citizens.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, we can rarely agree on something that is really so important and so necessary. the directive to adapt attacks to our changing critical infrastructure system, just as society is changing, is really a moment of collective congratulations. we must also congratulate the french presidency on this last-minute agreement. we can't afford these attacks. for example, less than a month ago in spain, through the punto neutro judicial, a system managed by the general council of the judiciary that connects the networks of different public administrations, the attackers accessed millions of sensitive data: identity cards, income statements, bank accounts, addresses, working lives, business relationships of each and every citizen who appeared in these connected databases, politicians, businessmen, publicly relevant figures. they took everything: the complete databases of the services of the tax agency, social security, the public employment service, the general directorate of police, foreign affairs or the general directorate of traffic. these and other types of critical infrastructure are the main vulnerabilities in the hybrid wars of the present - the commissioner said today - and of the future. in ukraine, the electricity, the communications are attacked. data hijacking and energy attack can lead to disaster for our societies. we are reacting. we're happy for that. but we have to be quick and we have to make this kind of vulnerability visible to our societies. today is our lucky day.
mr president, i welcome this directive i was calling for since the beginning of my mandate. as putin continues to wage his brutal and aggressive war against the people of ukraine, he’s also continuing in his attempts to weaken and divide our democracies. disinformation, cyber-attacks, espionage, strategic corruption of businesses and political cycles. that’s why it is crucial that we seek attacks on critical infrastructure in this broader context and develop necessary tools. for this reason, i welcome the protection of critical infrastructures directive and the extension of the scope to 11 sectors, in order to better reflect the scale of the threat. however, we can never adequately protect ourselves if we allow malign regimes to invest in our critical infrastructure. the case of nord stream 2 must be a lesson not only for our dealings with russia, but also when it comes to chinese communist party and others. such regimes must never again be allowed to use our critical infrastructure to undermine our resilience.
mr president, commissioner johansson, this parliament welcomes this revision of the directive on the reinforcement of critical infrastructure and critical entities, which is part of the reinforcement of the security of europeans against hybrid attacks, dramatically brought to light by putin's war against ukraine, and which extends the security of europeans beyond energy and transport to other sectors affected, such as banking, such as digital infrastructures and, of course, such as food supply and, above all, health and public health data. but i would like to make one further comment: now that this european parliament is turning 70, which is the age at which one normally retires or accedes to retirement, this parliament has every reason to feel younger than ever and it is therefore important to modernise the language as well. when we talk about resilience, about critical entities, are we telling europeans clearly what this is about? my answer is no. we can be much more graphic and effective if we tell them that we are strengthening their security against sabotage and against attacks on people's privacy and privacy, and that we are better protecting their fundamental rights in the world in which we live.
member of the commission. – thank you very much for this important debate. it clearly showed that we all agree on the importance of better protecting critical entities and the importance of this legislation that you will vote on today. i will again reiterate my special thanks to the rapporteur, mr šimečka, and the shadows, but also, of course, to the french presidency and the council, which managed to reach a swift agreement on this important legislation. we have to take responsibility together as a union, and the best action we can take right now is to make sure that we are ready for what is to come. the directive will soon enter into force. it’s now up to the member states to use it to full effect ahead of time, and to carry out the stress tests to bolster our preparedness and response to protect our infrastructure and our people.
rapporteur. – mr president, madam commissioner, dear colleagues, thank you so much for the discussion and thank you for the support. it is quite rare that there is such a broad consensus in this parliament from the left to the right. and not just a consensus and an agreement on this text that is going to be voted on later on, but also consensus and an agreement on the kind of threats that we face and on the kind of actions that we need to take. i note that a lot of you, colleagues, have approved of the expansion of the sectors that will be covered in this directive to 11. i am also happy that there’s strong agreement on that. i should also mention, highlight, that the philosophy of this directive is somewhat different from the last one, and i appreciate that. i think this is also where there is a consensus: that we’re moving from just protecting particular pieces of critical infrastructure – assets and buildings and whatnot – to a philosophy where we’re focusing on the resilience of those essential services. and i think this reflects this new and dangerous world that we’re entering where the risks can't all be eliminated. there will be threats, there will be incidents. and we need to be clear with our citizens that not with this directive, not with anything we can do, can we eliminate all the risks falling from natural catastrophes to war to hybrid sabotage attacks. what we can do, though, is to minimise those risks and to make sure that when incidents happen, we are prepared and our entities, be they public or private, are able to bounce back and carry on with the services that are essential for our societies and for our single market. and this is what this directive does. this is what it’s for. and with its approval and with its implementation, the european union, its citizens, its single market will be all the more secure. so let me again say thanks so much to the commission, to the french presidency, to all my colleagues from the political groups. and i would just reiterate the call of the commissioner for member states to implement this as swiftly as possible.
in writing. all eu countries should take effective measures to ensure a more representative representation of women not only on the supervisory boards of different companies but also at all tables where important measures are taken. however, the situation varies considerably from one country to another, with some countries, such as slovakia, where women represent only 25% of such decision-making bodies, still having a long way to go. if we take into account that up to 40% of managers should be women by 2026, taking into account, of course, the necessary qualifications and experience, the path for countries like slovakia will not only be long, but also difficult. in practice, this also means that if equally qualified candidates apply for the post, then the representative of the less represented sex must be given priority. those companies that fail to meet the criteria prescribed in the directive, such as the transparency of the selection process, will have to face sanctions.
in writing. women face many artificial barriers in life that men do not have to face, and it is right to remove these barriers. but setting quotas for private firms is not about breaking down barriers, it's about setting outcomes. it's like if the nhl, instead of having the last team in the draft with the best chance of getting the first pick, had a rule that the last team in the draft automatically wins every other game. the real solution should be to remove barriers, for example by making it easier to combine work with childcare.
mr president, europe is running out of steam. the west is mired in recession, while the brics countries are gaining strength and preparing for gradual expansion. i'll be honest, i don't agree with china being labeled as a threat to europe. yes, as a competitor, yes, but as a threat? please, how many wars in the world did china start? or how many countries have china invaded? just for comparison, in the 246 years of its existence, the united states of america has been at war with someone for 231 years, that's 94% of the time. and they're labeled peacemakers, and china is labeled by some as a threat. i don't like this policy where you label everyone outside the western bubble as non-democratic states, as totalitarian or even as threats or enemies. if europe is to prosper, it must work with the east.
madam president, after 70 years of the european union's imperfect foreign policy, we have come to the conclusion we have come to, namely that there is a war in europe, there is a crisis, a food crisis, an energy crisis. and a crisis that may lead europe to a greater conflict than it is. i agree with what mr. borrell said. such a course of action should be avoided in relation to china, the situation should not be exacerbated, china should be seen as a partner, a trading partner and negotiated with them on the principle of 'equal to equal'. and as far as some of the strong or eager meps here who think that europe is about the centre of the universe and that we can dictate to somebody how to act in their own country, what to do, what to work, i would probably compare the position of the european union and china to a situation where an elephant, an elephant and an ant, an elephant and an ant are walking on a bridge and the ant says: "but we're going to die!" we simply cannot afford to act in a way that would get rid of another global trading partner that has never really attacked anyone, as we... (chairman interrupts speaker)... that's all i have to say.
madam president, we spend a lot of time discussing the rule of law standards for new members, but once they are in it seems that they can ignore those same standards, because they become members of the club and it’s the club of member states. it’s mostly a system of cover-ups. president metsola went to barcelona over the weekend. i hope she found good weather there in my beloved hometown, a city where i have not been able to set foot in the last five years. because if i did, i would suffer an arbitrary arrest. so what’s the credibility about rule of law? we only look at it for the candidates or for some of the members, depending on their power. in any case, i do hope that ms metsola does not help spain in their political persecution. i’m happy that she denied that she was planning to strike four members of this parliament of our rights as representatives of more than two million people that elected us to this parliament.
madam president, commissioner, colleagues, eu enlargement has been one of the most successful policies of the union. it has transformed the continent, established democracies, modernised economies, and united us in diversity. as we face russia’s attack against ukraine, the eu must focus on its foundations. european enlargement must be based on shared goals and principles, respect for the fundamental rights of individuals and the rule of law for all. european values have real political meaning. all of us inside the european union, must once again become serious about enlargement. our commitment to a european future for ukraine and moldova must transform our internal discussion about a wider europe. we must do all it takes to help ukraine win its just war, and even more importantly, to win a lasting and prosperous peace based on european policies and institutions. we must equally deliver on our long-term commitment to the western balkans. we expect our partners in the region to stand with us, not to bypass us. we expect true reforms, not lip service paid to europe. but this is also our task. as russia continues to pursue its hybrid warfare across the western balkans, we, as europeans, must be politically clear. we will do what it takes to make europe prevail in this region, to make democracy trump authoritarianism, to bring a european way of life to all people of the western balkans. it’s time to believe that enlargement can become one of our greatest achievements again, dear colleagues.
mr. chairman, we hear a great deal of determination to agree on asylum legislation. i expect this agreement to reflect reality and put safety first. security, responsibility and solidarity is an approach that is clearly demonstrated in aid to ukraine. we are helping ukraine to defend itself against the aggressor, and therefore we put security and peace first and we are also helping ukrainian refugees in solidarity. we don't want people running away from their homes and dying at sea. nobody wants that. but we see the obvious reasons. dictators like bashar al-assad and others in the middle east and africa don't mind at all that their citizens have to flee violence and hunger. putin and lukashenko are using this as a tool in their fight against eu unity. and that's why we need to address this situation now, now, and with the tools at our disposal, and we can't wait. agreements with third countries, the end of smuggling mafias and, above all, the protection of the external borders against illegal migration must be our priority, a task for the member states and the european commission. it is not possible to criticise the czech republic for introducing increased controls at its borders and for detaining and returning refugees from third countries. if the external borders are not protected 100%, if refugees are not detained in registration centres immediately after entering the eu, we will have to guard this external border. frontex is a tool to help. and i want to finish by thanking all of you who are doing your part, the thousands of police officers and volunteers who are helping people who are fleeing.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the new right-wing government of giorgia meloni has been in office in italy for a month and instead of dealing with the real problems of the country, it has already attacked the humanitarian ships geo barents, humanity and ocean viking, a shameful action whose only aim is to send a message to the electorate on the skin of human beings. all of this is counterproductive. what is needed now is a solution shared at european level. the mediterranean action plan is an important proposal, but more must be done to coordinate search and rescue operations. the first country of arrival criterion must be abolished and solidarity and responsibility of the member states must be strengthened, not just returns. the italian colleague, mr bartolo, is doing a great deal of work in this respect, also on behalf of our political group. migration is a global phenomenon and must be addressed as such. the countries of the union will only achieve results if they are able to cooperate, with shared solutions, starting with those who are most prepared to take them forward. we must move forward together.
mr president, the european union has faced a number of challenges in recent years, with increased migration being one of the most significant. the current system is not working, but for years member states have been unable to agree on how to reform migration and asylum policies. some countries carry a huge burden, and russian aggression has shown that a refugee rush can affect virtually any eu country in a short time. my country is also hosting hundreds of thousands of ukrainian refugees. what was previously unimaginable for eastern european countries, millions of refugees on their territories, became a reality overnight. i too voted for the temporary measures in favour of italy and greece last term, in the hope that a balanced solution for the whole of the union would be found. but that has not really happened until now. our aim should be to move away from temporary solutions and to establish a predictable system that makes it possible to manage migration. protect the schengen area, of course, but also increase diplomatic efforts and conclude readmission agreements, establish hotspots outside our territory. what we're experiencing today may be nothing compared to the movement of hundreds of millions of climate refugees in the middle of this century. these people are already born and will have to leave their homes because of extreme weather, natural disasters. already, their numbers are comparable to the number of refugees fleeing wars. the migration waves will hit the entire planet. our only chance is to realize this as soon as possible and use the time to make a real preparation. i hope the council understands that. and i also hope that the deal will be done as soon as possible.
i would like to thank the commissioner for his reply. in the debate on asylum, we rarely talk about what it's really about: people who have experienced terrible things, people fleeing war, people fleeing persecution, people dying on the way to safety. and not only is this mostly missing from the debate, but for reasons of populism, these people are then also presented as a threat, are labeled as illegal, even though the human right is clear: every person has the right to apply for asylum. everyone has the right to be judged according to the rule of law. as european legislators, we have a responsibility to create a framework of rules in which human rights are respected and enforced. and this must not be allowed to fail in the name of national petty-bourgeoisism; we must not allow this to fail by inciting violence against the weakest. we can stop the deaths in the mediterranean, we can stop the pushbacks at the external borders, we can get people out of the camps. let us ensure that we, as the european union, fulfil our obligations through common european solutions, solidarity and humanity. we are sending a message to the council: do not boycott this!
mr president, i would like to thank you. it is noted that the commission is working hard to move forward with a european solution for asylum and migration. the fact that we have not progressed in the negotiations since the refugee crisis in 2015 is an understatement for the member states. we need to ensure a dignified and humane reception for people seeking asylum in europe. not everyone who seeks asylum and comes to europe has the right to asylum, but we still have an obligation to organise an orderly reception, to ensure a reception in solidarity between member states, and to process asylum applications in accordance with the geneva convention. we know that women and children on the run are particularly vulnerable when they are in the clutches of smugglers and can also be victims of human trafficking. the same is true of the perilous journeys across the mediterranean. so we need to agree in the eu on a common migration policy to prevent this from happening. it's time now.
mr president, commissioner, commissioner, we have been debating this question of a single european union solution to the problem of migration for seven years. i'm skeptical, and i have reason to be. we have spoken here of united europe, solidarity, humanity, rights, but we have a service blocker which is the council. and i'm giving an example to be well understood. if my country has been held at the gates of the schengen area for 11 years, what unity, what solidarity are we talking about? if we have a regulation voted by parliament, voted by the commission and it is not applied because the blocker is the council. because one or two member states, for petty national interests, invoke various reasons unrelated to the regulation. how can i believe that we will have a pact on migration agreed by all member states? and yes, i do believe that my country, my citizens, have accumulated frustration, resentment, economic loss. yes, the european union is growing in skepticism. and that's why i'm asking the council to make romania part of schengen.
mr president, how much cynicism and, above all, how much hypocrisy there is in this house. when i left, in 2019, i thought that something had improved, and three years later we are still the same or maybe worse. there is no european landing protocol, no legal and safe routes, the criminalisation of ngos continues and, above all, the first port of landing criterion has not been abolished. that's totally immoral. i was lucky enough to be on a rescue boat. and i say lucky, because i've seen the tragedy on the front lines. i have seen how the libyan coastguard deterred the ship open arms when we were wandering for ten days, until the spanish state gave permission to enter the port of barcelona after being denied in the ports of malta and in the ports of italy, with a populist government in italy, and even more populist is the one now. so these speeches that these people on the far right have are immoral and we have to say it like that, commissioner. we cannot allow the commission and the council to fail in this new attempt and to continue to criminalize those who save lives, a job that is worthy and deserves our respect.
mr. president, i have to ask you... the migration crisis is not to be treated, but prevented. hungary's southern border is practically under siege by migration. this year alone, authorities have prevented some 250,000 illegal border crossings. illegal immigrants and human traffickers often violently attack border guards. since 2015, hungary has spent 650 billion forints on the protection of the southern border. the european union provided only 2 percent of this. in the meantime, he supports ngos that facilitate illegal migration by sea or land. in order to prevent migrants from being stranded at the serbian-hungarian border, austria and hungary have agreed further measures with serbia to curb illegal migration to the eu along the western balkans route, as the eu's asylum system appears to have failed.
member of the commission. – mr president, minister bek, honourable members of this house, together with vice-president schinas i have been listening to this long and intense debate. of course, there’s been a lot of emotions, and that is very understandable because we see men, women, children in very dangerous situations, also losing their lives in the mediterranean or in a sealed lorry along the western balkan routes. so it’s understandable that we are emotional, because saving life is always our first responsibility. there’s also been a lot of focus on the problems and challenges that we have. i’m dealing with this on a daily basis. we have huge challenges. we have seen an increase of irregular arrivals along both the western balkan routes and the central med route. we have seen also that in nationalities this increase is not caused by more people that are fleeing prosecutions or in need of international protection. we see an increase of nationalities that are probably not in need of international protection. we have seen an increase along the western balkan routes of pakistanis, cubans, indians, turks. we have seen an increase on the west med route of bangladeshis, tunisians, egyptians. of course this needs to be addressed. that’s why i was in bangladesh, in pakistan last week, to discuss with them the swift return of those not eligible to stay, but also legal pathways and help for the returnees. that’s why we are presenting and launched a new anti-smuggling partnership with all the western balkan partners. that’s why we presented last monday a 20—point action plan to counter the western balkan route. that’s why we will have an extraordinary council on friday to discuss this. so the challenges are huge, and of course irregular arrivals has to be addressed. this is not a proper way to come to the european union. but i would like to use this opportunity to talk about some other issues, actually, that some of you, many of you, have been calling for – a european solution on asylum and migration. we are so strong when we work together. when we are united and tackle challenges together we can do a lot. let me just remind you that we managed the situation where lukashenko tried to sell tickets, instrumentalising migrants into the european union. we managed that together and, together with third countries, we have managed to decrease the irregular arrivals through the western mediterranean route and atlantic route, thanks to good cooperation also with our african partners. we managed last year to give humanitarian admission for 35 000 afghans fleeing the situation in afghanistan. we are managing right now a situation with more than 4 million ukrainian refugees by activating the temporary protection directive. we can do so much more when we work together. i heard somebody call that it’s time to stop debating and start working. the good news is that work is ongoing. the negotiations on the new pact on migration and asylum that we presented two years ago is really taking big steps forward. there is a very good momentum in the council. thanks to the czech presidency, but also thanks to the previous french presidency, we are working intensively together with the member states and are reaching new steps almost while we are speaking. also here in parliament, good progress has been made on all the files and parliament and council has agreed on a roadmap, and i think this is a unique situation. there is a real good opportunity that we can make the decision on the whole pact on migration and asylum during this mandate. but time is running so we have to start the real negotiations and trilogues now to be able to finalise all the files during this mandate, to show everybody that migration is not a threat. migration is something that we need, but we need to manage migration and we need to welcome people on legal ways, but we need to prevent irregular arrivals and the risk of people’s lives.
president-in-office of the council. – mr president, vice-president skinner, commissioner johansson, honourable members, i wish to thank you for this very timely and important debate today. these discussions are not easy, but they contribute to finding appropriate responses to the current challenges. there is no doubt that the situation in the mediterranean and at our external land borders must be addressed in an effective and timely manner, in accordance with european and international law. and we need to avoid at all costs loss of lives. as i have already stressed, both improved cooperation with third countries and progress on the negotiations on the migration and asylum pact remain the only sustainable way to achieve a better and more robust migration and asylum system that will serve all member states well. the presidency and the council remain committed to work intensively with our partners, both to address urgent short term measures, as well as to advance to work on a systemic solution in line with the roadmap on migration and asylum that combines responsibility and solidarity, as a number of you have recalled here today. this requires a positive political environment, which will allow our institutions to move forward together.

the next item on the agenda is the statement by the council and the commission on the global biodiversity framework after 2020 and the un convention on biological diversity cop15 (2022/2959 ((rsp)).
member of the commission. – mr president, minister, honourable members, thank you very much for this rich debate and for your comments and interventions, of which i took good note, and thank you very much for your great support. let me come back just to a couple of points that some of you raised. first of all, of course, resource mobilisation. yes, this will be a key point in the negotiations. the package must cover the means of implementation in the broad sense. we need to scale up financing from all sources, and in particular domestic finances. we also need to remove, redirect or repurpose environmentally harmful subsidies and incentives, and we need to increase positive incentives and align all financial flows with biodiversity objectives. we want clear and operational provisions on mobilising resources from all sources. without this, the framework would be a dead letter. because i have also heard some of you drawing parallels to what have happened in sharm el—sheikh, let me also mention that without any significant increase in official development assistance, it will be very difficult, if not impossible, to strike an ambitious deal. combined efforts by eu member states, as well as from a wider range of donors, will be necessary to achieve full impact. at cop11, the parties agreed on doubling biodiversity—related international financial resource flows to developing countries. the eu has delivered on this commitment, and the commission will double again its financing over the period 2021—2027. france and germany also made significant pledges. we are pushing other donors to come up with similar increases. we will need a very practical and pragmatic conversation if we want to identify solutions that can work for all. the eu and member states certainly want to avoid to agree on a pledge that at the end of the day cannot be delivered. we should avoid a situation, as for climate, where perceived lack of financing becomes a political stumbling block for full implementation. last, ms paulus, let me say that based on assessment by the iucn, we have a clear list of species that are at risk of extinction. we need urgent action by 2030 to save these species. however, the vast majority of species is unknown to us, and yet the ipps global assessment in 2019 estimated that one million species is at risk of extinction. the 2050 goal should be to save all the species, bringing the rate of extinction back to the natural background level. dear colleagues, members of the parliament, let me use very clear words. our biodiversity life on earth is in danger. we are part of that biodiversity. we too are in danger. so we must stop biodiversity loss and restore nature. therefore, our key priority is a successful cop. the negotiations in montreal are our chance to adopt a framework that credibly addresses biodiversity crisis.
president-in-office of the council. – mr president, honourable members, commissioner, i would like to thank all members for their interventions. they confirm the strong and continued interest of this assembly in the protection of biodiversity and the desire for the eu to play a leading role at the un biodiversity conference. the council adopted its conclusions on 24 october. we are now working on fine-tuning our positions on the issues on the agenda of the conference, with the aim to provide the negotiators with a solid, but at the same time flexible mandate. please allow me to conclude by reaffirming the presidency’s commitment to do its utmost to defend the eu’s ambitions in canada to adopt an ambitious, comprehensive and transformative post-2020 global biodiversity framework that includes the long-term 2050 goals, 2030 intermediate outcomes and action-oriented 2030 targets, to effectively and simultaneously address the direct and indirect drivers of biodiversity loss.
the debate is over. the competent authority shall inform the applicant of the reasons for the refusal.
in writing. i believe that the 15th un conference on biodiversity will be a paradigm-shifting conference. world leaders will come together to agree on the global protection of our biodiversity. the ep called for an ambitious, comprehensive and transformative post-2020 global biodiversity framework, including long-term 2050 targets, intermediate outcomes and action-oriented 2030 targets, that effectively and simultaneously addresses both direct and indirect drivers of biodiversity decline. this overall framework needs to be funded. that is why i believe it is absolutely necessary and i support the allocation of 10% of european funds to nature conservation. i support the creation of a coalition of stakeholders, both from the private and public sectors, to achieve the global framework for biodiversity post-2020. in this regard, i underline the usefulness of the 'solution agenda' developed under the paris agreement for developing a positive agenda for all stakeholders with an interest in the united nations framework convention on climate change and call for the inclusion of similar actions in the post-2020 biodiversity framework.

mr president, this is a very strange resolution, which asks for what has long been legally possible. all the instruments we are asking for are already in the hands of the member states, including the killing of problematic wolves, bears and lynx, where this is really the last and only option. it should be made clear that the initiative was taken by the agri committee, but the original intention was to open up the habitats directive, to reduce the protection of wolves, bears and lynx and to allow for their extensive hunting. i have no understanding for such ambition, mainly because of the enormous importance of these species for ecosystems and biodiversity, as well as for the great support of the public. i am therefore pleased that we have a negotiated text which i fully support, and i also urge all of you to stop or close this chapter, to address the really important things, such as finding compatibility with this planet, which will not be possible without coexistence with wild unregulated nature.
madam president, it is certainly good that we are setting targets for digitisation. unfortunately, we don't have a very good selection. they don't measure results for the citizen. for example, the objective of making key public services available online. it is not enough that the service is available online, because the online service can be designed in such a way that citizens lose more time than if they visited the office on foot. for those who do not believe, read about how the online registration service for the covid vaccine worked in slovakia, my member state. instead, targets would be more appropriate, such as how much time has been saved, how much costs have been reduced or how much labour productivity has been increased by digitalisation. such objectives are often missing from the design, and i think that's a shame. i'm sure you will.
madam president, poverty, fear, insecurity, these are also the realities of millions of young people in europe. these are difficult times. the covid-19 pandemic, as well as the war, has brought anxiety, fear, and a sense of isolation. in europe, but also in slovakia, in my country, there is a growing number of young people who have extremist views, they are faced with open aggression, we even recently saw a young man murdered in slovakia out of hatred. young people today need our support and understanding all the more. we need to reassure young ones that their views and opinions are very important to us. young europeans must feel our support. we must not underestimate the future of young europeans, because they will one day be the ones who decide what europe will be.
the commission has already made a number of proposals. stronger, connected, resilient, prosperous: these are the words of the commission's new website on 'rural areas', which was launched on friday. i am pleased that the commission has recognised these challenges. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the rural areas are not left to the detriment of the environment. there can be no class societies here. the rural areas must be supported, they must be able to keep pace with the climate change, social and economic challenges. the most important issues need to be addressed. it is about transport planning, mobility, schooling, education, demography, welfare, broadband, social cohesion and civic engagement. and that is why i thank mrs carvalhais for the very good report - and in particular for the fact that the subject of predators has been raised. this is also an important part of the rural areas being protected and the rural areas being able to be managed. this is part of the acceptance and part of living in rural areas.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i support this report because it is moving in the right direction. in the european union, for every farmer under 40, there are three over 65. so the village is dying. between 2003 and 2016, the number of farms decreased by 32%. but when the village is well connected and infrastructure is in place and when people can live well from farming or other work, then life in the village is very nice, and housing is much cheaper, which is extremely important for families and demographics. we must therefore be more decisive and effective in adopting policies that strengthen rural development and domestic agricultural production, rather than encouraging the import of inferior food of questionable origin and lining the pockets of retailers and large corporations. without a prosperous village, there is no prosperous city. there is no healthy, home-cooked food, no thriving country.
madam president, i would first of all like to congratulate the rapporteur on her work and, ladies and gentlemen, we are all aware of the importance of rural areas, whether we are talking about food security, combating climate change or tourism. however, european policies have failed to develop these areas, so that today only 11% of farms in the european union are run by farmers under 40. investments in broadband internet, water services, road connectivity, healthcare and education are indispensable. commissioner, we must be aware that only when the gap between urban and rural areas is closed will we be able to really talk about reversing demographic trends. the synergy of the funds and the strengthening of the leader programme must be matched by the reduction of red tape in the process of accessing european funds. the war in ukraine has shown us once again the importance of preserving the ability of rural areas to provide quality and affordable food. that is why i want to stress the need for the european commission's policies to be anchored in the new geopolitical realities.
madam president, what is the value of a culture? the rural areas of the european union cover more than 80% of the territory but are home to only one third of its inhabitants. thousands of peoples with diverse traditions and diverse ways of life are at risk of disappearing through depopulation. the european institutions must ensure its survival. the european union's cohesion policy must promote the development of these areas. the new mobility plans must put an end to the isolation of our towns, which is sometimes caused by a lack of infrastructure. we must ensure connectivity that makes teleworking possible and we must work to implement the rural pact. but we can also use the potential of cohesion policy to fix population on the territory. we can offer incentives to people who choose to live in the rural world; promote sustainable tourism that shows their ways of life; value a sustainable way of life that is respectful of our planet. we cannot allow centuries-old traditions of our rural peoples across europe, which are at risk of depopulation, to be lost because of the inaction of the institutions. we have to get involved. we need involvement at all levels. and the european union must lead this engagement, because the value of a culture is incalculable.
madam president, the fear of wolves or bears belongs to fairy tales like the one about little red riding hood or to lands where the sand poured and the water flowed. not until the 21st century. the killing of animals has brought many species in europe to the brink of extinction. bears, wolves, lynx, bison, beavers and many more. our approach to nature was conquering. to spoil, to rob, to kill. this approach has brought us to the brink of mass extinction. will we be among them? the countryside needs progress, quality education, healthcare, accessible transport connections, high-speed internet, but not the subjugation of nature. i believe that in the third millennium, we don't need to kill predators again. we don't have to prove ourselves superior and subjugate other life forms. if we want to survive, we must learn to live together, both among humans and with other life forms, such as wolves and bears. so that our children don't learn about nature from fairy tales.
madam president, i was not pleased with the report on the vision for the union's rural areas by 2040. it does not propose appropriate solutions, and in particular does not have properly identified the causes of rural backwardness. the aim of placing young people in unproductive jobs in rural areas cannot be part of the strategy. why do we want to sacrifice them? the oecd study gives us some clear recommendations. it is not enough to redistribute money to poor regions, structural changes are needed. inefficient grants are holding back innovation and productivity growth. however, productivity differences are the main reason for the backlog. we should give the regions autonomy in local taxation and promote fiscal decentralisation. these solutions are missing from the report, and i think we will not make progress without them.
madam president, i'm afraid we're at it again. the need to reduce wolf protection has been mentioned in rural development plans. i am told that we are to be consistent with parliament's previous rather unfortunate position. at the standing committee for the berne convention on 2 december 2022, all 27 member states unanimously rejected switzerland's request to reduce the protection of the wolf, thus clearly declaring the need for its further strict protection in the european area. so if we're going to be consistent, let's start by being consistent with our own countries' position. demonizing the wolf and asking for non-systemic and obviously dysfunctional steps to hunt it will not help the countryside. nature is not the enemy of the countryside, but its ally in the fight against climate change and the decline of species wealth. so let's start working with her, please. it is also the instrument with the greatest potential for the development of underdeveloped and declining rural areas.

madam president, we are in the midst of a major energy crisis caused mainly, but not only, by the russian aggression in ukraine, and we must all the more accelerate the transformation of our energy sector. in other words, it is about strengthening our strategic autonomy, that is, energy self-sufficiency, and thus mainly reducing our dependence on third countries, whether it be russia or qatar or azerbaijan. the main response is the development of renewable energy, which is becoming the cheapest source of energy, and the massive investment in its use. however, the lengthy authorisation procedures, which are indeed standard in some countries, are an obstacle to their wider use and therefore need to be accelerated and simplified without prejudicing the rights of the local communities with whom dialogue is needed. the commission also found that the union industry was not able to obtain sufficient sales volumes of the product concerned from the sampled union producers. solar panels, wind turbines, but also nuclear fusion, together with biogas and green hydrogen, are the only energy sources we will have to rely on in the future if we do not want to become dependent on third countries and fossil fuels.
madam president, renewable energy sources are indeed key to combating climate change, reducing energy prices and ensuring a continuous supply of energy to the european union and ultimately to liberating ourselves from the dictates of fossil fuels. indeed, we need to remove the administrative barriers that prevent us from making the most of them and developing their full potential. however, it should be borne in mind that the production of renewable energy can and does damage protected areas and therefore conflicts with nature conservation, and therefore its production and development should be a priori focused on areas where such conflicts do not occur. i am particularly critical of this in relation to hydro and biomass energy production, where too much benevolence in pursuing a public interest overriding necessarily leads to the damage of rivers and the disappearance of precious forest areas. we have had enough of these protected forests being cut down and live rivers being cemented to understand that further destruction of the natural environment is simply unacceptable.
madam president, slovakia is certainly an inspiring example for all the countries of the western balkans that want to become members of the european union, how to proceed on this path and what reforms need to be implemented. it's good that you're putting so much energy into this region. he is also close to my country and to me personally, because i am slovak. the russian invasion of ukraine and the changes in the current geopolitical situation have made us realise that the european union's strategic interest is a stable and secure environment in its immediate neighbourhood, regardless of which of these countries will or will not eventually become a member state of the european union. the western balkans have been waiting in the waiting room for a long time for membership in the european union, whether it's serbia or montenegro or other countries. but today, in the current situation, and as a result of the tirana summit, there is a certain change in the perception of the whole process, and the frustration of these countries is changing into sober to cautious optimism. and the european union is also recovering from some of the exhaustion of enlargement and is committed to really taking these countries seriously. it would be good to continue this and we must help each other to do so.
mr president, both of the strategic frameworks we are discussing were adopted at a time when we were not even aware of the challenges we face today. during the covid pandemic, culture was one of the areas that suffered the most. we're not even out of the pandemic yet, and we're already in a war and the challenges that are associated with it, which are rewriting the modern history of europe. europe must also respond to these challenges in the field of culture. social inequality is deepening, and discontent is growing, populism is growing, and of course extremism is growing. at the same time, europe is moving towards new digital technologies. of course, artificial intelligence is being used more and more. all these changes are happening at a very rapid pace, and it is precisely in these difficult and rapidly changing times that culture has the greatest potential to lift the spirits, to bring people together, to strengthen their mutual identity and cohesion. however, funding for culture by the member states is still significantly under-resourced. and while we in the european parliament have managed to increase the budget for the creative europe programme, it is still not enough. the optimal solution could be to use funding for cultural support in synergy with other programmes such as horizon, europe or erasmus+. this would not only be a financial link but also a cross-sectoral link, particularly in the field of education, which i consider to be very crucial, and in the field of our cultural heritage. it is an integral part of the identity of each one of us.
madam president, we are building a social europe in which no one can be left behind. and yet we have 18 million children living in poverty. this is an emergency that we must respond to at both european, national and regional level. every third family with children in slovakia is at risk of poverty as a result of the crisis and inflation. single-parent households with more children are the worst off. poverty is a risk factor that increases the likelihood that a child will encounter malnutrition, poor health care, poor education, crime, domestic violence or other pathological phenomena. according to scientists, poverty reduces a child's chances of fulfilling his potential and living a good life. the child guarantee is an excellent instrument for improving the quality of life of children living in poverty. member states have allocated money from the esf+ to this, slovakia even allocating more than 14%. now the most important thing is to make sure that the resources really get to every single child in need. i consider this to be the most important role in which the european institutions must play a key role and not rely solely on the political will of national governments and politicians, which children tend to overlook because they cannot yet vote.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your report. we are discussing today the guarantee at european level that children can grow up without poverty and with a carefree childhood. having children is one of the happiest things that can happen to you. at the same time, the old saying is true: children expand the attack surface of fate. this is why the current crises - inflation, rising rents, rising interest rates on loans, rising prices in general - have particularly affected families with children. that is why the european child guarantee is particularly important. so i can only wholeheartedly argue that this is the wrong place to save. children should be supported in their talents. next year is the european year of skills and abilities giving children the chance to learn something and then to do it as a profession, to be able to do something and then to do it with pleasure this is also so important according to the old important motto that the best social programme is a workplace. my home country is called children's austria because we have been doing this for a long time, that children come first. in austria there is childcare allowance, the family bonus, and i would also like to see the child guarantee implemented at european level. children's noise is the music of the future.
mr president, dear commissioner, minister, the war in ukraine and its consequences in europe increased even further the need to support children in situations of poverty in europe. energy prices, food prices, all of those are affecting many children in europe. therefore, europe must react. esf+ is based on national pre-allocated financial envelopes. such envelopes can only be increased by modifying the mff. we call on the commission to come with a proposal for a dedicated budget to turn the child guarantee into reality. this supplementary programme with a dedicated budget will complement the esf+ with additional support for tackling child poverty on top of the national pre-allocated financial envelopes. this is not a new policy that would duplicate the existing ones, but a key eu-wide boost that would be essential for providing support and assistance to children in situations of poverty. children are our future and no child should be left behind.
mr president, 21% of european children are at risk of poverty or social exclusion, which is almost 18 million. if you add the energy crisis, the war in ukraine, the food crisis, covid-19, inflation -- never before have so many families been impoverished so fast. my country, spain, is the third country in the european union with the highest risk of child poverty and social exclusion, behind romania and bulgaria. one in three children in spain is at risk of poverty, a rate well above the european average. spain is also the sixth country with the highest inequality of children. this child guarantee is timely, necessary and a priority. it aims to ensure that every child in europe has access to basic services: free education and healthcare, healthy nutrition and decent housing. children must be at the centre of all our policies. it is necessary to break the cycle of poverty transmission and, in this context, to protect, in particular, the most vulnerable children: children with disabilities.
the child poverty in the european union, one of the richest regions in the world, is unacceptable and is still growing. the covid pandemic, the war in ukraine, showed that the systems that were designed to end child poverty are not as strong as we thought they would be. and so we have to regret and take responsibility that even 18 million children are poor. and child support is important not only to get children out of poverty, but to keep them out of poverty. a separate budget is therefore necessary to implement the child guarantee. not all member states are really responsible in their approach to implementing the child guarantee. and even nine member states have not yet drawn up national implementation plans. this is just one example of the importance of european action to overcome child poverty. and it is true that a separate budget is needed, but it is equally true that a common political will is needed.
mr president, the ambitious target of lifting at least 5 million children in the european union out of poverty or social exclusion is a good start for the european child guarantee. but the work on this instrument is not over. until the basic living conditions for all children in the european union are guaranteed, we cannot speak of success. social exclusion is a complex and multifaceted phenomenon. its key factors are lack of resources and poverty, but also unequal access to services due to various forms of disadvantage. the proposal for a european child guarantee is particularly important in the context of the socio-economic consequences of the covid-19 pandemic and also of the economic crisis we are still living under. the solutions that member states are gradually and belatedly presenting need to be integrated into a broader policy framework to address the multidimensional nature of social exclusion. the commission should also be able to assess the impact of the measures on the implementation of the union's policy on the protection of children and adolescents, in particular by examining the impact of the measures on the implementation of the union's policy on children and adolescents. we need more commitment from member state governments and above all the release of adequate amounts of funds to achieve a multi-dimensional systemic solution for our children's future.
member of the commission. mr president, ladies and gentlemen of the european parliament, the idea of the child guarantee was born here. today you have shown a continuing will, since you have shown that this parliament wants to be the defender and spokesman for the millions of children in europe who have no voice. the child guarantee is a good start, an encouragement and a strong invitation to member states. i would also like to see the same debate on child poverty in each member state in the 27 national parliaments. the commission is hereby engaged; she's not resigned, i can guarantee that. it is determined to work with the member states, with the funds at its disposal or, in the end, at the disposal of the member states , and i fully understand that the circumstances have worsened further. the danger of increasing child poverty in europe is more than real since inflation has hit many households and families. i believe that the desire to think about how to deal with this situation and to strengthen the means available to combat child poverty is a completely legitimate desire, which i understand and which the commission can certainly share. i can assure you that the commission will remain in constant dialogue with you to ensure that what has been decided and implemented on the existing funds is actually implemented and, if necessary, strengthened to deal with a situation that has deteriorated.

madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the pandemic crisis first and the consequences of the russian-ukrainian conflict second have generated a double-digit inflationary spiral, the likes of which have not been seen for some time on our continent. it is time for quick and urgent solutions, which provide answers and support for consumers, households and businesses, starting with the fight against the increase in energy prices, on which we hope to see decisions already at the next european council to stop speculation. for months now, the price quotations for gas on the european market, as well as the consequences of putin's criminal action, have been based on purely speculative logic. in view of the circumstances, once again, we need the real solidarity of europe from those who have made extra profits in recent months. finally, in the light of the current economic scenario with rising interest rates, i believe that a concrete signal is needed from the european union and the member states to alleviate the needs of consumers, households and businesses through an extension of the moratoriums on business and mortgages. we do not need adjournments on these matters, but decisions.

member of the commission. – madam president, honourable members, i think that this debate was very useful to share and to give us the sense of a social emergency that we have to address. very important, in my view, first to understand where this social emergency is coming from. i am not referring to, of course, our historical problems of social inequalities, but i am referring to the recent social emergency. and i think that the reason is not on the green transition. the reason is not on sanctions. the main reason is the russian aggression in ukraine and the consequences of this russian aggression on prices, energy, food, inflation. how to face this challenge? president-in-office of the council. – madam president, honourable members, commissioner, thank you again for this debate. it is indeed very timely given the precarious situation of our most vulnerable citizens and businesses; the cohesion of our societies is at stake. we are all well aware that there will be a price to pay for all of us as a result of russia’s actions. we need to make sure the burden is fairly distributed. the issues debated remain at the core of the council agenda, as demonstrated by last week’s discussions on the european semester and the european care strategy, this morning’s agreement on repowereu and many others. as i said here earlier today, the czech presidency is working hard to find a solution regarding gas price caps, and i am cautiously optimistic that we could still achieve results in the days to come. difficult times require determination, solidarity and the right policy response. this means finding the right balance between the economic, employment and social dimensions of the semester. our debate in the council, and this one here today, and our continuous endeavours give me confidence that, despite all the difficulties, we are all working for the same goal – helping our citizens to go through this multidimensional crisis.
in writing. rising inflation, rising energy prices and eroding purchasing power are current challenges that we need to address immediately with concrete measures that have an immediate positive impact on citizens' lives. people don't have to bear the economic burden of this context and we can't leave anyone behind. the situation is all the more serious as the cost increase has a significant impact primarily on vulnerable groups, low-income families or the elderly who were already facing many difficulties. it is important to use the funds available efficiently, but also to identify those new resources that will allow us to implement the necessary solutions. one step is to stop the speculation in the energy market, identified by the experts in the field, and tax those who made huge profits. the implementation of the european minimum wage, for which our political group and the psd have made significant efforts, can ensure decent wages throughout europe and better pay for workers. at the same time, in the run-up to the european year of skills, we must also focus on measures related to qualification and re-qualification, in order to increase the chance of workers in the labour market. there's a lot to do, but solutions can't wait. only together can we successfully overcome these challenges.
president-in-office of the council. – madam president, honourable members, commissioner, this presidency, we have experienced, like many others before us, many difficult discussions in the council. but experience shows that diplomacy and perseverance pay off since, in the overwhelming majority of cases, we manage to find solutions to overcome our differences and break the deadlock. true, this takes countless hours of talks and efforts to understand each other’s positions. translating sometimes into late night or early morning compromises like we have been experiencing these days. but this is how the union works. the number of files which remain blocked over prolonged periods of time is rather insignificant, statistically speaking. our swift reaction to the recent crisis shows our unity and capacity to find agreement in the interest of us all. i would like to recall that unanimity is a voting modality provided by the treaties, albeit currently an exception from the rule, which is qualified majority voting, unanimity is typically applicable to decisions in the common foreign and security policy area. well, treaties require the council to act unanimously; the act can only be adopted when member states vote in favour. but, importantly, the treaty provides that abstentions do not prevent the act from being adopted. in the area of the common foreign and security policy, there is also the so-called constructive abstention, which allows a member state that uses this possibility not to apply the adopted decision while permitting the union to go forward. this is what has been successfully used in the context of the european peace facility. while there is a lot we can do through discussions and persuasion, a member state remains free when expressing its vote in the council, and this is a fundamental element of our european democracy. after all, council members are responsible towards their citizens and their national parliaments for the action, including how they vote in the council. treaty change would, of course, be one of the possible avenues that may also be considered to bring more areas under qualified majority voting. however, as you know, it is a very heavy procedure. this possibility has been discussed in the follow up to the conference on the future of europe. more importantly, in many areas, it is possible to switch from unanimity to qualified majority voting without modifying the treaties. indeed, the treaties contain one general and the six specific so-called passerelle clauses. they allow the european council or the council also acting by unanimity, but through a simple procedure, to switch to a qualified majority in the council. as a follow up to the conference on the future of europe the council has started discussing the possible use of passerelle clauses to switch to qualified majority. the czech presidency has notably initiated exploratory discussions on this issue, focusing on the common foreign and security policy. i look very much forward to hearing your views. i will have to leave the room for a short while as i have a duty to sign several legislative acts together with president metsola. but my colleagues will be here and report to me afterwards. my personal and friendly advice to honourable members would be to, instead of attacking the council as the institution, rather focus the energy on reaching out towards national parliaments, as these are the bodies that national governments are accountable to.
member of the commission. – madam president, minister, honourable members, we all know that the lisbon treaty extended the system of qualified majority voting to a large number of policy areas on which the council used to take decisions by unanimity. for instance, in the area of justice and home affairs. the lisbon treaty also added the new so-called pastoral clause – which the minister was referring to – giving the council or the european council the possibility to move from unanimity to qualified majority voting in certain areas for certain acts. however, unanimity still applies under the current treaties in a number of policy areas, and the pastoral clauses remain unused – also because you need unanimity to deliver the pastoral clause. the commission has long been pushing to move towards more qualified majority voting. this was also called for in this commission’s political guidelines from july 2019. policy fields that would benefit from this change are energy, taxation, or some aspects of the common foreign and security policy such as sanctions and human rights. in the conference on the future of europe, citizens also called for moving from unanimity to qualified majority voting in some policy areas. today, i can only confirm that the commission fully supports the call for moving away from unanimity in some policy areas. this can already be done under the rules of the current treaties. we stand ready to engage with both parliament and the council on this matter, and the commission will always be on the side of those who want to reform the eu to make it work better.
i am speaking on behalf of the ppe group. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. i have been here in this european parliament since 2009 and over the years i have been reminded - dear mr verhofstadt, you have been here even longer than i have - that certain precarious dossiers are subject to repeated national vetoes and that there are repeated long, long, long delays because a member state is in conflict. in the field of foreign policy, this is extremely precarious, considering that we have now had the case of hungary, which, in order to be able to override the conditionality mechanism inadequately for itself in the country and to undermine the standards which the commission rightly demands, is trying to blackmail the council into accepting its aid for ukraine only if hungary has to, so to speak, apply fewer rule of law standards. i must say that i cannot express my indignation enough at the fact that a member state of the european union and viktor orbán are even trying to do this, because, quite simply, aid to ukraine is absolutely necessary and internal budgetary issues and questions of the rule of law must not be linked to the ability to act in foreign policy. i would like to congratulate the czech presidency on having managed to use the conditionality mechanism for the first time in a difficult situation and still find a solution. we're gonna have a lot of questions to answer. we need to have a capacity for action in foreign policy without unanimity.

on behalf of the id group. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, unanimity is a fundamental principle of the european construction. it is the weapon which enables each member state of the union to block a decision which would harm its vital interests. general de gaulle recalled this in the name of france in 1965, when he used the empty chair policy to force europe to give up imposing on it a reform it did not want. its successful resistance led to the luxembourg compromise of january 1966, which is still in force, and which enshrines this national veto power. unanimity is still enshrined in the european treaties. it is required for european council decisions and for the most important council decisions. i therefore condemn the challenge to it by president macron and by today's debate. the majority of this parliament criticises hungary for vetoing aid to ukraine in protest against the freezing of its subsidies. on the contrary, it believes that hungary was right to do so. its firmness has also enabled it to reach an agreement with the union. let us protect unanimity, because it is the only principle that allows member states to retain their sovereignty within the union.
on behalf of the ecr group. – mr president, dear colleagues, many want to abolish the veto to make things easier, not really better. democracy should be fair and honest, not easy – and easy for whom? democracy is difficult. autocracy is easy. it should remain the basic right of member states, as it is the last rampart of defence for the small, weak and vulnerable against the strong, big and bullish. it is rooted in the tradition and history of eu. it is a normal procedure in the eu, and big member states use it on a regular basis. it is an expression of the wisdom of the founding fathers, whose wish was to create a balanced community of equals. since lisbon, the few vetoes left remain the only way for small and medium-sized member states to protect themselves against the diktat hegemony of bigger states and their directorates. the veto serves the vital interests of the abused, the weapon of the weak meant to establish the balance when they face a risk of being bridled and abused. removing the veto would also put in danger the cohesion of the eu. the eu is about consensus, not divisions. to those who demand the cancellation of the veto, do it yourselves first. be the example by giving up the veto for your countries through a solemn declaration not to use it; create an enhanced avant-garde. do not practice hypocrisy. germany declared that it would veto enlargement if the veto were not removed from the treaty. it is kafkaesque and grotesque. to those who demand the cancellation of the veto, do it yourselves first. solemnly declare not to use it. good luck.
mr president the double standard on the veto issue is unacceptable in the european union. it is a very hypocritical attitude that while hungary is untrue and baseless accused of breaking up and vetoing european unity when it courageously stands up for its position and values, the old member states can do so without consequences in much more serious cases. these cases are called "disagreement" and never "veto". the title of today's debate reflects that bias. politically misleading. what you call the mainstream liberal veto is nothing more than the unanimity of decision-making enshrined in the founding treaties, which is the basis of good faith cooperation. european integration was created by the member states, who are the masters of the treaties. that's why the founders guaranteed the right to have their say in any decision that was important to them. the abolition of unanimity would mean the end of the sovereignty of the member states and the completion of centralisation in brussels. it would make it impossible to think differently, to disagree, and it would undermine european unity. the union cannot become a united states of europe serving federalist interests.
